Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template 
• 
Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-
reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc. 
• 
Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details 
provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be 
reflected in all relevant documents.  
• 
The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not 
exceed 60. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual 
service standard time. 
• 
The recommended font is Arial, size 11.  
• 
Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals 
will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, 
subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. 
Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.    
 
Please submit the completed proposal to: 
fundingproposal@gcfund.org 
Please use the following name convention for the file name: 
“FP-[Accredited Entity Short Name]-[Country/Region]-[YYYY/MM/DD]”

A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages) 
1. 
Thailand is one of the 16 countries identified globally as being in the ‘extreme risk’ category of those most 
vulnerable to future climate change impacts over the next 30 years.1 An extensive coastline, rural communities highly 
dependent on agriculture, and its extensive populous urban areas, located on flood prone plains, shape Thailand’s 
vulnerability. Severe drought and flood events2 are likely to be experienced increasingly in the near and longer-term 
future, as a result of the combined effects of a more vigorous hydrological cycle and enhanced surface drying.3 The 
IPCC Fifth Assessment Report (AR5), indicates that in Southeast Asia temperatures overall have been increasing at a 
rate of 0.14°C to 0.20°C per decade since the 1960s, and are projected to increase from 0.8°C to 3.2°C above pre-
industrial levels by the end of the century4, with differences between regions and microclimates within and across 
Southeast Asia.  A positive trend in the occurrence of heavy (top 10 percent by rain amount) rain events is also 
predicted5 with key determinants such as the amount and intensity of rainfall, and the rate of evaporation set to affect 
surface water flow and water availability.6 This results in a significantly greater frequency and intensity of flooding during 
the wet season, and extended drought periods during the dry season, presenting a significant challenge to effective 
water management in Thailand. 
 
2. 
 The frequency of extreme drought has been rising7 due to increases in evapotranspiration, combined with 
increases in rainfall variability (less frequent rain of higher intensity) 8 adversely impacts economic activities and 
agricultural livelihoods. Until a few years ago Thailand was the largest exporter of rice in the world with below-normal 
rainfall potentially crippling the agricultural sector and slowing economic growth.9 Thailand’s agricultural sector in 
particular will be impacted by changing precipitation patterns with increasingly common extreme rain events and floods 
(an increase of precipitation of 8 percent is expected by 2050)10, as well as more extensive drought periods.11 In 2011, 
66 out of 77 provinces in the country were affected by flooding, with over 20,000 sq. km of agricultural land damaged, 
and nearly 900 lives lost.12 In 2012, the World Bank reported Thailand suffered USD46.5 billion in total damages and 
loss and required an estimated USD14 billion in loans for rehabilitation and reconstruction from the 2011 flooding 
alone.13 The recent drought in 2015-2016 is estimated to have resulted in losses of USD3.4 billion. Future climate 
change projections indicate an increase in both the magnitude and frequency of drought and extreme rainfall leading

A 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.0 | PAGE 3 OF 65 
 
to these events. Projected negative impacts are estimated to induce losses in Thailand’s agriculture nationally during 
2040 – 2049 of between USD24 billion and USD94 billion.14   
 
3. 
Climate impacts on agriculture will particularly affect poor households. Poverty in Thailand has a predominately 
rural-profile, which fluctuates according to vulnerabilities in the agricultural sector, such as faltering economic growth, 
falling agricultural prices, and droughts.15 In 2013, for example, 80 percent of the country’s poor 7.3 million live in rural 
areas, and a further 6.7 million were living within 20 percent above the national poverty line.16 Proportionally, the Central 
and Northern Regions of Thailand have the highest levels of poverty. Sukhothai, Phitsanulok, and Uttaradit Provinces, 
included as part of the Northern Central region, have higher poverty levels when compared with other parts of the 
country.    
 
4. 
With climate extremes expected to increase, climate-informed water management and climate-resilient water 
infrastructure will be critical in order to prepare for and respond to floods and droughts. In Thailand’s National Adaptation 
Plan 2018, Flood Control and drought management are both highlighted as key priorities, with a focus on Chao Phraya 
River Basin. Given the cost of upgrading existing water infrastructure across the country, the RTG is seeking to 
complement its grey infrastructure with ecosystems-based adaptation measures. As agriculture households are the 
most vulnerable to changing climatic conditions, an integrated solution which brings together water management and 
agriculture is proposed. The project therefore seeks to adapt water management and agricultural livelihoods in the 
Yom and Nan river basins to climate change induced extreme weather events (droughts and floods), through 
interventions across three Outputs:   
 
• 
Output 1:  Enhance climate and risk informed planning in the water and agricultural sectors through 
improved climate information and cross sectoral coordination 
• 
Output 2:  Improve water management through strengthened infrastructure complemented by EbA 
measures, for greater resilience to climate change impacts 
• 
Output 3:  Reduce volatility of agriculture livelihoods in drought and flood prone areas through strengthened 
extension support and local planning, investment in on-farm adaptation measures and greater access to 
finance and markets 
 
5. 
The project would directly benefit 62,000 people in the project site area, with indirect benefits reaching 471,561 
people in the project districts, and ultimately 25,000,000 of the Greater Chao Phraya River Basin. Further, better 
integration of EbA measures would have environmental benefits, while capacity building interventions of the project 
would support cost-efficient and effective water and agriculture planning. Project design, which includes artificial 
intelligence to support climate-informed planning, precision agriculture for efficient water use and applies the internet 
of things (IoT) concept for sharing and applying data, has been guided by Thailand 4.0, which aims to shift Thailand’s 
agriculture sector towards an innovation-driven and interconnected sector. At the same time, the project also supports 
low tech interventions to help farmers respond to changing rainfall patterns.  These include on-farm EbA measures 
(e.g. farm ponds), small scale equipment to support water saving farming practices (e.g. system for rice intensification 
(SRI)) and community nurseries.  Training will be provided to ensure that extension services can support farmers with 
adaptation measures, and the project will provide support to market access for products resulting from climate resilient 
practices.    
 
6. 
UNDP and FAO currently support (through a BMU funded project) support the Ministry of Agriculture and 
Cooperatives (MoAC) to enhance their agriculture sector adaptation planning, e.g. through an updated climate change 
strategy and action plan to align with the CCMP and as sectoral input to Thailand’s National Adaptation Plan (NAP). 
The MoAC is implementing the the Agricultural Strategic Plan on Climate Change (ASPCC) 2017-2021 whereby RID 
takes lead for the Strategy 2 (Adaptation Actions).  Furthermore, the Office of National Water Resource (ONWR) 
functions as the regulating agency in proposing policies, formulating master plan on water resources management, 
responsible for management and supervision as well as integration on the implementation plan of water related-
agencies in accordance with the Water Resource Management Act (2018), the ONWR has developed the 20-year 
Master Plan on Water Management (2018-2037), aimed at solving Thailand’s chronic drought, flood and wastewater 
problems. The Master Plan also stresses the importance of the need to bring in new ideas and technologies to address 
water related challenges which are exacerbated by climate change.

 PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION 
B.1. Climate rationale and context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages) 
Climate vulnerability baseline 
7. 
Pressure from climate change induced variations in precipitation patterns (rainfall volume and intensities) 
challenge the functionality of existing water management practices through the use of existing canals and flood gates, 
which are increasingly inadequate to contain increased rainfall and consequent discharges during the wet season. 
Extreme rain events enhance flood risk during wet seasons, while increasing  evapotranspiration reduces water 
availability – both present serious challenges for effective water management and a productive agriculture sector.  
 
8. 
Thailand has 25 river basin systems (including 254 sub-basins). Of these river basins – there are two principal 
systems, the Mekong and Chao Phraya River systems.  The Greater Chao Phraya River Basin Area consists of eight 
sub-river basins including Ping, Wang, Yom, Nan, Chao Phraya, Sakaekrung, Pa Sak and Tha Chin, covering an area 
of 158,586 km2 with a population of approximately 25 million people. The average annual rainfall is 1,100 mm while the 
average annual runoff is 34,600 million m3.  In the northern part of the basin, the entire storage capacity is approximately 
27,446 million m3, while the central area can store only 1,808 million m3. The Chao Phraya River will not overflow its 
banks if the discharge does not exceed 2,800 m3 per second, which was exceeded during the devastating 2011 floods 
due to higher discharges from the Yom-Nan basin.   
 
9. 
The topography of the Yom-Nan river basin in particular, and the related challenges it presents in managing 
changes in precipitation and related water flow, make the area an adaptation priority for Thailand.  The Yom River 
(watershed area 23,600 km2), and Nan River (watershed area 34,300 km2) flow from the northern mountain system 
and join together at Nakhon Sawan. Upstream along the Nan River, the Sirikit Dam reservoir (capacity 9.5 billion m3, 
catchment area 13,000 km2, built in 1974) was constructed for multiple purposes, including flood and drought 
management. There is, however, no major dam or reservoir that can regulate water throughout the year in the Yom 
River basin. Plans to build the Kaeng Sua Ten River Dam (1.15 billion m3) for flood and drought protection were not 
approved by CSOs or communities.  In the lower part of Yom and Nan Rivers, the river gradients are around 1/10,000 
to 1/15,000 which is relatively flat and the width of the Yom River at the downstream section is narrow with low capacity 
for water retention.  Specific to the Yom-Nan area, RID has designed for enhancements to the existing canal and 
regulator network in the Yom and Nan river basins at 13 critical schemes. The designs consider the increased capacities 
needed to retain water (so called ‘Monkey Cheek’ concept) in the upper reaches of the watersheds, as well as the 
policy of government to not relocate the area’s population.  Planned under RID, the estimate cost of upgrading all 13 
schemes is approximately USD 42 million over the next 20 years.   
 
 
Key climate change risks and hazards 
Increased temperatures and rainfall variability  
10. 
Thailand has experienced significant country-wide warming over the last four decades, as well as associated 
warm extremes.17 There is evidence that maximum, mean and minimum temperatures in Thailand as a whole have 
increased significantly by 0.86°C, 0.95°C and 1.45°C respectively over the 1955-2009 period (see Figure 1).  For 
temperature extreme indices, a significant upward trend is seen over the annual number of warm nights and days, the 
annual occurrence of warm spells, tropical nights, summer days, and the highest and lowest values of daily minimum 
temperatures – indicating a significant shift in the temperature distribution toward warmer conditions over the recent 
decades. In contrast, significant downward trends are observed in the annual number of cold nights and days, the 
annual occurrence of cold spells, diurnal temperature range and annual extreme temperature range.  These impacts 
are consistent with the fifth assessment report (AR5) from the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC), 
which indicates that temperatures in Southeast Asia have been increasing at a rate of 0.14°C to 0.20°C per decade 
since the 1960s and predicts increases from 0.8°C to 3.2°C for the overall region by the end of this century.

Figure 1: Maximum, mean and minimum temperature18 
 
11. 
Nationwide averages of rainfall show little change in annual totals since 1950 (see Annex 2, section 1.7).  
However, the IPCC highlights the positive trend in the occurrence of heavy (top 10% by rain amount) rain events, and 
the influence of climate change on several large-scale phenomena affecting the region19.  This is consistent with simple 
daily intensity index (SDII) observations in Thailand which show a consistent increase between 1955 and 2014 both 
nationally and over the northern and central areas of the Chao Phraya basin (see Figure 2) and with recent analyses 
of 44 long record quality controlled first order surface weather stations across Thailand for the period from 1955 to 
2011, which indicate that precipitation has become less frequent throughout most areas, though precipitation events 
have become more intense.20 These results are further consistent with reduced numbers of rainy days and increases 
in mean daily rainfall intensity demonstrated for stations in the Chao Phraya basin (see FS, section 1.7).

individual stations (solid triangle trend >95% significance) 
 
Extreme events exacerbated by climate change 
12. 
Downscaling of the ECHAM4 GCM using PRECIS, projects precipitation increases over the whole country 
under both A2 and B2 scenarios (see Annex 2, section 1.5.2). The length of rainy season are simulated to be similar

to the present, with increases in rainfall intensity and an increase of total precipitation by 2050.22 These results are 
confirmed through an analysis of the wider ensemble of models from the CMIP5 (37 GCMs), CMIP6 (24 GCMs) and 
CORDEX (7 RCMs) archives. These model ensembles demonstrate an expected increase in total annual rainfall of the 
order of 10-15% (Annex 2, section 1.7.4) 
 
13. 
Increasing temperatures and potential evapotranspiration have resulted inincreased incidences of severe 
droughts (as measured through the standardised precipitation evapotranspiration index, SPEI), which are likely to 
intensify in their magnitude due to climate change (see Annex 2, section 1.7.3 and 1.7.4). These increases in potential 
evapotranspiration have been shown to lead to increases in rice crop water requirements during all months, and these 
increases in requirements are greater than increases in rainfall, leading to increases in irrigation water requirements 
under climate change (see Annex 2, section 1.7) 
 
14. 
Given the above evidence and that detailed further in Annex 2 (particularly section 1.7), the following key 
climate change impacts on the Chao Phraya basin have been identified: a) Increases in potential evapotranspiration, 
crop and irrigation water requirements; b) increases in annual rainfall which will manifest mostly through increases in 
rainfall intensity; c) increases in river discharges beyond current levels (including the devastating 2011 floods). 
 
Key impacts of temperature changes, rainfall variability and extreme events on water management and agriculture 
Impact on water management in the Chao Phraya River basin 
15. 
The Greater Chao Phraya River Basin Area consists of eight sub-river basins including Ping, Wang, Yom, Nan, 
Chao Phraya, Sakaekrung, Pa Sak and Tha Chin, covering an area of 158,586 km2 with a population of approximately 
25 million people. The average annual rainfall is 1,100 mm while the average annual runoff is 34,600 million m3.  In the 
northern part of the basin, the entire storage capacity is approximately 27,446 million m3, while the central area can 
store only 1,808 million m3. The Chao Phraya River will not overflow its banks, if the discharge does not exceed 2,800 
m3 per second.  Several studies have projected the future river discharge in the Chao Phraya River Basin, using future 
climate projections indicate that peak discharges are expected to increase during the next 30 years.23 24 25  
 
16. 
Annex 2, section 1.7.4 shows that the projected increases in annual rainfall result from a relatively strong 
increase in rainfall in the peak of the rainy season (Aug-Oct). An earlier study26 of hydrological impacts of climate 
change demonstrated that even a weak rainfall increase (relative to increases projected using the full range of models 
in the CMIP5/CMIP6/CORDEX ensembles) is strongly amplified in the hydrological system to create a considerable 
impact on streamflow in the lower parts of the basin. This and the increases in runoff in the Chao Phraya simulated 
using 6 GCMs27 suggests that a wetter future will result in a considerable increase in flood discharges beyond current 
levels. This is confirmed in other data; the majority of CORDEX models (>75%) combined with a rainfall-runoff model 
simulate increases of approximately 50% (median estimate) in discharges of the Chao Phraya basin (see figure 38, 
Annex 2).  
 
17. Overall, the following is the summary of the analysis for climate change for the project: 
• 
Whilst station trends are mostly <95% significance, there are consistent trends between stations showing: 
increases in daily rainfall intensity, decreases in number of rainy days (this is consistent with future 
projections). This is consistent with existing peer-reviewed literature; 
• 
Historically, there has been an increasing risk in drought as measured by SPEI (measures rainfall-
evapotranspiration) but not SPI, implying that increases in evapotranspiration are mostly responsible and 
are greater than any increases in rainfall; 
• 
Ensemble projections [CMIP5 (37 GCMs), CMIP6 (24 GCMs) and downscaled CORDEX (4-7 RCMs)] 
clearly show future projected increases of 10-15% in annual rainfall. The MRI model is at the lower end of

=== PAGE 11 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.0 | PAGE 7 OF 65 
 
 
these estimates, but still shows increases in rainfall intensities which lead to increases in discharges during 
the main rainfall season when combined with a calibrated hydrological model; 
• 
Future temperature increases of about 2°C under RCP4.5 are virtually certain and consistent with station 
and gridded datasets. This leads to increases in evapotranspiration; 
• 
Crop water requirements (due to increases in evapotranspiration) and irrigation water requirements 
(accounting for changes in rainfall) both increase in all months; growing both rainfed and irrigated rice will 
require more water and more efficient farming methods in the future; and 
• 
Significant increases (approximately 50%) in modelled maximum discharges (using CORDEX models) are 
consistent with the +10% in annual precipitation since the catchments amplify the rainfall signal and the 
increases occur during the rainy season. This is also consistent with simulated increases in runoff for the 
basin found in peer-reviewed literature. 
 
18. 
RID already practices water management in the critical Chao Phraya River basin to stave off excess flooding 
during the wet season and to ensure water availability during the dry season, using canals and regulators, including 
reservoir operations. In the upper section (upland area), dams and reservoirs are used to store and regulate the flow 
of water. In the middle section (low lying area), water is drawn into designated low-lying areas to store the water, for 
release when the rain intensifies to prevent flooding in the lower (urban) part. The Action Plan of Integrated and 
Sustainable Flood Mitigation in Chao Phraya River Basin includes a) soil improvement and conservation in the upper 
river basin by reforestation and rehabilitation of forest areas in the river basin in Ping, Wang, Yom, Nan, Sakae Krung, 
Tha-Chin and Pa Sak, as well as reservoir construction in Yom, Sakae Krung, Nan, and Pa Sak water basin.  And in 
the lower section of the basin (urban and industrial area) flood diversion canals are used to divert excess water to the 
Gulf of Thailand, and dams are used to further regulate the distribution of water for industrial and agricultural use.  
 
19. 
However, existing infrastructure, particularly in the proposed project area (Yom and Nan river basin, in the 
Uttaradit, Phitsanulok, Sukhothai provinces, middle section, low lying area), are not adequate to cope with the 
increasing pressures of climate change. With more intense wet seasons, this basin will be increasingly unable to slow 
runoff to the central plains, and the greater Bangkok area. And with drier dry seasons, the water that the infrastructure 
is able to retain is insufficient to cover the irrigation needs of farmers for the extended period. The middle section (the 
proposed project area) serves a critical function along the broader Chao Phraya River, as it has historically been able 
to slow or keep excess water from the downstream areas that cannot manage the water flow due to limited natural 
capacity. The channel capacity of the Yom River at Si Satchanalai district is 2,397cms-1, which then reduces to just 
565cms-1 by the time it reaches downtown Sukhothai. In addition, the longitudinal river slope decreases from 1:2,300 
to 1:11,000 at this point, and creates a point of overflow in the area of Sukhothai that connects Phitsanulok and Phichit 
provinces.  
 
20. 
With rainwater as the country’s most important fresh water source, increases in temperature and evaporation 
will lead to water stresses as well as water scarcity during certain periods in the Chao Phraya River Basin area.  Annex 
2, section 1.7.4 further showed a strong impact of the projected increase in air temperature and PET on agricultural 
water demand in the near future: both crop water demand and irrigation water requirement increase by up to 30%. 
 
21. 
Increased runoff during the wet season and drier dry seasons will also accelerate soil erosion and inhibit 
groundwater resources, as well as increasing downstream flooding. The central plain area of Thailand (including the 
provinces of Phitsanulok, Sukhothai and Uttaradit) is a large plain, formed through sediment deposits as a result of 
fluvial processes into flood plains, terraces and swamp areas. In terms of hydrology, the central plain is also the main 
reservoir of groundwater for Thailand. It holds eight separate aquifers. Effective water management pursuing water 
retention during wet periods, has dual-value in not only slowing water flow during periods of flood, and reducing the 
volumes of water that continue downstream during wet periods, but also serves to secure water to be saved for use 
during the dry periods, assisting to maintain soil fertility and reduce sediment loss from agricultural lands, providing 
environmental flows and retaining ecological system functions, as well as allowing recharge and permeation to 
groundwater sources. 
 
22. 
It is therefore crucial that water management provides: (i) flood protection, particularly given the risk to several 
cities located along the Yom River downstream; and (ii) adequate water storage through irrigation canals, weirs, 
backwater areas, diversion structures and embankments for farmers during the drier dry season.  
 
Impact on agriculture and agriculture livelihoods

35 Attavanich, 2013 
23. 
While flooding can damage crops and infrastructure across the country, drought affects crop production.28 
Thailand has been experiencing in recent years its worst periods of drought in decades, with significant impacts on 
overall food production and water availability. In 2008, for example, 10 million people across 55 of Thailand’s provinces, 
experienced water shortages due to severe drought.29  Drought in 2015 disproportionately impacted the agricultural 
sector, particularly subsistence farmers. The 2015 drought affected more than 250,000 people and cost USD400 million 
in government spending.  In 2016, MoAC provided USD2 billion in soft loans to farmers in order to mitigate the impact 
of water shortages (drought) to the agricultural sector.30  Estimates of total losses resulting from the 2016 drought are 
estimated at USD3.4 billion or 0.85 percent of GDP.31  
 
24. 
Thailand has, in general, two annual rice-growing periods of wet season and dry season rice crops.  Although 
called wet season rice, a portion is also irrigated.  Wet season rice accounts for around 72 percent of Thailand’s annual 
rice production32 and is heavily dependent on monsoonal rain with up to 70 percent being directly and entirely rainfed 
crop.33  The other 30 percent of wet season rice is grown in the Chao Phraya River Basin and is irrigated from the 
upper catchment reservoirs and areas of water retention and controlled water management. Rice is the main agricultural 
crop of the Chao Phraya River Basin, as the basin has the greatest production advantages in terms of highly productive 
land, and irrigated areas.34 In the Chao Phraya River basin region, climate projections show trends of increasing 
temperature with fluctuating precipitation35 resulting in increases of crop and irrigation water requirements. Calculations 
for the region, found that on average (with variations between the provinces) projections for the periods during 2040-
2049, farmland values per rai (0.16 hectares), are projected to decrease from USD2,703 to USD2,068. In terms of 
climate change adaptation activities, rice farmers currently try to reschedule their crop planning in order to adjust to the 
season, however they often lack reliable climate forecasts.  
 
25. 
Farmer households and farmer communities are the most vulnerable groups to climate change in Thailand. An 
agriculture survey conducted during the development phase of the proposed GCF project (see Annex 23), showed that 
most respondents in the Yom and Nan River basin were dependent on rice farming as a source for income (95.8 
percent of households surveyed). Household incomes in the area are split on average as THB 174,345 (USD 5,050) 
per year income generated from crop revenues, THB 31,652 (USD 918) from fishing revenue, and THB 2,895 (USD 
84) generated from livestock revenues annually. In some of the areas where interviews took place for focus group 
discussions, rice farmers attempt to do three rounds of rice cropping per year. This is not consistent, however, and 
farmers are facing increasing volatility due to rainfall variability. For instance, cropping was drastically changed during 
the 2015-2016 drought due to constrained water availability.  
 
Baseline efforts and investments 
Climate information for the water and agricultural sectors 
26. 
Given the cross-sectoral impacts of challenges related to water management, RID established the Smart Water 
Operation Center (SWOC) to link data with all relevant agencies for water resource management in a systematic 
manner during drought and flood crises. This center brings together information from the Thai Meteorological 
Department (TMD), the Hydro and Agro Informatics Institute, the Geo-Informatics and Space Technology Development 
Agency, the Department of Water Resources, and the Hydrographic Department of the Royal Thai Navy, to be compiled 
as central information for water analysis and forecasts. SWOC serves as a command center for water resource 
management for Thailand.  
 
27. 
While there is a hydro-meteorological network in place, only recently has the data from the network started to 
get systematically analyzed, tailored for use, and disseminated for agricultural and water management purposes.  
SWOC and Thailand’s research institutes have worked with the academic-King Mongkut Institute of Technology North 
Bangkok (KMUTNB) for developing a water forecast decision making tool, namely the Intelligence-based Real-time 
Decision-Making Modeling (IRDM) tool. This tool already serves RID’s decision makers in managing water in Chao

=== PAGE 13 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.0 | PAGE 9 OF 65 
 
 
 
36 Thailand ‘Technology needs assessments report for climate change adaptation’ 
Phraya river basin. IRDM can calculate the amount of rainfall and inflow of water into reservoirs during the wet season 
and design water release schedules for 12-month periods, with a relatively high level of accuracy. Its mathematical 
models can be used to forecast inflows into different water sources, support water management plans for individual 
irrigation zones and estimate agricultural yield and revenue.  
 
28. 
IRDM is a collaborative effort between 5 organisations - RID, the Agricultural Research Development Agency 
(ARDA), the National Research Council of Thailand (NRCT), the King Mongkut’s University of Technology North 
Bangkok (KMUTNB), and the Thailand Research Organizations Network (TRON).  IRDM is a milestone in enhancing 
the institutional coordination between the agricultural and irrigation departments, as well as to develop the public 
forecasting and modelling capacity36. However, continuous support is needed to refine and upgrade the IRDM tool in 
order to also provide forecasts for shorter time frames (e.g. monthly or weekly) and tailor and distribute risk information 
to the needs of subnational decision makers and farmers/end-users. This can, ultimately, enable farmers to enhance 
their resilience against climate change induced droughts and floods through better water management planning. 
 
29. 
The concept of integrated water resources management (IWRM) based on the river basin model, is contained 
within Thailand’s water policies and management. However, as found by a study by the World Bank (2012), Thailand’s 
water resource management framework would benefit from a number of improvements – particularly in regard to 
strengthening and streamlining the IWRM approach through sectoral planning and programming. Given the 
dependence of the agriculture sector on water availability and sustainable water management, there is a need to bring 
climate change data into the respective strategies and plans, and to strengthen collaboration between the water and 
agriculture sectors. Work is already ongoing which can be built upon for more integrated planning. A broader (national) 
expert review ideally should take place, reviewing and analyzing the extreme events to provide further lessons and 
recommendations to the Strategic Committee on Water Management for integration into any future strategic plans. This 
assessment also found that reviewing and passing the water bill, would benefit from high level championing of the 
IWRM model in instituting the political clout required to institute IWRM throughout sectoral development planning long 
term. Institutions need to strengthen their roles in coordination to ensure that IWRM can take place, with a focus on 
management instruments and an enabling environment being established to engage inter-agency and widely with 
stakeholders. 
 
30. 
With the growing challenges and particular vulnerabilities related to the agriculture sector, the RTG also seeks 
transformational change to the agriculture sector. The National Strategy - Thailand 4.0 aims to shift Thailand’s 
agriculture sector towards an innovative-driven and interconnected sector through the integration of the concepts and 
approaches from sufficiency economy, smart farm, precision agriculture, intensive agriculture, sustainable agriculture, 
green economy, and agricultural biotechnology. Thailand 4.0 is an economic model that aims to unlock the country 
from several challenges (i.e. middle income trap, growing inequality and imbalanced development) resulting from past 
economic development models which place emphasis on agriculture (Thailand 1.0), light industry (Thailand 2.0), and 
advanced industry (Thailand 3.0).  The four objectives of Thailand 4.0 are economic prosperity, social well-being, raising 
human values and environmental protection. 
   
31. 
While significant progress has been made related to data collection, analysis, and sharing of information, there 
are opportunities to improve coordination between the water and agriculture sectors, building on IRDM to ensure policy 
makers are equipped with the tailored information needed for climate-informed planning and in line with the ambitions 
of Thailand 4.0.

=== PAGE 14 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.0 | PAGE 10 OF 65 
 
 
 
 
Water Management in Chao Phraya River Basin 
32. 
To cope with these additional climate-induced flood and drought risks, RID developed a 20-year roadmap 
towards Intelligent Organisation: Achieving water security and rendering value-added service within 2036 (see Annex 
2). Investment in the Yom and Nan river basin area is part of a series of interventions ongoing and planned by RID to 
enhance water management in the Greater Chao Phraya River Basin Area.  
 
33. 
RID already practices water management in the critical Chao Phraya River basin to stave off excess flooding 
during the wet season and to ensure water availability during the dry season, using canals and regulators, including 
reservoir operations. In the upper section (upland area), dams and reservoirs are used to store and regulate the flow 
of water. In the middle section (low lying area), water is drawn into designated low-lying areas to store the water for 
certain periods for release when the rain intensifies to prevent flooding in the lower (urban) part. And in the lower section 
of the basin (urban and industrial area) flood diversion canals are used to divert excess water to the Gulf of Thailand, 
and dams are used to further regulate the distribution of water for industrial and agricultural use.  
 
34. 
Existing infrastructure, particularly in the proposed project area (Yom and Nan river basin, in the Uttaradit, 
Phitsanulok, Sukhothai provinces, middle section, low lying area), are no longer adequate to cope with the increasing 
pressures of climate change. With more intense wet seasons, this basin is increasingly unable to slow runoff to the 
central plains and the greater Bangkok area resulting in flooding. And with drier dry seasons, the water that the 
infrastructure is able to retain is insufficient to cover the irrigation needs of farmers for the extended period, resulting in 
maladaptive responses by farmers. The middle section (the GCF project area) serves a critical function along the 
broader Chao Phraya River, as it has historically been able to slow or keep excess water from the downstream areas 
that cannot manage the water flow due to limited natural capacity. The channel capacity of the Yom River at Si 
Satchanalai district is 2,397cms, which then reduces to just 565cms by the time it reaches downtown Sukhothai. In 
addition, the longitudinal river slope decreases from 1:2,300 to 1:11,000 at this point, and creates a point of overflow 
in the area of Sukhothai that connects Phitsanulok and Phichit provinces.  
 
35. 
The central plain area of Thailand (including the provinces of Phitsanulok, Sukhothai and Uttaradit) is a large 
plain, formed through sediment deposits as a result of fluvial processes into flood plains, terraces and swamp areas. 
In terms of hydrology, the central plain is also the main reservoir of groundwater for Thailand, it holds eight separate 
aquifers. The increased runoff during the wet season is resulting in rapid soil erosion and inhibiting groundwater 
recharge.  
 
36. 
The RTG seeks to find greener solutions to this challenge. Investment towards effective water management 
in the Yom-Nan river basin would have multiple benefits – i) slowing water flow to downstream areas during periods 
of flood risk, ii) retaining water for use during the dry season by farmers in the immediate area, and iii) assisting to 
maintain soil fertility and reduce sediment loss from agricultural lands, providing environmental flows and retaining 
ecological system functions, as well as allowing recharge and permeation to groundwater sources.

=== PAGE 15 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.0 | PAGE 11 OF 65 
 
 
 
37 Uppasit, S., Silaratana, N., Fuangswasdi, A., Chusanatus, S., Pavelic, P. & Kriengsak, S. 2013, ‘Managed aquifer recharge using infiltration 
pond: Case study of Ban Nong Na, Phitsanulok, Thailand’, Journal of Earth Science and Engineering, 1. Pp 14-22

http://www.davidpublisher.org/Public/uploads/Contribute/55078898cb5e0.pdf  
37. 
To inform needed enhancements to the broader Chao Phraya River system, RID commissioned a feasibility 
study, recommendations include:  
• 
The capacity of drainage system should be improved to enable a drainage of water from low-lying areas at the 
beginning of the rainy season. Canals and waterways are needed to be dredged to the depth that can drain 
these low-lying areas. These waterways should be managed to connect to each other. 
• 
The capacity to store water volume within the areas during the late rainy season and the beginning of the dry 
season should be increased to allow a sufficient storage of water to irrigate the area during the dry period. The 
dredging of waterways through the constructions of additional water regulators should be implemented for the 
purpose especially at the end of waterways connecting to the Yom River and Khlong Bang Kaeo Canal. 
• 
The capacity to divert water into the area should be improved. Additional infrastructure could enable the inflow 
of water from the Yom River (possibly to be drawn from the Ping River where a project is underway) and from 
the Nan Basin through the feeder canal of the Nan River- Khlong Mem and the ditch of Khlong Wang Khon. 
The construction of water regulator facilities (Khlong Muang Chang and Khlong Ta Kay) in the Yom River and 
Khlong Bang Kaeo Canal are needed. 
• 
An expansion of the irrigation zone in the area can be developed through improved water management 
planning in the Yom River Basin. This can also help in the preparation for the potential development of a large 
additional reservoir and would benefit the water co-management plan with the neighboring basins. 
• 
Specific to the Yom-Nan area, RID has developed designs for enhancements to the existing canal and regulator 
network in the Yom and Nan river basins at 13 critical schemes. The designs consider the increased capacity 
needs of the area, as well as the policy of government to not relocate the area’s population.  Planned under 
RID, the estimate cost of upgrading all 13 schemes is approximately USD 42 million over the next 20 years.   
 
Agriculture livelihoods in flood and drought prone areas 
38. 
Rain variability and drier dry periods are affecting agriculture livelihoods in the proposed project area.  Support 
is needed to both increase resilience of vulnerable agriculture livelihoods as well as to prevent maladaptation, which 
could inadvertently worsen conditions. 
 
39. 
Through consultations with communities undertaken during project development, farmers were asked about 
measures employed to respond to drought conditions. A disproportionate number resorted to groundwater resources 
for irrigation. Constructing artesian wells to access groundwater was the most widely employed on-farm response for 
dealing with drought, despite the significant costs involved.  
 
40. 
Overall, groundwater extraction in Thailand has dropped significantly since the introduction of the 2004 
Groundwater Act, and a comprehensive monitoring system is in place, using 156 monitoring stations and 474 
observation wells operates as an extensive network around greater Bangkok and its vicinity, and across the country 
1,351 monitoring wells.  There is, however, extraction of groundwater at a rate higher than can be recharged naturally 
– which has caused a decline in groundwater levels, land subsidence and seawater intrusion in certain areas.  The 
Central Plain area of Thailand, which holds the Provinces of Phitsanulok, Sukhothai and Uttaradit – is a large plain, 
formed through sediment deposits as a result of fluvial processes into flood plains, terraces and swamp areas.  In terms 
of hydrology, the Central Plain is also the main reservoir of groundwater for Thailand. It holds eight separate aquifers. 
The groundwater that farmers have increasingly come to rely on during dry periods, particularly in and around 
Phitsanulok, has faced continuous level decreases due to over-extraction for irrigation37.  Within the northeastern area, 
groundwater too is affected by drought.38   
 
41. 
Farmers have also been adapting autonomously to climate change by shifting to other crops such as fruit trees, 
and/or adopting and/or returning to use of aquaculture or aquaculture-diversified livelihoods. Similarly, fast growing 
and/or water resistant crops are used in the areas that are submerged by flood for long periods. Fishing and fish 
products are also utilized when rice and agricultural livelihood productions are inhibited by shorter grow periods and 
the early onset of rainy season or reduced rainfall and drought periods. In response to drought, farmers interviewed 
during project design, employed autonomous adaptation strategies that are wide-ranging. However, resorting to 
groundwater, and constructing artesian wells to access groundwater was widely employed as an on-farm response.  
Second was diversifying income streams with external employment. 
 
42. 
The ability of rice farmers to adopt climate-resilient practices successfully is dependent on access to knowledge 
on climate change and its impacts on agriculture and natural resources, and on alternative practices, e.g. growing more

=== PAGE 16 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.0 | PAGE 12 OF 65 
 
 
 
39 Intelligent Based Real-time Decision-Making 
40 Thailand’s Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC), 2015 
climate resilient crops and applying innovative agriculture and water saving approaches and technologies. Per 
consultations, farmers have been dependent upon their past experiences in gauging the water conditions to determine 
cropping seasons, while flood forecasting tends to rely upon information passed on by farmers further upstream. To 
reduce their risk to conditions limiting their ability to complete the cultivation season cycle – in terms of flood forecasting 
errors, or impacts of water insufficiency, most farmers in the area have settled on using short growth duration varieties 
of rice. These varieties are considered as being of lower quality than long duration rice, and the prices that farmers 
receive for this variety is less. 
 
B.2. Theory of change (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagram) 
43. 
The project targets to improve the water management capacity of the RID and local communities in the critical 
area between the Yom and Nan Rivers (Phitsanulok, Sukhothai and Uttaradit provinces) to address the core problem 
that climate-induced droughts and floods threatening agriculture livelihoods and infrastructure in Yom and Nan river 
basins, and increases flood risks downstream’ (see problem statement in Theory of Change below). The targeted river 
basins are key to Thailand’s flood risk management and irrigation system, and host some of the most vulnerable groups 
of society. Therefore, this project will address the following root causes and barriers to mitigate flood and drought risks, 
and enhance the resilience of local communities: 
 
Lack of reliable data and risk information on localized climate change impacts available  
44. 
The knowledge base on risk information and climate change impact projections is growing (e.g. the first version 
of IRDM39 tool has been developed), however, little reliable and localized projections for changing temperature regimes 
and precipitation patterns for shorter time periods exists. This reduces the ability to predict droughts, precipitation 
patterns and other weather-related information to enable a climate change responsive water and irrigation 
management, as well as to plan adaptation strategies and climate-responsive cropping patterns.  With this data, climate 
information can be further tailored for local level, as well as household level, planning.  
 
Insufficient coordination between water and agriculture sectors for effective integrated water resources management 
45. 
As detailed in its NDC, a top adaptation priority for Thailand is to promote and strengthen of IWRM practices 
to achieve water security, effective water resource management to mitigate flood and drought.  To achieve this, greater 
coordination is needed across sectors, in particular water and agriculture as the most vulnerable to climate change.     
 
Limited technical and financial capacity for the climate-informed upgrade existing infrastructure  
46. 
As an extreme risk country 40 , RTG faces daunting costs related to both response to extreme events 
exacerbated by climate change, as well as investments in adaptation. Existing water management infrastructure must 
be upgraded, to better prepare for drier dry seasons and to better manage increased rainfall during shorter periods. 
Current engineering designs for the planned upgrade to water management infrastructure (i.e. regulators) do not 
adequately take into account climate change, and could result in higher than expected repair costs or a short life of the 
investments (e.g. infrastructure needing replacement within a short period).   
 
47. 
A review of existing engineering designs is needed, and enhancement as necessary, to ensure resilience to 
climate change – ensuring efficiency and effective use of public resources.  Further, due to the high upfront costs of 
water management infrastructure, and long-term operations and maintenance (O&M) costs, financial support is needed 
to complement government investment.      
 
Limited use of EbA measures to complement grey infrastructure for water management  
48. 
Thailand generally uses grey infrastructure (e.g. regulators) for water management.  Existing plans and designs 
for the Yom-Nan project area and the Greater Chao Phraya River Basin Area do not include EbA measures. RID seeks 
to complement grey infrastructure with ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) measures however RID experience in this 
area is limited.  Combining grey infrastructure with EbA measures would not only support critical ecosystem services, 
but also reduce overall costs (i.e. lower upfront investment and long-term O&M, compared to a purely grey infrastructure 
investment).     
 
49. 
The project area is heavily altered by small settlements, farms, and cultivation; villages are scattered throughout 
the catchment area with many irrigation canals cutting through the area. While the area is significantly utilized, there 
are still different ecological habitats present. Lines of bushes and trees can be found quite often in between paddy 
fields or other farmland areas. As existing water management systems must be enhanced to be more climate-resilient, 
there is an opportunity to also introduce EbA measures to complement planned grey infrastructure investments by RID. 
An assessment on the possibility to integrate EbA measures was conducted during the development of this proposal

=== PAGE 17 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.0 | PAGE 13 OF 65 
 
 
(see Annex 22). The assessment concluded that there is a high potential to integrate EbA approaches in the project 
area, with specific recommendations include:   
• 
Application of modeling comprising of three different methodologies each of which build on each other:  
hydrological river basin and water allocation modelling, hydraulic modelling, and erosion and sediment 
transport; 
• 
River restoration, with duration curves and depth-discharge curves informed by the above models; and 
• 
And the conduct of a fish survey to inform fish passes. 
 
50. 
With targeted support to RID in planning and implementing EbA measures in the GCF project site area, RID 
can then replicate or expand on use of EbA, to complement grey infrastructure, in their plans for the Greater Chao 
Phraya River Basin Area.   
 
Farmers lack of access to climate information and support to adapt to climate change 
51. 
Closely related to the lack of localized climate information, there is currently a constrained access to information 
and support about climate change and adaptation measures to communities and farmers. While communities are 
already experiencing flood and drought related impacts, without information and support there is a risk for maladaptive 
measures (e.g. such as ground water extraction for water intensive crops). Diversification to climate-resilient agricultural 
practices (e.g. adapting drought tolerant crop varieties, techniques using short cycle crops) and water saving 
technologies (e.g. on farm ponds) requires specific knowledge, skills, and technology transfer that is limited, as 
communities lack the technical assistance and support owing to limited capacities among peers and local extension 
staff.  
 
52. 
Importantly, with adaptation or change comes uncertainty. Farmers lack sufficient information on markets, and 
training on accessing new markets, to make informed decisions on changes in crops, agriculture practices and related 
risk mitigation measures.  
 
53. 
Below is the theory of change for the project:

Figure 5: Theory of Change 
 
 
Project objective, outcomes, and impacts 
 
54. 
The target communities of the proposed project are in three provinces of the northern region of Thailand of the 
Greater Chao Phraya River Basin, in the confluence of the Yom and Nan Rivers: Phitsanulok, Sukhothai, and 
Uttaradit. These provinces were selected by RID under MoAC, in consultation with the Office of Natural Resources 
and Environmental Policy and Planning (ONEP), given the impacts of climate change to the area’s critical function in 
water management in Thailand. The objective of the proposed project is to adapt water management and agricultural 
livelihoods in the Yom and Nan river basins to climate change induced extreme weather events (droughts and 
floods). The Fund level impacts of the project are an increased resilience of: i) infrastructure and the built environment 
to climate change threats; iii) ecosystems and ecosystem services; iii) most vulnerable people and communities; and 
iv) health and well-being, and food and water security. The GCF-related outcome of the project is Strengthened 
adaptive capacity and reduced exposure to climate risks of green and grey water infrastructure, and agricultural 
livelihoods in the Yom and Nan river basins. The paradigm shift is to move towards climate risk informed water and 
livelihood management through tailored climate information, technical support, and the adoption of innovative 
technologies and practices, such as ecosystem-based adaptation. 
 
55. 
First the project will address the barriers related to the low availability of reliable, localized, and easily 
accessible climate and risk information, as well as the improve the inter-ministerial coordination and distribution of risk

=== PAGE 19 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.0 | PAGE 15 OF 65 
 
 
information to end-users. To do this the project will: (i) strengthen institutional capacities for generating climate risk 
information, which will directly support the continuous water management and irrigation planning; (ii) enable to 
mainstream climate change risk information into public planning processes through improved inter-ministerial 
coordination; and (iii) disseminate climate change information to the community and household levels. This includes 
upgrading the IRDM tool to provide forecasts for shorter time frames (e.g. monthly or weekly) and tailor and distribute 
risk information to the needs of subnational decision makers and farmers/ end-users. This can, ultimately, enable 
farmers to enhance their resilience against climate change induced droughts and floods through better water 
management planning. 
 
56. 
Second, the project will address the barriers of limited technical and financial capacities to cope with intensifying 
climate change induced risks for water management and irrigation infrastructure in northern Chao Phraya river basin. 
GCF resources will be used to match public finance to: (i) strengthen water infrastructures and its management in the 
Yom and Nan river basins, to reduce flood risks and increase resilience of agriculture dependent communities to 
droughts; and (ii) enable RID to expand their capacities to implement ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) in their 
planning and implementation approaches, which has the potential to be transformational to Thailand’s traditional 
planning approaches that solely focussed on hard structure interventions. 
 
57. 
Finally, GCF resources will help to overcome barriers related to a low awareness of and access to resilient 
livelihood practices, lack of technical and financial capacities, and limited adoption and scalability hindering farmers to 
diversify to climate-resilient livelihoods and implement adaptive livelihood strategies. GCF resources, combined with 
leveraged RGT co-financing, will ensure that: (i) generate localized climate assessments and adaptation options 
analysis to capacitate local decision makers and agriculture based communities to integrate climate change risk 
information into climate change resilient livelihood and adaptation action planning; (ii) promoting EbA on-farm solutions 
to enhance drought and flood resilience of local farmer communities; and (iii) support farmer household capacity to 
access markets for climate resilient agriculture products. A particular focus will be given on creating linkages of local 
producer groups and farmers with buyers, including the promotion of cooperatives and organic products. 
Complementing this effort, Krungsri Bank will provide training to farmers on financial literacy, so that they are more 
aware, and can better avail themselves, of market research and financial mechanisms. 
 
58. 
Through these inter-related activities, the project will strengthen the resilience of water infrastructure and 
agriculture-reliant communities to cope with increasing climate change induced floods and droughts in the Yom and 
Nan river basins – benefiting communities and private sector through reduced damages or disruption, and relieving 
pressure on public resources related to flood and drought relief programmes. The project will also enhance the capacity 
of the RID to incorporate climate change risk information into water management planning processes and integrate 
EbA approaches into their portfolio of adaptive solutions, alongside traditionally used hard infrastructure interventions. 
Furthermore, the project will improve the quality of and access to climate change risk information available for decision 
makers at different levels and local communities. This will lead to the overall GCF, project-level outcome of 
strengthened adaptive capacity and reduced exposure to climate risks of green and grey infrastructure, and agricultural 
livelihoods in the Yom and Nan river basins. 
 
59. 
This greener, integrated approach is new for Thailand.  Best practices of the project will inform water and 
agriculture planning in the broader Chao Phraya river basin, as well as other river basins in the country.  As poverty in 
Thailand has a largely rural profile, the project can have wide impacts on addressing the climate challenges faced by 
the most vulnerable in the country.  Through partnerships and innovative approach of the project, there is significant 
potential for enhanced engagement with the private and finance sectors.  This is already happening through with the 
new parallel financing from the Bank of Agriculture and Agriculture Cooperatives (BAAC) – a line of credit of USD 16 
million through financial products with incentives for farmers to practice climate resilience.  A letter of commitment is 
included in Annex 13.  GCF resources will support the design of the financial product.     
 
B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2000 words, approximately 4 pages) 
Project objective, outcomes and impacts 
 
60. 
The target communities and schemes of this proposed project are in three Thai provinces of the northern region 
of the Greater Chao Phraya River Basin, in the confluence of the Yom and Nan Rivers (see Figure 6): Phitsanulok, 
Sukhothai and Uttaradit.

=== PAGE 20 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.0 | PAGE 16 OF 65 
 
 
 
Figure 6: Provincial map of Thailand. The larger area highlighted by a red dashed line is the larger Chao Phraya river basin. The 
smaller circle within that area is the site of the proposed GCF project, between the Yom and Nan rivers.   
 
61. 
These provinces were selected by RID under MoAC, in consultation with the Office of Natural Resources and 
Environmental Policy and Planning (ONEP), given the provinces’ high exposure to climate change induced floods and 
droughts, and the area’s critical function in water management in Thailand. The project site is situated in the lower area 
between two of the four main rivers that form the Chao Phraya River system - the Yom and the Nan rivers. Their basins 
provide a water retention function, slowing the flow of water before it gets to the country’s central plain, and ultimately 
the Bangkok metropolitan area and the Gulf of Thailand.  
 
62. 
With co-financing and GCF financing, the project seeks to a) support climate informed water management, 
planning and investment, and b) support vulnerable farmers in reducing volatility to changing climatic conditions.  and 
planning project seeks to enhance climate-informed and integrated planning as well as reduce disruption to smallholder 
farmers.   will achieve the objective through three complementary outputs: 
 
• 
Output 1:  Enhance climate and risk informed planning in the water and agricultural sectors through 
improved climate information and cross sectoral coordination 
• 
Output 2:  Improve water management through strengthened infrastructure complemented by EbA 
measures, for greater resilience to climate change impacts 
• 
Output 3:  Reduce volatility of agriculture livelihoods in drought and flood prone areas through strengthened 
extension support and local planning, investment in on-farm adaptation measures and greater access to 
finance and markets 
 
63. 
The project will complement ongoing efforts from RTG to enhance the resilience of the country and its citizens 
against climate change induced extreme weather events, such as floods and droughts. More specifically it will contribute 
to climate-risk informed decision making in the fields of water management and livelihoods planning processes through 
enhancing the knowledge-base on climate change, making information easily accessible for a spectrum of stakeholders 
and improve inter-ministerial coordination and information sharing. The project, furthermore, will reduce the flood risks 
within and downstream of the project site through green and grey infrastructure developments/ enhancements 
(including through ecosystem-based adaptation approaches), and increases the amount of water available for irrigation 
for local farmers during dry seasons, which are projected to increase in length due to climate change. The agriculture-
based communities in the project areas will be supported in shifting towards more climate change resilient livelihoods

=== PAGE 21 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.0 | PAGE 17 OF 65 
 
 
 
41 Considering a total of 124,095 households in Bang Rakam, Prom Piram, Pichai, Kong Krailas and an average of 3.8 people per 
household. 
42 The Sufficiency Economy Philosophy (SEP) originates from the development philosophy of the late King Bhumibol Adulyadej (Rama 9) and 
builds on three pillars: (i) Moderation; (ii) Reasonableness; and (iii) Risk Management. In addition to the SEP, the New Theory of Agriculture 
(NTA) was developed to help Thai farmers to build resilience against economic crisis and natural disasters. Proposed adaptation actions 
under the NTA include multi-cropping systems, diversified land-use, focus on households rather than commercialisation (mass production), 
and promotion of resilient seed varieties. 
through the adoption of agriculture technologies and practices (e.g. using drought resilient crops), and integration of 
climate risk information in their decision-making processes. 
 
 
Rationale and methodology for targeting of the project areas and populations  
64. 
The project targets to enhance the water access and flood resilience of, particularly agricultural-based, 
households in the Yom and Nan river basins in light of intensifying climate change impacts. The targeting for the total 
direct beneficiaries is based on the entirety of people directly residing in the districts that form the Yom and Nan river 
basins, due to their high vulnerability to drought and flood impacts. The population of the targeted districts (solely based 
on population figures of each sub-districts within the proposed project site), which leads to a total of 62,000 people as 
direct beneficiaries (for output 2 and 3). The targeting for the direct beneficiaries for livelihood interventions is anchored 
in identifying the people most exposed to observed and projected drought and flood impacts, which are those household 
members currently engaged in agricultural income generating activities. The indirect beneficiaries, benefiting from 
improved water management and flood control, will comprise of the entire population in the proposed districts (471,561 
people)41, as well as the 25,000,000 people of the greater Chao Phraya river basin area. 
 
 
Output 1:  Enhance climate and risk informed planning in the water and agricultural sectors through improved 
climate information and cross sectoral coordination 
(GCF Grant: USD 1.683 million, Co-financing USD 1.532 million) 
65. 
GCF resources will support the improvement of climate risk-responsive decision making in Thailand through 
the enhanced quality and availability of tailored climate information for both water management and agriculture 
livelihoods planning. This can be reached through improved climate projections and the dissemination of tailored to 
relevant stakeholders to support the integration into policies and planning across sectors (especially the water and 
agriculture sectors) – comparing supply to use and demand, and making adjustments in line with the philosophy of 
sufficiency economy 42. This Output will also support inter-ministerial coordination, enabling stronger collaboration 
between key decision-making bodies in Thailand’s water and agriculture sectors to incorporate climate risks and 
adaptation strategies in their sectoral planning processes. Importantly, tailored climate information will be generated 
and disseminated to agriculture households for application in household level planning.  
 
Activity 1.1. Strengthening capacity to generate tailored climate information to inform water management and 
agriculture planning  
 
66. 
This Activity aims to strengthen the climate risk-informed planning and implementation of water and agriculture 
sector interventions. The work under this Output will build upon the IRDM tool, which can forecast the amount of rainfall 
and inflow of water into reservoirs on the rainy season and optimize water release schedules for a 12 months ahead (a 
tool jointly developed by integrated data sources from several Thai organisations to enhance the utilization of weather 
information and projections to inform water management and irrigation practices throughout the Chao Phraya River 
Basin. Under this output the IRDM tool will be updated to increase the level of forecasting precision as well as generate 
projections for shorter time frames than annually (current standard of the tool).  The climate analysis will also inform 
the necessary upgrades to existing infrastructure, namely the canal and regulator network in the Yom and Nan river 
basins. Per the recently-conducted feasibility study undertaken by the Royal Irrigation Department, in order to 
adequately regulate water during wet and dry season, water gates need to be upgraded and existing canals need to 
be enlarged to ensure greater climate resilience.  Other plans to be supported by Activity 1.1 include Agri-Map (a 
mandatory and key zoning policy of MoAC, endorsed by the National Economic and Social Development Council 
(NESDC)), as well as support agriculture planning at the farmer level.    This will require procurement of computing and 
measurement equipment and related training to technical staff in its use.  Training will also be provided to RID and 
TMD to tailor products with information relevant for water management, with consideration of agriculture needs, 
including rainfall, runoff, temperature, and evapotranspiration.  The Thai Meteorological Department and RID will 
develop the national scope. They will engage the national and sub-national researchers on climate change fields (policy, 
technical modelling) to the activity.                                                                                                                                
 
67. Sub-activities include:

Activity 1.2. Facilitating inter-ministerial coordination for climate-informed and integrated planning 
 
68. 
In order to create an enabling environment to mainstream climate change considerations into planning 
processes in public entities of relevance to water management and livelihoods development, the project aims to 
promote the inter-ministerial coordination and flow of information. This Activity will support coordination between the 
Thai Meteorological Department, the Hydro and Agro Informatics Institute, the Geo-Informatics and Space Technology 
Development Agency, the Department of Water Resources, and the Hydrographic Department of the Royal Thai Navy 
for improved information sharing. The project will provide technical support in developing standard operating 
procedures (SOPs) for the related committee for information sharing across government stakeholders, established 
under the TMD mechanism/forum. This will entail reviewing existing practices for dissemination of climate information, 
revising in consultation with partners and related sensitization.   
 
69. 
Support from the project will ensure consideration of existing committees to ensure complementarity, including 
integration with ongoing national adaptation plan (NAP) processes, which include capacity building and coordination 
efforts.  As this project is an adaptation priority for the RTG, the project activities feed into national and international 
commitment on climate action.  The Government has established the National Committee on Climate Change Policy 
(NCCC), chaired by the Prime Minister. The NCCC is responsible for (i) national climate change policy and strategy; 
(ii) determination of national positions in international negotiations under the UNFCCC and any relevant international 
agreements; and (iii) monitoring and evaluating implementation results of government agencies, as stated in the 
national policy and strategy. There are four sub-committees under the NCCC, namely, the Sub-Committee on Climate 
Change Policy and Planning Integration, the Sub-Committee on Climate Change Knowledge and Database, the Sub-
Committee on Climate Change Negotiation and International Cooperation, and the Sub-Committee on Action for 
Climate Empowerment and Public Relations. Each Ministry has issued an order for the appointment of a Climate 
Change Focal Officer to ensure inter-ministerial coordination. According to the reporting line, the Director General of 
RID reports to the Permanent Secretary of the Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives (MoAC). Senior Management 
at RID is part of the Sub-Committees, and through this role can help ensure knowledge sharing, lessons learned from 
the project. The Sub-Committee members will be invited to the project seminar/workshop bi-annually under the Activity 
1.2.   In addition, Thailand’s National Adaptation Plan (NAP) is under the Cabinet approval process and will then submit 
to the UNFCCC. UNDP/GCF is also supporting ONEP to update and implementation of the NAP. ONEP is expected to 
get an approval by the end of this year.  
 
70.

opportunity for collaboration and complementarity

Output 2:    Improve water management through strengthened infrastructure complemented by EbA measures, 
for greater resilience to climate change impacts 
(GCF Grant: USD 9.640 million, Co-financing USD 13.485 million) 
74. 
The second Output focuses on complementing RID investment to improve water management in Yom-Nan 
river basin area. GCF resources will support the necessary assessments to ensure that existing designs for the canal 
regulators/flood gates at 13 schemes (see Figure 7 below) are adequate given future climate projections.  Given the 
high upfront costs of the basin-wide investment required to adapt to climate change, this will ensure long term 
sustainability of government investment. GCF will also complement government co-financing and planned long term 
investment by RID in the entire Yom-Nan river basin by supporting the upgrade of infrastructure at two of the planned 
schemes (13 schemes in total).   
 
75. 
Water management in Thailand is largely dependent on grey infrastructure, although there are best practices 
available where use of reservoirs has been established (i.e. Monkey Cheek). GCF resources will support RID in 
identifying and implementing ecosystems-based approaches to complement the planned grey infrastructure.  While 
ecosystems-based measures alone cannot provide address all climate risk challenges, complementing grey 
infrastructure with ecosystems-based solutions can be cost-efficient solution while also generating environmental co-
benefits.  This integration of EbA approaches has the potential to be transformational to Thailand’s traditional planning 
concepts for water management that focus widely on hard structure interventions, through upscaling across the 13 
schemes in the Yom-Nan river basin area and the greater Chao Phraya River Basin.  
 
Activity 2.1.   Climate-informed engineering designs for the 13 schemes of the Yom-Nan river basin, and 
upgrade of 2 water infrastructure  
 
76. 
The climate information and enhanced coordination of Output 1 will inform the review of existing designs for 
water infrastructure, ensuring they are adequate given climate change and importantly that they are both responsive to 
the needs of people in the immediate Yom and Nan river basin area, as well as adequate to perform the function of 
water management as part of the greater Chao Phraya River Basin under projected climate change.  Based on the 
review, enhancements will be made to the existing designs as necessary, including those to be supported through co-
financing under this project.   
 
77. 
While the review of designs will be for the all 13 schemes, GCF resources will only support the actual upgrades 
at one scheme at Khlong Muang Chang – which is considered as a critical scheme by RID as part of the canal and 
regulatory network in the Yom and Nan river basins.  GCF resources will cover the upgrade construction cost of the 
two floodgates, size 6.00 * 5.00 meters, two-drainage type - increasing drainage capacity from 50 cubic meters/second 
to 100 cubic meters/second for flood mitigation and water storage for agriculture and consumption in Kong Kailash 
district in Sukhothai, and the Bang Rakam district in Phitsanulok. The cost is estimated at USD 5 million and includes 
contractual services and equipment for the canal head regulator and related appurtenant works. Ownership of related 
immovable objects will ultimately be with RID. 
 
78. 
These upgrades alone however will not be sufficient, therefore the RTG through RID co-financing will support 
dredging canal and strengthening embankment at the Klong Muang Chang canal (USD 3 million), and operation and 
maintenance in Yom-Nan river basin (USD 4.24 million) as well as reconstruction of the Khlong Ta Kay culvert located 
in the Phrom Piram district, Phitsanulok province (USD 4 million). Further, RTG through RID has allocated resources 
for operations and maintenance of canals and regulators, including: maintenance of irrigation canals, headwork and

43 Monkey Cheek is a flood-control concept initiated by His Majesty the King of Thailand, who observed that most monkeys, when they 
obtain bananas, usually store them in their mouths, and then gradually chew and swallow them. The concept of water detention is to 
temporarily store excessive water during flooding period and afterwards gradually drain it, once the floods recede.

Activity 2.2.  Complementing of grey infrastructure with EbA measures and Integration of EbA approaches into 
water management policy and planning 
 
81. 
Activity 2.2 focuses on the promotion of EbA approaches in Thailand in order to widen their range of adaptation 
options in the field of water management to cope with climate change induced pressures. The project seeks to retrofit 
ecosystem-based adaptation processes into the area, through the re-introduction and/or strengthening of integrated 
farming practices (such as rice-fish management), as well as the re-introduction and strengthening of vegetation and 
waterflow systems, within the reinforcement of the existing canal channels and large irrigation system structures already 
in place. Given that much of the project is focused on the retention of water in the upper tracts of the project area – 
some areas will have water areas increased (e.g. monkey cheeks43) whereby wetlands management techniques can 
be employed, and the re-introduction of some of the original fauna and flora can take place. The ‘greening’ of reservoirs

=== PAGE 25 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.0 | PAGE 21 OF 65 
 
 
and canals is likely to take place through use in part of riparian vegetation sections – to act in combination with sediment 
filtration systems for more effective soil management and anti-topsoil runoff loss solutions. Ecosystem-based 
Adaptation (EbA) measures, in terms of improvements to water infrastructure through restoration principles include 
vegetating river banks, using erosion management techniques combined with vegetation buffers to conserve soil and 
maintain water quality, the slowing down of water flow, and the recharge of groundwater sources during periods high 
precipitation for water access during dry periods; soil conservation measures, among others. Appropriate measures 
will be introduced to complement the existing water management to slow water flows during flood periods and enable 
water to permeate to the ground, and to mitigate potential environmental impacts from the construction of two proposed 
hard infrastructures. These may include: wetland / floodplain / oxbow lake restoration; living weir; riparian zone 
rehabilitation / bank stabilization  by vetiver grass, buffer zones, sedimentation traps and/or an appropriate combination 
of measures (see Figure below).  Support to national partners will also include capacity building for EbA, integration of 
agriculture and biodiversity into EbA measures, floodplain development and conservation, river restoration, fish 
migration structures, and longitudinal connectivity of the Yom River. 
 
 
Figure 8: Example of a typical cross-section with berms and vegetation 
 
 
82. 
Indicative EbA measures used for proposal development are based on the variable factors of the potential EbA 
features and the physical profile of each location, and the related costs per site for budgeting are based on previous 
EbA interventions in Thailand. Assessments and analyses preceding selection will consider interventions which are 
effective while benefiting the most, in terms of both area and beneficiaries. Since the EbA measures will be undertake 
in and around the cultivated areas, the spatial coverage is indeed estimated based on these areas. The estimation will 
be vetted and refined during the participatory process with the communities whereby the final determination of the EbA 
measures will be made. A comprehensive Annex 18(b) - Lessons Learned from Baseline Projects has been included 
in the FP package detailing the process by which EbA measures will be identified/selected. In addition, the proposal 
package includes the safeguards measures to be put in place to protect farmers in the area. Please refer to Annex 6(b) 
– Environmental and Social Management Framework.
