44 https://www.adaptationcommunity.net/ecosystem-based-adaptation/eba-mainstreaming-cycle/ 
 
 
83. 
Following best practices and lessons learned, final selection of the EbA measures will follow a consultative 
process, informed by vulnerability and economic assessments. GIZ is a Responsible Party for implementing this activity.  
GIZ’s framework for mainstreaming EbA employs an iterative six-step approach (known as the adaptation 
mainstreaming cycle, see below). It also provides tools and methods that for each step. This guidance is in line with 
the Voluntary guidelines for the design and effective implementation of ecosystem-based approaches to climate change 
adaptation and disaster risk reduction upon request by the CBD Conference of the Parties (COP), in its decision XIII/4, 
to which GIZ contributed44.  Further, this approach is consistent with national regulations and policies.  On 2 February 
2021, the NWRC has announced a new regulation that ‘In any area of water resource management initiatives, 
information must be provided sufficiently to the private sector, relevant people and communities to create awareness 
that will be useful in participating in the management of water resources in the area’.  The consultative selection process 
will also formalize responsibility and accountability among district authorities, the Department of Water Resources and 
RID for maintenance of the investments.  Should identified measures exceed the budget available in the GCF project, 
additional resources will be requested from government partners.   
 
 
Figure 9: Adaptation mainstreaming cycle 
 
84. 
A climate-sensitive IWRM approach needs to be applied in order to improve the framework conditions for 
sustainable water management and allow for better mainstreaming of adaptation targets, incl. EbA, into the water 
sector. The concept of climate-sensitive IWRM focusses on strengthening capacities in the planning process of water 
related bodies while integrating climate change adaptation needs in policy frameworks (national level) as well as in the 
planning and design of water management measures at river basin scale (sub-national level). This includes a 
comprehensive capacity development strategy using improved diagnostic analysis as well as emphasizing the 
prioritization and budgeting of most suitable adaptation actions. It will be essential to raise the awareness of the direct 
and co-benefits of ecosystems, their economic value and critical role for adaptation to climate change. To contribute to 
the overall monitoring and evaluation of the impact of the different measures in terms of water security, on the flood 
situation and on socio-economic developments digital tools (incl. UAVs/ drones) shall be applied. The digital solution 
based M&E methodology that will enable monitoring quantitative and qualitative changes at river basin scale is 
established and operated in collaboration with academia and linked to local and national monitoring system(s) of 
institutions like HII for the documentation of and reporting on climate change adaptation actions in the water sector.   
 
85. 
The EbA measures are to be integrated in a climate-sensitive River Basin Management approach (reflected in 
the River Basin Master Plan – RBMP) that builds on climate risk and vulnerability information and an integrated water 
resources management (IWRM) concept. Quantity & quality of societal benefits are to be reflected as well (in terms of 
impact on livelihoods e.g. source of income, reduced risks as well as in terms of ecosystem services e.g. reduced

=== PAGE 27 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.0 | PAGE 23 OF 65 
 
 
erosion, extreme event buffering for flood and drought situations).  In close cooperation with the Thai water sector GIZ 
is currently in the process of developing an EbA Guidebook as a national standard for the systematic identification, 
prioritization and selection of EbA measures at river basin scale. The EbA Guidebook is to be complemented by a Code 
of Practice (CoP) for selected EbA measures that will cover specific design issues. The EbA Guidebook and CoP are 
to be finalized by end of 2021 and are planned to be published under the Thai Office of the National Water Resources 
(ONWR). RID and DWR are participating in the development of the EbA Guidebook and CoP.  The national standard 
builds besides others on the Guidebook on Climate Risk Assessment for Ecosystem-based Adaptation and joint cause-
impact chain analysis as key elements of a joint prioritization and selection process for effective EbA measures.  
https://www.adaptationcommunity.net/wp-content/uploads/2018/06/giz-eurac-unu-2018-en-guidebook-climate-risk-
asessment-eba.pdf.

2.2.5.

Output 3:   Reduce volatility of agriculture livelihoods in drought and flood prone areas through strengthened 
extension support and local planning, investment in on-farm adaptation measures and greater access to 
finance and markets 
(GCF Grant: USD 5.617 million, Co-financing USD 0.361 million) 
87. 
The project area is dominated by agriculture activities.  Farmers in the area are particularly vulnerable to 
climatic conditions and would be directly impacted by any interventions in the area.  It is critical therefore that 
interventions in the project area engage farmers, and the project is informed by their experiences and needs.

=== PAGE 28 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.0 | PAGE 24 OF 65 
 
 
 
Figure 10: Land use map of project area 
 
88. 
During consultations, farmers expressed the need for specific support to improve their climate resilience, (i.e. 
not just information on the design, but tailored support on implementation of measures to better cope with changing 
climatic conditions). Farmers requested guidance for on-farm measures for water storage during the dry season, 
support on drought resilient species and any related changes to agriculture practices to ensure viability of crops and 
help with business skills for better market access.  To that end, under Output 3, GCF will support training to inform 
climate-informed decision-making related to agriculture practices, technical expertise and implementation of on-farm 
interventions for farmers in the immediate project site, with related training materials that can reach across the 
agriculture sector in Thailand through Farmer Field Training (FFT).   
 
89. 
While the majority of farmers in the project sites grow rice, there are different types of agriculture and agriculture 
land in the area.  Interventions and training will be tailored to what is suitable and proven effective in those conditions.

Activity 3.1. Application of climate information in household agriculture planning and strengthening related 
support through extension services 
 
91. 
Effective application of climate information into household agriculture planning will require shifting the crop 
choice and crop calendar to correspond to the changes in temperatures and rainfall patterns.  For this, inter-agency 
coordination at the local, provincial, and central levels is needed to adequately support agriculture households to make 
the necessary changes in order to successfully adapt to climate change.  
 
92. 
Based on the tailored climate information of Output 1 and a close collaboration with the IRDM programme and 
with KMUTNB, GCF resources will support knowledge sharing and training to provincial extension officers from the 
Department of Agricultural Promotion under MoAC and agriculture households on climate change impacts on 
agriculture in the Yom and Nan river basins. GCF resources will support awareness raising, informed adaptation 
measures and training to communities, such as related to appropriate timing for agriculture practices and agriculture 
crops or technologies which are less water-intensive (e.g. precision farming, application of Unmanned Aerial Vehicle 
(UAV)). Conservation agriculture, on-farm water storage and aquaculture, linkages to market for diversified crop options 
are some of the key supportive measures to improving agricultural livelihood resilience. 
 
93. 
Informed by vulnerability and risk assessment under Output 2 and the climate information of Output 1, district 
level action plans will be developed.  The action plans will also provide a basis for the type of support households in 
the project area will require.  For instance, where assessments indicate water scarcity issues for the village regions of 
the project target area, action plans may include the constructing of on-farm ponds. Agriculture extension officers will 
receive training to support the changing needs of farmers in the area.  
 
94. 
In partnership with King Mongkut's University of Technology North Bangkok (KMUTNB), training will be 
provided to farmers in 22 sub-districts on using and applying the information from the app developed under Output 1.  
Training will include adaptation measures such as, changes to crop choice and crop calendar which may be required 
due to climatic changes, and that result in both reliable productivity as well as more sustainable use of natural resources 
by farmers.   
 
95. 
The project will provide technical support and bring in local experience to share lessons learned and best 
practices related to principles and application of, for example, conservation agriculture, organic agriculture, 
agroecology, integrated farming, as well as water saving approaches such as water saving techniques related to rice, 
such as system of rice intensification (SRI) to respond to drier conditions and changing rainfall patterns45. 
 
96. 
Technical support applies to both extension services and Farmer Field Schools.  The Famer Field School model 
is established as a platform for farmers to provide practical learning approaches for livelihoods and in replicating good 
practice techniques. Whilst being complementary to the Agricultural Extension Services approach and provision, the 
Farm Field School differs in that it uses participatory teaching methods and is specific to adults and farmer needs – 
providing opportunity for farmer experimentation with different farming techniques and crop varieties, allowing 
demonstration, and generating observation and analysis of the outcomes.   GCF resources will support climate-resilient 
agriculture trainings to farmers through existing farmer field schools, which will serve a long-term mandate of facilitating 
a transformation in agricultural practice through the promotion of ecologically supportive climate smart practices. In 
consultations during project development, farmers expressed training fatigue, asking that training be accompanied by 
on-farm support.  The project will support on-farm and community-based activities, including demonstrations and 
regular follow ups. 
 
97. 
Thailand has recognised the potential for technology to provide positive results for improved production, quality 
and agricultural products, and importantly their ability to be resilient to climate change. The National Strategy -Thailand 
4.0 includes ambitious targets for agriculture, aiming to transit traditional agriculture to smart agriculture and increase 
farmer income by fivefold in 20 years; leveraging innovation and digital transformation. The advancement of technology 
in agriculture is also in line with Thailand’s 20 year Agricultural Development Plan (2017-2036) and “MOAC’s Digital

=== PAGE 30 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.0 | PAGE 26 OF 65 
 
 
Agriculture Strategic Plan (2017-2021)” (For further details, refer to paragraph 153). Therefore, the project aims to pilot 
precision agriculture with IoT system and the usage of drones (see illustrative figure below).  
 
 
 
 
98. 
Precision agriculture will be employed at five sites, covering 50 farms. IoT sensors will be installed at the farm 
and drones with multispectral camera will be used for agricultural area survey. Data will be collected and processed on 
the cloud server by using the AI from commercial software. The data from IoT sensors and the drones will be linked 
with the result of climate change data forecasting for decision making. Related information could help inform farmers 
to divide their fields into smaller and homogeneous zones, assess water quantity needs and even predict crop yield.  
Equipment will be owned and maintained by the district, and data incorporated into activities under Outputs 1 and 2.  
Results will be documented and disseminated to farmers in the area as well as provinces/districts with similar landscape 
and climatic conditions.  As data can inform district planning, further upscaling can be done by district authorities 
through public resources – consistent with Thailand 4.0 aims to shift Thailand’s agriculture sector towards an innovative-
driven and interconnected sector through the integration of the concepts and approaches from sufficiency economy, 
smart farm, precision agriculture, intensive agriculture, sustainable agriculture, green economy, and agricultural 
biotechnology. A more tailored application of the technology may be upscaled by farmers through available loan 
instruments (for larger farms) or through the credit to be established by BAAC (see Sub-activity 3.2.5).  Trainings will 
be combined where appropriate for efficiency purposes. .  It is to be noted that in the project, district authorities would 
be responsible for O&M for this intervention and related resource commitments (e.g., personnel/finance) would be 
confirmed before roll out of interventions. 
 
 
It is not expected that individual small farmers invest in IoT/drone technology, though groups of farmers may find it 
feasible – bridging the technology gap between small and larger farms; in additional, data collected from IoT can help 
enable policy and planning therefore benefiting smallholder farms.  The use of IoT is meant not only to improve farm 
outcomes (e.g., increased yields), but also to provide information that can help with local planning and resource 
allocation (this is in line with National Strategy-Agriculture 4.0, which aims to shift Thailand’s agriculture sector towards 
an innovation-driven and interconnected sector).  By focusing on smallholder farmers for the intervention, data is 
collected from farms that need the greatest support.  IoT is an innovative technology (approx. $1M of the total GCF

Activity 3.2.  Implementation of on-farm climate resilient measures to improve drought and flood resilience and 
improved access to finance for sustainable agriculture 
 
100. 
Based on results of cause-effect analysis and related action plans, on-farm water storage and groundwater 
recharge mechanisms will also be explored, including on farm storage/rainwater infrastructure, installation of 
underground water traps 48 , vegetation management to promote recharge, canal and waterway management 
constructed and natural wetlands, and so forth 49. Techniques assist to prevent the dropping of the watershed. 
Groundwater recharge collects the water during the wet season for use during the dry season when the demand is 
highest. In the cases where farmers are using chemical fertilizers and pesticides – artificial recharge must only be done 
using filtration/percolation techniques as to prevent contamination. The project will, however, promote the usage of 
organic alternatives to chemical fertilizers and pesticides.   
 
101. 
To support the EbA measures of Output 2, community nurseries will be established.  The preparation for the 
seedling nursery will be in small pieces of land equipped with water pumps to drain those submerged pieces of land. 
In other cases, the project will support farmers to cooperate and establish community nurseries on higher ground.  
Importantly, this can provide means of longer term support to vulnerable farmers during periods of flooding.   Ownership 
of the community nurseries will be with the famer cooperatives in each sub-district. These cooperatives operate under 
the legal framework, managed by the Cooperative Promotion Department, under the MoCA.  GCF will also support 
investment in small scale equipment to farmers to support EbA through water conservation and better soil management 
(e.g. alternate wetting and drying technologies that measure more precisely the amount of water needed for rice).  
Selection criteria for beneficiaries will be guided by vulnerability and priority, and economic needs of beneficiaries. 
Economic needs include a) elderly farmers, b) newly graduated (unemployed) youth, households with savings not 
higher than 50,000 THB and household income not higher than 100,000 THB, and c) households with usable area not

102. 
To support farmers with access to finance for investments, GCF resources will also support the co-design of a 
credit instrument for farmers practicing climate resilient agriculture.  BAAC has committed USD 16 million for this effort.  
It has been counted as parallel finance for the proposal and the commitment letter is part of Annex 13.   The BAAC 
credit will be available to small-scale farmers, community farmers/cooperatives, agriculture SMEs, as well as larger 
agricultural businesses. The range of credit available is expected to be 9,000 THB (USD 300) – 10 million THB (USD 
320,000).  The co-design of the credit instrument will follow a consultative process engaging relevant ministries and 
communities, to ensure that the product is one that both supports climate resilient agriculture investments and on-farm 
EbA measures, and suits the needs of farmers in the project area.

Activity 3.3.  Capacity building for farmers to support market access for climate resilient agriculture products 
 
104. 
Uncertain yields due to climate change and falling prices for traditional rice varieties create volatility to farmer 
livelihoods. During consultations, farmers shared challenges faced by increased temperatures and changing rainfall 
patterns (particularly drought periods), but also expressed concerns over implementing changing due to the lack of 
knowledge about and access to markets.  Knowledge sharing workshops will be conducted, so that farmers can learn 
from each other and benefit from each other’s best practices and lessons learned.  To support market access, the 
project will establish an online market platform, providing an additional means for farmers to reach consumers. 
 
105. 
GCF resources will also support farmers with market access through training and support with identifying links 
to buyers and markets for climate-resilient agriculture products, including development of contract farming (incl. 
hospitals, schools, restaurants), linkages through rice cooperatives, linkages to local gatherers and processors, 
linkages to private sector and community support agriculture (CSA) for larger markets at Phitsanulok province, and 
comprising of larger market buyers, processors, and cooperatives.  With support from Krungsri Bank, farmers will also 
receive training on business skills to help convert networking opportunities into buyer-seller partnerships.  
 
106. 
Sub-activities include: 
3.3.1. 
Farmer to farmer knowledge sharing workshops, including reciprocal learning processes (e.g. site visits by 
farmer field schools to project schemes)  
3.3.2. 
Establishment of online market platform to increase visibility and marketability of farmers’ products  
3.3.3. Training to farmers to support market access for farmers, including capacity building related to financial literacy, 
linkages to buyers and markets, requirements for contract farming (incl. hospitals, schools, restaurants), 
linkages through rice cooperatives, linkages to local gatherer and processors, linkages to private sector and 
community support agriculture (CSA) for larger markets at Phitsanulok province, and comprising of larger 
market buyers, processor, cooperative

109. 
The Project Board is responsible for making, by consensus, management decisions when guidance is required 
by the National Project Director. Project Board decisions will be made in accordance with standards that shall ensure 
management for development results, best value money, fairness, integrity, transparency, and effective international 
competition. In case a consensus cannot be reached within the Board, final decision shall rest with the UNDP 
Programme Country Director. The Project Board will meet every six months. 
 
110. 
Project Board will consist of a group of representatives responsible for making consensus-based strategic and  
management decisions for the project. It will oversee the project implementation; review compliance with the Royal 
Thai Government, UNDP and GCF requirements; and ensure implementation of the management plan for the risks 
identified. The Board will be comprised of: 
 
• 
An Executive (role represented by National Implementing Partner) that holds the project ownership and 
chairs the Board. The Executive will be RID (Chair). 
• 
A Senior Supplier representative providing guidance regarding the technical feasibility of the project, 
compliance with donor requirements, and rules pertaining to use of project resources. This role will be 
fulfilled by UNDP in its capacity as GCF Accredited Entity;  
• 
Senior Beneficiary representatives who ensures the realisation of project benefits from the perspective of 
project beneficiaries 
 
111. 
The National Project Director is a senior representative from RID who is responsible for the overall direction, 
strategic guidance, and timely delivery of project outputs.  
 
112. 
The Project Manager will run the project on a day-to-day basis within the parameters laid down by the Project 
Board. The Project Manager will end when the final project terminal evaluation report, and other documentation required 
by the GCF and UNDP, has been completed and submitted to UNDP. Project Manager is responsible for day-to-day 
management and decision-making for the project. The National Project Director’s prime responsibility is to ensure that 
the project produces the results specified in the project document, to the required standard of quality and within the 
specified constraints of time and cost.  
 
113. 
UNDP will transfer funds to the Royal Irrigation Department (RID), under the Ministry of Agriculture and 
Cooperative as “implementing partner”.  Once approved, UNDP Thailand will transfer funds to RID for implementation.  
The internal governmental implementation arrangements shall be formalized through a project board established by 
the Director General of RID, under the MoAC (“Project Board”), agreed minutes of the Project Board decision, 
memorandum of understanding and work plan. Furthermore, RID has agreed to select the King Mongkut Institute of 
Technology North Bangkok (KMUTNB) and GIZ based in Thailand as the Responsible Parties for Implementation 
(Output 1, Output 2/Activity 2.2 respectively). The engagement of Responsible Parties shall follow the IP regulations 
and rules. Below is a diagram of the funds flow:

UNDP provides a three-tier oversight and quality assurance role involving UNDP staff in Country Offices and

and independent project oversight and monitoring functions. This role ensures appropriate project management

the Project Board cannot delegate any of its quality assurance responsibilities to the Project Manager. The project 
assurance role is covered by the accredited entity fee provided by the GCF. As an Accredited Entity to the GCF, UNDP 
is required to deliver GCF-specific oversight and quality assurance services including: (i) Day-to-day oversight 
supervision, (ii) Oversight of project completion, (iii) Oversight of project reporting. 
 
B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages) 
116. 
The planned interventions target the Yom and Nan river basins, in Uttaradit, Phitsanulok, and Sukhothai 
provinces, which are key to cope with increasing climate-induced flood and drought risks to enhance the resilience of 
the population living in the wider Chao Phraya River Basin, including the Bangkok Metropolitan Region with around 
15m inhabitants. As described in Section C.2, the river basins currently provide a critical function of managing the river 
flow and regulating irrigation, whereas the last water infrastructure improvement in these areas was in 1994-1995. 
However, in light of climate change the current precipitation patterns change and more frequent consecutive dry periods 
and severe flooding in wet seasons test the limits of the existing infrastructure. This enhances the flood risks as well 
as poses a serious threat to the already marginalised poor local farmers and agriculture day labourers during dry 
periods due to higher risks of losses and damages of yields and feasibility to pursue certain livelihood options. As 
existing water management infrastructure is no longer able to effectively respond to climate change induced changes 
in rainfall patterns, enlarging the storage capacity of irrigation systems, natural canals, or monkey cheeks for keeping 
excess water for later use (i.e. the second dry season rice) is needed. 
 
117. 
Alternative financing options to address transformative change for the most vulnerable farmers as well as to 
enhance the wider water management, particularly introducing innovative EbA approaches, are limited, as the planned 
interventions do not yield aggregate, large-scale financial reflows. A Financial Appraisal was conducted for the project, 
please see Annex 3.  It found that the proposed activities under Output 1 – technical training to staff of RID and TMD, 
strengthening the climate forecasting and water management tool, dissemination and distribution of climate risk 
information to at-risk households through technology – aim to strengthen climate risk-informed planning, decision 
making and inter-ministerial cooperation, generating benefits which are of larger public good in nature such as making 
available easily consumable climate risk-information for households and significant improvement in capacities of 
government agencies. Similarly, public good nature of benefits will be generated from Output 3, which will train farmers 
on climate risk-informed decision making in their agricultural practices, provide technical training on building on-farm

=== PAGE 36 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.0 | PAGE 32 OF 65 
 
 
water management interventions for farmers and help farmers develop business skills for developing market access. 
While the proposed activities under Output 2 build grey infrastructure in the form of upgraded floodgates and canal 
embankments, the resulting benefits are larger good in nature without directly identifiable and measurable financial 
flows to either the project beneficiaries or the project sponsor. The increased storage capacity improves access to 
water for agriculture and public consumption during dry seasons and the increased drainage capacity mitigates / 
minimizes the impact on / losses to agricultural lands. It must also be noted here that the direct beneficiaries from the 
investment in grey infrastructure are the most-vulnerable groups in the region with little or no ability to afford increased 
cash outflows.  Therefore, the proposed project will not generate any “directly identifiable, attributable and quantifiable” 
incremental financial revenues or produce any “directly identifiable, attributable and quantifiable” tangible financial cost 
recovery/savings of resources to either the project owner (Royal Thai Government - RTG) or to the project beneficiaries 
(direct beneficiaries in the targeted project area) during and after implementation of the proposed project. Due to this 
larger public good nature of the benefits derived from this funding proposal, repayment of any kind of loan from the 
project benefits / beneficiaries is not feasible.  
 
118. 
GCF involvement is critical for this proposal since there is an overwhelming evidence of that amidst other 
potential factors such as increasing population, degradation of ecosystem and poor management of the grey 
infrastructure, climate change being can be attributed as the only major factor behind the increasing frequency and 
severity of extreme climate-related flooding and drought events in the project area of The Greater Chao Phraya river 
basin. In addition, alternative financing options to address transformative change for the most vulnerable farmers are 
limited as the planned interventions do not yield any aggregate, large scale financial reflows.  
 
119. 
An integrated project such as this one, however, can usher in the paradigm shift needed to adapt to the climate 
impacts on the water and agriculture sectors in Thailand.  The project will inform water planning and investment not 
only in the Yom-Nan river basin, but also the broader Chao Phraya river basin.  Upscaling and replication will support 
Thailand’s most vulnerable/poor communities, which have a largely rural profile.  Further, the project can prove catalytic 
in mobilizing additional resources, as is already evident in the co-financing from Krungsri Bank to strengthen financial 
literacy among farmers, and the parallel financing from BAAC’s in the form of a USD 16M credit for farmers practicing 
climate informed and sustainable agriculture.  In addition, UNDP Thailand is laying the groundwork for greater 
engagement of the private sector.  UNDP Thailand has partnered with the Securities and Exchange Commission 
(approximately 750 listed companies) and Global Compact Network Thailand (50 members) to raise awareness among 
businesses on shifting from ESG to SDGs and integrating SDGs Impact Measurement and Management Framework 
into their business and investment strategies.  The first seminar was held on 24 February with over 400 participants 
attending the events, including representatives from listed companies, investors,  financial regulators, government 
officials, and academia.  In 2020, UNDP Thailand is planning to focus on introducing the SDG impact assessment 
framework called the UNDP Business Call to Action's Impact Lab to the market. UNDP Thailand will also select some 
institutional investors to test the UNDP's SDGs Assurance Standard on Private Equity and Bond. To promote the 
localization of SDGs and awareness raising among SMEs, UNDP Thailand is partnering with Thai Chamber of 
Commerce, who has the local presence in all 76 provinces across Thailand. UNDP Thailand will provide sustainability 
related workshops to local SMEs in their network as well as work with these businesses on how to engage SDGs lens 
into their business activities. And in the pipeline, UNDP Thailand is discussion on project development on plastic waste 
management with Unilever, accelerating Social Enterprises with True Digital Park, community investment funds with 
Singha Estate, as well as farming technology demonstration with the Siam KUBOTA.  Agriculture is a critical sector in 
Thailand, and each of these initiative creates opportunities to engage private and finance sector entities in related work 
to support climate resilience.  This information has been incorporated into the FP as well.Such Private sector 
participation in the geography and the sustainable agriculture and water management sector is limited to Corporate 
Social Responsibility (CSR) activities and spending budgets, which the project is already leveraging. Another potential 
option of Donor aid was explored but it typically does not include / understand evolving climate change threats and is 
usually focused on humanitarian activities.   
 
B.6. Exit strategy and sustainability (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

=== PAGE 37 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.0 | PAGE 33 OF 65 
 
 
120. 
The proposed project has been designed in close consultation with and involvement of relevant government 
agencies and technical line departments, development organisations (e.g. GIZ, UNDP) and existing Water User Groups 
in the target river basin. These consultations and discussions are complemented by best practice approaches in water 
management, climate-resilient agriculture, ecosystem based adaptation (EbA), and climate information and modelling 
as detailed in the Feasibility Report (Annex 2). Building on this foundation, the project ensures that the investments as 
well as the results of the interventions are sustained beyond the project period and in the longer-term through the 
following elements of project design and implementation. 
 
121. 
Building on existing and established networks: The project will strengthen existing networks and 
coordination mechanisms, including Water User groups and farmer field schools (existent in project area), as well as 
housing climate information data centres in combination with existing monitoring and information centres – providing 
location and staff after the project duration timeframe is complete. The farmer field trainings to enhance their capacity 
to cope with climate change impacts and learn adaptation strategies, will partly be developed through farmer field 
schools and other existing local networks. This can ensure the sustainability as these knowledge hubs and networks 
will allow the aggregation of traditional knowledge and practices with climate-resilient technologies and innovative 
practices. 
 
122. 
Supporting ecological systems: GCF resources will be used to promote the application of ecological 
principles to water management to trigger a paradigm shift within the RID and RTG’s water management approaches 
towards more integrative solutions (including EbA) following a landscape-ecosystem approach based on the river 
system. Building on the experience of GIZ in the field EbA, the project will work on technical capacity development of 
key decision makers and technical officials to mainstream the, for Thailand, new approach to water management into 
their water and irrigation planning processes. The technical capacity building will be undertaken at both national and 
subnational (project area) levels to reach a variety of key public officials to sustain the efforts and reach long-term 
project goals.  
 
123. 
The project invests in institutional capacity building, so for longevity as well as for integrating cross-sectoral 
and inter-agency approaches to shared project goals. It, further, promotes institutional planning and coordination across 
government officials and communities. Project outputs will also contribute to enhancing organisational capacity of 
farmers to plan for and implement climate-risk informed local water management solutions and integrate climate 
information and advisories for water management ensuring their financial and human resource viability post-project. 
The approach is to strongly engage communities at several levels of planning and execution. By doing so, the project 
will not only ensure that the investments respond to beneficiary needs but also ensure that community organisations, 
particularly including women, will have capacity to keep improving approaches, even as climate variability increases 
and seasons become more unpredictable.  
 
124. 
Co-investments by government institutions, financial sector and communities: The project leverages 
domestic co-financing with a total co-financing contribution of the Royal Thai Government through the Royal Irrigation 
Department (RID) of USD 16.26 million during the 5-year implementation period, and an additional USD 17.26 million 
for operation and maintenance beyond the GCF-funded project period. RTG resources will support investments into 
water management infrastructures, technical capacity development of public officials, IRDM upgrade, skills transfer to 
farmers, market linkage creation, and the promotion of climate change resilient livelihoods promote. They will also 
support implementation of resilient drinking water solutions, especially shouldering the cost of project and post-project 
O&M for the implemented infrastructure, as well as aim to install the remaining 11 proposed water management 
infrastructures in the Yom and Nan river basins in the coming decades through their normal budget allocations (in case 
they can’t be constituted through EbA approaches). In addition, the project leverages financial sector resources with 
USD 113,883 from the Krungsri Bank (part of GCF accredited entity MUFG) to conduct financial literacy training for 
local farmers in the project area.  To support further investments by farmers, BAAC has committed USD 16 million in 
credit to support farmers applying climate informed practices. Investments may include, for instance, climate smart 
farming technology and supplies, online marketing and sale of their organic rice, vegetable, fruit, startup business 
capital, social enterprise set up and cash flow. The BAAC financial product will be designed through a collaborative 
process, supported by the GCF project.  
 
125. 
Ex-post plan for Operations and Maintenance of observing equipment, water management investment 
and IoT: An O&M plan (project and for post-project O&M), including the budgeting for the human and financial 
resources required for O&M for the project investments, is presented in Annex 21. The plan reflects local ownership 
and commitment for the long-term sustainability of the project activities and outcomes. The costs of developing a long-
term strategy for O&M, is provided for in the first two years, and the strategy will be reassessed towards project 
completion (final two years). GCF resources will finance and leverage financing to support the human and technical 
resources required for O&M initially, with a decreasing contribution towards the end of the project lifetime, after which

=== PAGE 38 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.0 | PAGE 34 OF 65 
 
 
 
 
domestic financing (from the budgets of designated authorities as reflected in the co-financing letters) will continue to 
support O&M.  RID will maintain facilities under Output 1 and 2, and other MoAC agencies will run Output 3 under 
future government regular budget.  Please see Annex 21 for the O&M plan. 
 
126. 
As the IoT equipment (including drones) will be owned by district government, local authorities will be ultimately 
responsible for O&M.  Some of the O&M cost is built into the sale price and warrantee from the dealers in the domestic 
market (Thailand). As IoT is promoted in Thailand, there are various avenues available to secure finance for any 
additional O&M as well as for further replication and upscaling. These include: a) the regular public budget, distributed 
to government departments under MoAC, b) the regular public budget for capacity building to farmers and innovation 
research and c) the Communication Authority of Thailand (CAT). District authorities will provide commitments for O&M 
prior to any roll-out of IoT.   
 
127. 
Ownership of equipment to be provided by the project: Most of the equipment will be owned by the farmers 
themselves (eg. water pumps, water level gauges), while others will be shared and therefore owned by farmer 
groups/cooperatives (eg cultivator, rotary weeder).  For shared equipment, procedures will follow existing borrowing 
system, managed by the cooperative.  Following this borrowing system, the day-to-day operations (fuel, manpower) 
would be the responsibility of the borrower.  Maintenance would be responsibility of the cooperative, with costs covered 
by the group’s bank account.  Ownership will follow the same set up as during the project.  The project will support 
farmers groups in the budgeting process if needed, to ensure funds are planned for regular maintenance of equipment.  
Project results will be tracked and shared with farmers and cooperatives to inform decision making for further 
replication/scale up.  Should additional funds be needed for further replication/scale up, the BAAC credit will be 
available for investments in adaptation.

C.2. Financing by component  
128. 
The project seeks USD 17.534 million in GCF support with a total co-financing of USD 16.378 million which 
includes  co-financing of USD 16.264 million from the Royal Thai Government (RTG) through the Royal Irrigation 
Department (RID) during the implementation period for operations and maintenance (O&M) of the GCF investment, as 
well as for (i) repair and improvement of the existing irrigation schemes, (ii) upgrading of the Khlong Muang Chang 
canal regulator and (iii) improvement of the Khong Ta Key culvert; and co-financing from Krungsri Bank (a member of 
MUFG-global financial group) that will contribute USD 113,883 during the project implementation period to provide 
financial literacy training to farmers in the project area – complementing GCF-funded activities under Output 3. In 
addition, the RTG has also committed USD 17.254 million for O&M for 15 years beyond the GCF-funded project period. 
 
129. 
In combination, GCF funds and co-financing will be used to realize 3 project outputs, namely to (i) enhance 
climate and risk informed planning in the water and agricultural sectors, (ii) improve water through strengthened 
infrastructure complemented by EbA measures, for greater resilience to climate change impacts and (iii) reduce volatility 
of agriculture livelihoods in drought and flood prone areas through strengthened extension support and local planning, 
investment in on-farm adaptation measures and greater access to finance and markets. To further support farmers 
access finance for adaptation measures, the Bank for Agriculture and Agricultural Cooperatives (BAAC) has pledged a 
line of credit of USD 16 million through financial products with incentives for farmers to practice climate resilience.  A 
letter of commitment is included in Annex 13.  This has been counted as parallel financing for this project. 
 
130.

implementation of the Activities referred to above. With respect to the Co-financing to be provided by Krungsri Bank, 
the arrangement shall be formalized through a memorandum of understanding and work plan. The commitment and

131. 
Under Output 1, the project will support capacity and technological needs for tailored climate information to 
inform climate resilient water management and agriculture planning, while under Output 2 the project will support 
capacity for climate sensitive integrated water resources management. Output 3 includes training to agriculture 
extension officers to support farmers with climate resilient agriculture, as well as equipment related to precision 
agriculture and development of an app to sell agriculture products. The total requested for these activities is 
approximately USD 4 million.

 EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA  
This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the 
GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.  
D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
132. 
The project will contribute to following GCF Fund-Level Impacts for adaptation:  
• 
Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people, communities and regions through 
the promotion of climate-resilient, sustainable and diversified livelihoods for 62,000 beneficiaries in the Yom 
and Nan river basins (Fund-Level Impact A1.0)  
• 
Increased resilience of health and well- being, and food and water security for through enhanced access to 
irrigation and water management practices (Fund-Level Impact A2.0) 
• 
Increased resilience of infrastructure and the built environment to climate change threats, through the upgrades 
of the critical water regulating facilities of Khlong Muang Chang and Khlong Ta Kay Reg and EbA measures to 
reduce flood risks for 25 million people in the Chao Phraya river basin as well as build environment, including 
Metropolitan Bangkok Area with 15 million inhabitants (Fund-Level impact A3.0) 
• 
And Increased resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem services, through the implementation and promotion 
of EbA measures (Fund-Level Impact A4.0) 
 
133. 
The project will reduce vulnerability by enhancing adaptive capacity and resilience of rural populations.  By 
applying a gender-sensitive approach, agriculture based communities will receive climate information and support to 
apply that information towards climate resilient agriculture practices, thereby reducing volatility brought about by climate 
change induced floods and droughts.  The project will also support institutional capacity for climate-responsive planning 
and development in water and agriculture management through the expected increase in use of climate information in 
decision making.  And support to upgrading critical infrastructure will enhance ability of water retention and 
management in the Yom and Nan river basins to cope with an intensification of extreme rain events and enhance the 
capacity to store water for irrigation purposes during increasingly drier dry periods; and strengthened institutional 
capacities, knowledge, and learning for climate-resilient water management and livelihoods security. 
 
134. 
The Yom and Nan river basins are of critical importance, as water management in this area would not only 
ease the severity of flooding for farmers in the vicinity, but also further downstream in the broader Great Chao Phraya 
river basin. In addition, adequate water management will reduce drought related risks to crops and agriculture-based 
livelihoods in the project region. The enhancements detailed would minimize loss and damage related to flooding and 
drought - benefiting vulnerable agriculture households and relieving pressure on public resources for related relief and 
programmes. 
 
135. 
The project incorporates lessons learned and best practices from several successful international efforts in 
order to enable a transformative impact through the climate informed improvement of water resource management and 
climate change resilient livelihoods planning. These incorporated best practices include the utilisation of Ecosystem-
based Adaptation (EbA) solutions for climate resilient water management and livelihood development. This integration 
of EbA approaches has the potential to be transformational to Thailand’s traditional planning concepts for water 
management that focus widely on hard structure interventions and could also be replicated (scaled-up) in the other 
river basins in Thailand (total 25). 
 
136. 
In addition, the policy support activities under the proposed GCF project and capacity development, e.g. in the 
field of water management modelling and forecasting under the updated IRDM tool and integration of EbA methods 
into climate-resilient planning and solution prioritisation processes, has the potential to be replicated in and set new 
standards for government entities involved in water management planning and livelihoods promotion in Thailand.  
 
2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

=== PAGE 43 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.0 | PAGE 39 OF 65 
 
 
 
 
137. 
The proposed project integrates sustainability strategies and pathways for replication and scale to catalyze 
impacts beyond the project duration. RTG aims to build on this project to showcase innovative solutions in the field of 
climate resilient water management and livelihoods promotion. Successful implementation of this project can transform 
traditional approaches of hard structure focused interventions for water management, provide access of farmers to 
tailored climate information, alternative livelihood options (including a focus on organic production), create vertical and 
horizontal linkages to buyers and suppliers, and provide RID with a new planning tool for localized irrigation and yield 
forecasting. All these climate change responsive solutions can be replicated and scaled up through the integration into 
planning and implementation in other river basins in Thailand. 
 
138. 
In particular, the following activities advance the potential for transformative impact beyond the project and 
form a paradigm shift potential of the project: 
 
o 
The upgrade and enhancement of a rainfall forecasting and operating tool. The project will enable RID, and its 
key academic institutions, to build on the IRDM tool to allow RID staff to forecast local rainfall patterns and 
runoff simulation. These forecasting and operating models is a key decision-making tool to inform water 
management to prevent floods and provide sufficient water for irrigation during dry periods. The tailored and 
localised information will, furthermore, be provided to subnational public entities and farmers (end-users) 
through mobile phone applications. This can inform local and farm-level decision making in light of increasing 
climate change induced flood and drought risks, as well as enhance transparency and water distribution 
decision-making in order to mitigate potential water distribution conflicts between water infrastructure operators 
and local stakeholders. 
 
o 
Ensure costly investments in grey infrastructure are climate informed.  By integrating climate change into water 
management infrastructure planning, the project supports cost-efficient investments – extending the life of the 
investment, and reducing costs related to loss and damage related to climate impacts and subsequent 
upgrades. 
o 
Develop RID’s capacities to integrate ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) in water management. While EbA 
measures have been tested in Thailand, RID’s interventions to enhance water management in the Chao Phraya 
river basin, and other basins in Thailand are traditionally focused on engineering solutions that utilize grey 
infrastructure. This project will develop the evidence base to support complementing grey infrastructure with 
ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) measures. Given the high upfront costs of grey infrastructure, the 
successful implementation of this project could showcase a effective and sustainable solutions, which are also 
lower cost.  This can be a scalable solution to increase the resilience of Thailand’s water infrastructure and, 
consequently, of the farmers dependent on the irrigation system in light of increasing climate change impacts, 
while rehabilitating ecosystems in Thailand’s river basins.  
o 
The project sets out the process of identifying local level agricultural community vulnerabilities to climate 
change impacts and livelihood response for farmers, as well as setting forth best practice measures and 
strategies in the planning and management. In light of increasing climate change impacts it is inevitable for the 
farmers to incorporate these increasing risks into their farming activities and livelihood planning. The project 
will enable the farmers, through capacity building, financial training (delivered through a private sector entity), 
and the introduction of adaptation technologies, alternative climate-resilient crops, and farming techniques to 
cope with increasing risks. 
 
o 
Integration of finance/private sector actors (e.g. Krungsri Bank and BAAC) into implementation of EbA 
technologies, support for climate resilient and sustainable agriculture investments and capacity building of 
farmers through financial trainings. In addition, the project will create linkages between farmers and buyers of 
climate resilient agriculture products. 
 
D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

=== PAGE 44 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.0 | PAGE 40 OF 65 
 
 
 
 
139. 
The project generates a number of co-benefits, not only technological advancement in climate change informed 
analysis, but also contributions towards social and economic development: 
• 
Reduced losses and damages due to enhanced planning for investment and preparation for slow onset climate 
change impacts as well as extreme events:  
• 
More efficient use of water resources, in line with sufficiency economy goals of the country  
• 
Promotion of less water-intensive agriculture practices to relieve pressure on water resources 
• 
Greater predictability and security for agriculture planning at household level 
 
Environmental co-benefits 
140. 
EbA measures and sustainable agriculture will promote positive environmental externalities such as soil/water 
quality, conservation and biodiversity. Investments in the water retention capacity of the area will also benefit ecosystem 
services. A study undertaken catchment wide on the upper parts of the Chao Phraya River Basin by Prattomchai (et 
al., 2014) looked at projected groundwater recharge under changing climate scenarios during the period of 2026 – 
2040, based on GCMs (MIROC, ESM, CHEM, HadGEM2, ES HadGEM and GFDK ESM2M) under scenarios RCP2.6, 
RCP4.5, RCP6.0, and RCP8.5. The study found that annual groundwater recharge (storage) is likely to reduce by 
12.9% as a result of the significant decrease in rainfall volumes in the April to June annual periods.51 It is expected that 
the retaining of water through retention options would have a positive influence on groundwater recharge, by allowing 
waters to be retained long enough to permeate to groundwater levels.  
 
Economic and social co-benefits 
141. 
By reducing volatility through support to sustainable agriculture, and expanding access to markets and finance, 
the project supports viability of agriculture livelihoods.  As vulnerable groups are prioritized for support, the project will 
support poverty alleviation, particularly for women as more than half of beneficiary households are female-headed.  
Greater viability in the sector can not only increase agriculture productivity and related income-generating capacity, but 
it can also help address challenges of youth employment and rural-urban migration.  By partnering with Krungsri Bank 
and BAAC, the project will support financial literacy and help provide access to finance to support climate resilient 
agriculture practices, thereby increasing competitiveness and economic growth potential. 
 
142. 
The project will contribute to several SDGs, namely: 1 (no poverty) and 2 (zero hunger) by focusing on 
vulnerable communities with higher incidence of poverty, 6 (clean water and sanitation) by improving water access 
during drought periods, 8 (decent work and economic growth) by reducing volatility of agriculture livelihoods, 15 (life on 
land) given the environmental benefits of EbA and 17 Partnerships for the Goals through a collaborative effort with 
government, UNDP, GCF and finance institutions (i.e. Krungsri and BAAC).  As the number of female headed 
households in agriculture is growing, the project can also contribute to SDG 5 (gender equality).  
 
D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
143. 
Over the last four decades, Thailand has made remarkable progress in social and economic development.  
However, the high exposure to climate change risks and impacts pose a serious threat to the country’s economy and 
could undermine some of the development gains achieved through investment by the RTG, partners, and communities.  
Water management in the context of changing climatic conditions is an adaptation priority. The country has relied 
largely on grey infrastructure, with related costs of necessary upgrades being formidable.  Further, agriculture is a 
critical sector for Thailand, which due to changes in rainfall patterns is being impacted.  IWRM has been stressed as a 
key to sustainable development going forward, and collaboration and joint planning across sectors will be necessary, 
which to date has been limited.   
 
144. 
In terms of vulnerability, rural areas, such as the proposed project area, will face significant climate-induced 
risks due to the relatively higher exposure to climate change impacts, as well as their socio-economic profiles. Poverty 
in Thailand predominately has a rural-profile, which fluctuates according to vulnerabilities in the agricultural sector – 
such as faltering economic growth, falling agricultural prices, and ongoing droughts.52  In 2013, for example, 80 percent 
of the country’s poor 7.3 million live in rural areas, and a further 6.7 million were living within 20 percent above the 
national poverty line.53 48 percent of Thailand’s provinces have a poverty head count of 10 percent or less, and a further 
25 percent of provinces have a poverty headcount rate of between 11 and 20 percent.54  
 
 
51 Masud, M., Soni, P., Shrestha, S. & Tripathi, N. 2016, ‘Changes in Climate Extremes over North Thailand, 1960 – 2099’, Journal of 
Climatology, Research Article, 206:4289454, Pp 1-18 
52 Overview, Thailand: http://www.worldbank.org/en/country/thailand/overview 
53 Overview, Thailand: http://www.worldbank.org/en/country/thailand/overview 
54 National Office of Statistics, 2015

=== PAGE 45 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.0 | PAGE 41 OF 65 
 
 
 
 
145. 
Most of Thailand’s rural society is engaged in agriculture related activities which generate around $40 billion in 
revenue, over 10 percent of national gross domestic product (GDP) 55 , 56 and account for 74 percent of water 
consumption in Thailand.57 The sectors role in job creation in the rural areas is higher than the national average of 
around 50 percent of society being dependent on the agricultural sector for their livelihoods. The total number of 
households engaged in agriculture in Thailand is almost 6 million (5,910,791 households) comprising a total 23,686,102 
(almost 24 million)58 of the country’s 67 million national population.  The agricultural sector in Thailand additionally, 
directly and indirectly, employs roughly around half of the country’s population.59 Overall, Thailand’s agriculture sector 
is dominated by smallholder farmers - most of who own their land outright, although tenancy is widespread in parts of 
the Central Plain. On average nationally, farmers own land sizes of around 20 Rai (equivalent to 3.2 hectares). 
However, in the project area, land ownership sizes tend to be larger, ranging from 25 to 29 Rai (4.00 - 4.64 hectares) 
per household with the area also employing a significant portion of the area’s population in seasonal laboring.  
 
146. 
Almost half of the population of Thailand is engaged in agricultural sector, where incomes are relatively low.  
The project target area was selected based on vulnerability and urgency, given proximity of communities to the river 
basin and reliance on water for agriculture as a livelihood. Proportionally, Sukhothai, Phitsanulok and Uttaradit 
provinces, included as part of the Northern Central region, have relatively high poverty levels. Impacts from climate 
change are more severely felt by those with higher levels of poverty, predominately due to the high level of dependence 
on natural resources, and in general, a limited capacity in terms of access to resources for responding to climate 
vulnerability and extremes.60  Higher levels of poverty for the Northern-Central region in turn, means that this area also 
has an increased level of vulnerability to climate change impacts, with lower levels of access to resources for adaptation 
strategies and in which to build climate resilience.  
 
147. 
The beneficial areas of this project are in the areas of 3 provinces of the Northern region of Thailand: 
Phitsanulok, Sukhothai and Uttaradit, where gross provincial product (GPP) from 2012-2014 of these 3 provinces was 
approximately 15.47% of the Northern region GPP. GPP of these 3 provinces from agriculture sector in 2014 were 
33.7%, 42.0% and 32.4% where the per capita GPP in 2014 were 98,126, 72,548 and 84,003 THB per person, 
respectively.  This compares with Bangkok and the surrounding area at 376,463 THB, Nong Bua Lamphu with 46,804 
as the lowest, and the national average of Thailand at 193,395 THB per capita.

148. 
The feasibility study (Annex 2) and stakeholder engagement summary (Annex 7(a)) includes field surveys and 
interviews with agriculture households in the targeted districts of the proposed GCF project.  Field surveys and 
interviews with local administrators and community leaders of the inundated lands show that floods are not a new 
phenomenon to the households due to the geographical location of their premises at low-lying lands.  However, great 
concern was expressed about increased incidence of drought and water scarcity, as well as potentially increasing 
periods and severity of flooding.  
 
149. 
The project will combine GCF and RTG resources to address the barriers to adaptation investments and 
catalyse community, public, and private sector financing to support the needs of the targeted agriculture-based 
communities and flood and drought prone areas. Capacities will be strengthened across key decision makers and the 
communities to plan for and manage investment in livelihoods and water security in the face of a changing climate.

Project will enhance the institutional framework for innovation, long-term planning for risk-informed water management 
and learning to promote an enabling environment for sustained resilience of the Yom and Nan river basins and its 
residents. 
D.5. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
150. 
The proposed GCF project is fully aligned with national priorities and builds on existing government 
programmes, including Thailand’s 20-year development plan, as well as the Water Management Strategy.  GCF support 
will address the identified additional activities/costs related to integrating climate change risks into government 
programmes, thereby extending the value of these investments.   
 
151. 
Thailand’s Technology Needs Assessment (TNA) report formulated in 2012 identified three highly impacted 
sectors in urgent need of adaptation technologies, namely: 
• 
Agriculture - in need of forecasting and early warning system technologies, crop improvement technologies, 
and precision farming technologies; 
• 
Water Resource Management - in need of networking (via pipes and canals) and management of 
infrastructures (including zoning), seasonal climate prediction, and sensor web using observation and/or 
modeling data; and 
• 
Modeling - in need of an integrated national data center, national data transfer/management process and 
the advanced research, weather research and forecasting (WRF - ARW) model, and an integrated model 
to address the need of agricultural sector and water resource management sector. 
 
152. 
Acknowledging the need to improve sustainable water resources management, the 12th National Economic 
and Social Development Plan (2017-2021) aims to promote economic and social growth that is environmentally-friendly, 
specifically by: 
• 
Reforming water management systems to ensure balance between users’ demand and supply as well as 
provision of access to water; 
• 
Enforcing environment acts and promote collaboration among sectors for integrated work plans and 
budgets; 
• 
Developing water resources information centers; and 
• 
And establishing water management organization at the local level, such as river basin committee and 
water users’ association. 
 
153. 
Efficiency is a priority in Thailand’s adaptation strategy, which is guided by the philosophy of Sufficiency 
Economy – moderation, reasonableness, and the need of self-immunity for sufficient protection from impact arising 
from internal and external changes. 61 Building on from the Sufficiency Economy approach – is the ‘New Theory 
Farming’, which compounds integrated farming systems, and the Buddhist approach of self-restraint combined with 
sustainable agriculture62. The 12th National Economic and Social Development Plan (2017 – 2021) aims to promote 
both ecologically sound economic and social growth. This is centred on water management system reformation to 
ensure user demand and supply balance with the provision of access to water.  
 
154. 
Thailand’s National Climate Change Policy was approved in Cabinet as well as the Strategic Plan on Climate 
Change (January 2008). It incorporates capacity building, research and development, awareness and public 
participation, and international cooperation in mitigation and adaptation. The Climate Change Master Plan (2015 – 
2050) sets out a long-term framework for the management of climate change in Thailand. The Plan focuses on 
adaptation across the water management, flood and drought sector; agriculture and food security; human health; 
tourism; natural resources sector, and; human settlement and security. This project targets two of these sectors, 
namely: (i) water management, flood and drought; and (ii) agriculture and food security.  
 
155. 
Development of Thailand’s Climate Change National Adaptation Plan (NAP) commenced in 2015 and currently 
ongoing towards completion of the NAP. The draft version of the NAP currently contains a focus on agricultural projects, 
of which the Enhancing climate resilience in Thailand through effective water management and sustainable agriculture 
project is complementary to and will strengthen and build upon the capacity building on climate information, and the 
upgraded IRDM tool (under Output 1) elements.  
 
156. 
The Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) for Thailand includes a shortlisting of adaptation key efforts. 
These include the prioritization of efforts which:  
 
61 Thailand’s Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC), 2015 
62 Amekawa, 2010:390

=== PAGE 47 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.0 | PAGE 43 OF 65 
 
 
 
 
• 
Promote and strengthen Integrated Water Resources Management (IWRM) practices into achieving water 
security, and effective water resource management to mitigate flood and drought 
• 
Safeguard food security through the guidance of the Sufficiency Economy Philosophy (e.g. agriculture and 
land management that promotes appropriate resource allocation and economic diversification at a 
household level; the sustainable management of community forests in order to promote community level 
food security) 
• 
Promote both sustainable agriculture and Good Agricultural Practice (GAP) 
• 
Strengthen disaster risk reduction and reduce the population’s vulnerability to climate risk and extreme 
weather events through enhanced awareness, coordination and local community adaptive capacity, 
especially within disaster prone areas, and  
• 
Strengthen climate-modeling capacity while promoting collaboration amongst relevant agency 
 
157. 
The project will also help realize Thailand’s Commitment to Climate Resilient Agriculture and Digital 
Transformation. The project is aligned with the country’s 20 Year Agricultural Development Plan (2017-2036) whose 
objective is to lay a foundation for long-term development leading to systematic growth and solutions to problems of 
the agriculture sector. The specific goals are as follows: (1) Farmers specialize in their professions, so-called “Smart 
Farmer;” (2) Farmer institutions have an efficiency in management, so-called “Smart Agricultural Group;” (3) Quality of 
agricultural products meet the customers need, in other words, being “Smart Agricultural Products;” and (4) Agricultural 
area and sector have potential, known as “Smart Area / Agriculture.” Strategy 3: Increasing Competitiveness in the 
Agricultural Sector through Technology and Innovations, having 3 development guidelines which are: Develop 
technology and innovations to drive “Agriculture 4.0” under the “Thailand 4.0” policy; Manage the agricultural 
information technology for ready access and utilization by farmers and Develop agricultural research works and 
information toward their commercialization.  
 
158. 
Furthermore, the project entails the roll-out of innovative technologies for climate solutions and advances use 
of IoT platforms as part of the country’s “MOAC’s Digital Agriculture Strategic Plan (2017-2021)” (Ministry of Agriculture 
and Cooperatives’ Committee on ICTs,  2017). The plan maps the long-term steps to digitalize agriculture starting from 
preparing the ICT system for being digital agriculture. For the first five years, the strategy envisions to work on the 
development of paradigms and framework for digital agriculture. This strategic plan comprises of three missions, which 
are to develop agricultural information system for proactive management, to apply appropriate digital technology with 
context to agriculture sector, and to support sustainable agriculture.   
 
159. 
Finally, the project was designed with extensive stakeholder consultations. Under the guidance of ONEP (office 
of the NDA) and RID, the project design was further informed by consultations and assessments conducted during the 
project development phase, which included government ministries, development partners, academia, social enterprise 
and communities (see Annex 7(a)). 
 
160. 
The proposed project builds on past community consultations, comprising of several rounds of stakeholder 
discussions on adaptation priorities, including flood and drought risk management through enhanced green and grey 
water infrastructure, and climate-resilient agriculture-based livelihoods. Stakeholder discussions took place over the 
period of 2016-2019, initiated and led by the RID and in coordination with the ONEP (NDA).  
 
161. 
The project, through field surveys and consultations with sub-national level decision makers, communities, and 
community leaders has integrated local level perspectives into the project design (see Annex 7(a)). In addition, 
consultations with technical officials within MoAC with expertise on water management and related modeling, 
economics, agriculture, and hydromet services, as well as academic institutions in order to incorporate best 
international and national practices, and seek wide support for the project interventions.  Given their expertise and 
experience ecosystems-based water management in Thailand, GIZ was consulted to inform the design the EbA 
elements of Output 2. 
 
162. 
The private sector and social business have also been consulted to ensure a pragmatic lens to the design of 
interventions supporting farmers. These include, Siam Organic Co Ltd, which focuses on producing healthy organic 
products, while applying practical business approaches informed by market research, to help small-scale producers in 
Thailand grow their way out of poverty.  Similarly, consultations with Krungsri Bank63 regarding supporting farmers with 
business skills resulted in a partnership for the project through co-financing for Output 3.   
 
163. 
Following a multi-stakeholder approach, the project once under implementation will continue to engage with 
numerous stakeholders, both at a national and local level. These include the implementing partner, and civil society

organizations (CSOs), NGOs, private sector organizations, academia, Office of Agriculture Economics (OAE), and other 
public entities. The roles and responsibilities of the various stakeholders are detailed in the Stakeholder Engagement

164. 
The proposal is aligned with UNDP’s comparative advantage in the areas of capacity building, providing 
technical and policy support, reducing barriers and creating enabling conditions for adaptation planning and 
investments. Specifically, the proposed project will build upon UNDP’s comparative advantage stemming from 
experience in working with governments and communities in Thailand and globally on: i) establishing and strengthening 
institutional, policy and legislative mechanisms; ii) building capacity; iii) undertaking risk assessments; iv) 
mainstreaming climate change adaptation, disaster risk reduction and early warning systems into development 
planning; and v) harnessing best practices and community-based approaches across different thematic areas for 
climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction. This includes experience with initiatives focused on transferring 
knowledge and technology via South-South cooperation. The UNDP Country Office (CO) in Thailand is well placed to 
oversee the implementation of the proposed project. UNDP has worked closely with RID on a number of projects, 
including the ongoing Climate Change Finance project, aimed at strengthening capacities of RID to formulate climate-
informed projects and secure finance for implementation. In addition, UNDP is working with the Office of Agriculture 
Economics (AOE) of MoAC through “Integrating Agriculture in National Adaptation Plans (NAP–Ag)” program. 
 
D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
165. 
An economic analysis of the proposed project was carried out (see Annex 3a, b) in accordance with the 
Guidelines for the Economic Analysis of Projects of United Nations Development Program.64 The economic efficiency 
of the investment was determined by computing the economic net present value (NPV) with an assumed 10% discount 
rate, and the economic internal rate of return (IRR). For consistency purposes, all proposals developed with the support 
of UNDP have opted to use a 10% discount rate, in line with the existing practice of multilateral development banks. 
  
166. 
Economic values (costs and benefits) are all measured in real terms of 2019. Economic costs of the project 
are net of taxes, duties, and price contingencies. Furthermore, the analysis assumes a shadow wage rate of 1.00 for 
unskilled and semi-skilled labour in Thailand. With an official unemployment rate estimated at less than 1.5%, this 
assumption is appropriate. Provided that the economic cost of labour in Thailand may be expected to be lower than the 
market wage rate (financial cost), we expect this assumption leads to significantly over-estimating the economic cost 
of the project, and under-estimating the true net economic value of the project.65  
 
167. 
For purpose of the economic analysis, it is assumed that the capital assets have an average longevity of 20 
years. Since, capital investment is expected to have been completed in 2025, it is assumed that this 20-year time 
horizon covers the period 2026 to 2045. Hence, the entire period of analysis covers the period 2021 to 2045. 
 
168. 
As is common when undertaking the economic analysis of investment projects, numerous assumptions were 
used to delineate the “with project scenario” from the “without project scenario”. These assumptions are presented and 
discussed in detail below. Assumptions were always made so as to under-estimate the true net economic value of the 
proposed investment project.    
 
169. 
The project realizes two important benefits of a different nature. First, climate change (projected changes in 
temperature and precipitation) are expected to have an adverse impact on agricultural productivity in the project area. 
Secondly, extreme weather events (extreme precipitation with associated floods, as well as drought) are expected to 
occur at a greater frequency and greater intensity in forthcoming decades. The proposed investment project is expected 
to alleviate both of these impacts.  
 
170. 
The net present value (NPV) and internal rate of return (IRR) of the proposed investment project are presented 
in the table below. The table shows the NPV to be positive under the assumed range of impacts of climate change on 
agricultural productivity, and an IRR in excess of 20%.
