I.
Overall assessment of the Secretariat
1.
The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following
remarks:
Strengths 
Points of caution 
The project contributes to partial protection of 
downstream areas, including vulnerable farmers 
and downstream urban areas in Bangkok, from 
increasingly frequent and intense flooding via the 
integration of ‘green with grey’ infrastructure 
leads to enhanced flood prevention and more 
resilient ecosystems and livelihoods. 
The Ecosystem Based Adaptation approaches for 
flood prevention require extensive participatory 
planning to be undertaken with communities, 
regional government, and experts. This has been 
integrated into the workplan and identified as 
important implementation stage. 
Thailand is a country regarded as in having 
amongst the most severe risks and vulnerabilities 
from climate change1.  The project addresses 
issues pertinent to the country and regarded as 
high priority within the National Action Plan. The 
project is the first project to the Green Climate 
Fund for Thailand and has high level support from 
the Thai government. This will facilitate potential 
upscale of the models, and development of grey-
green infrastructure models, innovative 
technology and supporting communities in 
adaptive livelihood development (through 
parallel finance credit line). 
There should be a plan for further upscale across 
Thailand’s watersheds and downstream areas 
developed during the project utilising this strong 
national support. 
The project includes significant financial 
contribution from the Royal Thai Government and 
Krungsri Bank and offers potential of a credit line 
from parallel finance for farmers from the Bank of 
Agriculture and Agricultural Cooperatives (BAAC) 
which has potential for transformative upscale of 
climate smart agriculture. 
The co-finance from financial institution needs to 
support smallholder farmers and small enterprise 
to upscale the adoption of climate smart 
agriculture. An implementation plan to upscale 
activities should be developed during 
implementation. 
1 Eckstein et al (2021). Global Climate Risk Index. Germanwatch.

=== PAGE 75 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 75 
 
 
Strengths 
Points of caution 
The project demonstrates an innovative pilot to 
implement best practice in water use efficiency 
(e.g., Alternate Wet Dry / Systems Rice 
Intensification) and innovative technology in 
precision agriculture linked to sensors reporting 
on soil moisture levels to provide information to 
supporting effective water level change through 
efficient management of the irrigation system as 
has been demonstrated by International Rice 
Research Institute. This cultivation technique can 
reduce GHG emissions by up to 50% and water 
use by 30%; digital technology assists in its 
effective application at site level. This is highly 
innovative and is in line with Thailand’s strategic 
policies to support agriculture, digital 
transformation, and climate change; in line with 
Thailand’s Digital Agriculture Strategy and 4.0 
New Economic Development Plan which aims to 
harness digital technologies to make Thailand 
more competitive, manage resources efficient, 
improve livelihoods and adaptative to climate 
change. Thailand is well-placed to support 
development of digital innovation into the 
agricultural sector. 
There is need to ensure strong community 
involvement throughout project implementation, 
so that farmer behavior that is not climate 
resilient (e.g., unsuitable crop choice or farming 
calendar) is discontinued. The improved rice 
cultivation methodologies require efficient 
management of irrigation system to utilize water 
effectively and requires cooperation between 
farmers utilizing the same irrigation source. 
Developing, testing and scaling-up innovative 
technologies should be a priority for the Green 
Climate Fund. They should not be evaluated under 
the same criteria for ‘cost-effectiveness’ as well-
tested business-as-usual applications since the 
future values of the technology is not yet 
incorporated into the model.   GCF can help 
support and facilitate the innovation transition 
from innovation to adoption. 
The project demonstrates best practice in water 
use efficiency (e.g. Systems Rice Intensification) 
and innovative technology in precision 
agriculture linked to sensors reporting on soil 
moisture levels to provide information to 
supporting effective water level change through 
efficient management of the irrigation system.  
This has potential to be upscaled and contribute a 
paradigm shift for reducing water use, 
greenhouse gas emissions. 
 
2. 
The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and 
conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum XVIII, titled “List of proposed conditions and 
recommendations,” respectively.  
II. 
Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment 
2.1 
Project background  
3. 
Agriculture provides one of the most important sources of income and employment for 
Thailand; Thailand is the 6th largest producer of rice and 2nd for global exports. Climate change-
induced shifts in precipitation and temperature patterns have enhanced flood and drought risk, 
increasing the intensity and frequency of both. Increased flood risk translates into accelerated 
soil erosion and impeded drainage, affecting agricultural productivity. This negatively impacts 
smallholder farmers who depend on agriculture for their livelihoods. The climate analysis 
clearly shows the following:

=== PAGE 76 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 76 
 
 
(a) 
Increased drought risk – The frequency of extreme SPEI has increased, supporting an 
increase in drought.  
(b) 
Increased risk of extreme rainfall – Historical station data show spatially consistent 
positive trends in annual rainfall and measures of daily rainfall intensity. Ensemble 
projections show clearly future projected increases of about 10-15% in annual rainfall. 
(c) 
Increased discharge – Significant increases (more than 50%) in discharge from an 
ensemble of RCMs and hydrological model are consistent with the +10% in annual 
precipitation since the catchments amplify the rainfall signal.  
4. 
The Chao Phraya River Basin Area is one of two principal river systems in Thailand. It 
consists of eight sub river basins: Yom, Nan, Ping, Wang, Chao Phraya, Sakae Krang, Pa Sak and 
Tha Chin. Working towards river basin-based integrated water management, the project 
integrates ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) ‘green’ measures with ‘traditional’ grey 
infrastructure for flood control and irrigation, supported by enhanced technology and climate-
informed planning and capacity development. The objective of the project is to improve water 
management, food security, and agricultural livelihoods of inhabitants in the Yom and Nam river 
basins to respond to the increased occurrence of climate hazards in the form of floods and 
droughts. Activities at the household scale are intended to directly benefit 62,000 people in the 
Phitsanulok, Sukhothai, and Uttaradit provinces (project sites) found in the Yom and Nan river 
basins, and at the basin scale to indirectly benefit 25,000,000 inhabitants of the Greater Chao 
Phraya River Basin, who live within or downstream of the project area. The countries national 
adaptation plan was developed by its countries leading scientists and climate change experts. 
The Thailand National Adaptation Plan recognises, and prioritizes, the importance of climate 
change adaptation interventions within the Chao Praya River Basin for downstream 
communities. 
5. 
The project seeks a GCF grant of USD 17.534 million, or 51.7 per cent of the total project 
cost (USD 33.911 million). The project is classified as environmental and social safeguards (ESS) 
category B. The Royal Irrigation Department of Thailand (RID) will co-finance the project at USD 
16.264 million during the years of implementation, and Bank of Ayudhya (Krungsri) will co-
finance the project at USD 0.114 million for supportive activities such as financial literacy 
training for farmers. RID has also allocated USD 17.26 million for post-project operations and 
maintenance.  
6. 
Significant parallel financing is provided by the Bank for Agriculture and Agricultural 
Cooperatives (BAAC) via their financial products aimed at climate resilient agricultural 
investments. This is pledged as a credit line of USD 16 million to farmers, cooperatives, social 
enterprises, and other small to medium-sized businesses nationwide.  
2.2 
Component-by-component analysis 
Output 1:  Enhance climate and risk informed planning in the water and agricultural sectors 
through improved climate information and cross sectoral coordination (total cost: USD 3.215 
million; GCF cost: USD 1.683 million) 
7. 
GCF resources will support the improvement of climate risk-responsive decision-making 
in Thailand by enhancing quality and availability of tailored climate information for both water 
management and agricultural planning. This will be achieved through improving the accuracy 
and precision of climate projections and the dissemination of tailored climate information 
products to relevant stakeholders, to support the integration into policies and planning across 
sectors, as well as comparison of water availability and demand, in order to make the needed 
adjustments.

=== PAGE 77 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 77 
 
 
8. 
This output will also enhance inter-ministerial coordination in water management, 
enabling stronger collaboration between key decision-making bodies in Thailand’s water and 
agriculture sectors to incorporate climate risk management and adaptation strategies into their 
sectoral planning processes. Importantly, tailored climate information will be generated and 
disseminated to agriculture households for application in household-level planning.  
Output 2: Improve water management through strengthened infrastructure complemented by EbA 
measures, for greater resilience to climate change impacts (total cost: USD 23.125 million; GCF 
cost: USD 9.640 million) 
9. 
This output focuses on complementing RID investments to improve water management 
in the Yom and Nan river basins area with ecosystem-based adaptation measures. GCF’s 
contribution will fund the review of designs for water infrastructure upgrades at the thirteen 
irrigation schemes in the Yom-Nan basin, to ensure that they are adequate given future climate 
projections.  Given the high upfront costs of the basin-wide investment required to adapt to 
climate change, this will support long term sustainability of government investment. GCF will 
also complement government co-financing and planned long-term investment by RID in the 
entire area of the Yom and Nan river basins by supporting the upgrade of infrastructure at two 
of the schemes. 
10. 
Water management in Thailand is largely dependent on grey infrastructure, although 
there are examples where use of ecosystem-based measures has been established. GCF 
resources will support RID in identifying and implementing ecosystem-based approaches to 
complement grey infrastructure. While ecosystem-based measures alone cannot address all 
climate risk challenges, complementing grey infrastructure with ecosystem-based solutions can 
be a cost-efficient solution while also generating environmental co-benefits.  This integration of 
EbA approaches has the potential to be transformational to Thailand’s traditional planning 
concepts for water management that focus widely on hard structure interventions, through 
upscaling across the thirteen schemes in the Yom-Nan River Basin area and the greater Chao 
Phraya River Basin. The project has significant potential for upscale within the basin and to 
other basins in Thailand, the elevated level of support from the Thai government will facilitate 
this upscale. 
Output 3: Reduce volatility of agriculture livelihoods in drought and flood-prone areas through 
strengthened extension support and local planning, investment in on-farm adaptation measures 
and greater access to finance and markets (total cost: USD 5.978 million; GCF cost: USD 5.617 
million) 
11. 
This output will involve providing training to facilitate climate-informed decision-
making related to agriculture practices, particularly implementation of on-farm interventions 
for farmers in the immediate project area. Training materials developed through this project 
will thereafter be applied throughout the agriculture sector in Thailand through farmer field 
training (FFT). The project area is dominated by agriculture activities, which are particularly 
vulnerable to climatic conditions. It is critical therefore that interventions in the project area 
engage farmers effectively.  
12. 
This output will be implemented in partnership and coordination with King Mongkut's 
University of Technology North Bangkok (KMUTNB) and Krungsri Bank. KMUTNB will help to 
deliver training to farmers on applying water forecast based on climate information to 
household level agriculture practices, and Krungsri Bank, through co-financing activities, will 
support farmers in developing their business skills for greater market access. Trainings under 
this output will be coordinated for cost-saving purposes, but importantly also to ensure that 
extension officers and farmers benefit from the collaborative approach of the project – bringing 
together expertise from the water and agriculture departments, academia, and the financial 
sector, as well as the farmers themselves.  
Project management costs, total: USD 1.593 million; GCF: USD 0.593 million.

=== PAGE 78 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 78 
 
 
III. 
Assessment of performance against investment criteria  
3.1 
Impact potential 
       Scale: N/A 
13. 
Thailand is identified as one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change risks 
and impacts due to geographical and topographical characteristics. Climate induced extreme 
weather events leading to increased flood and drought risk are reported as becoming more 
frequent and intense.  Climate analysis shows significant increase in temperature, significant 
increase in precipitation and significant increase in water discharge. 
14. 
This project directly targets existing water infrastructure (output 2) and the related 
governing institutions which need improvement, and communities which need further support 
(output 1 and 3). Stemming from a careful consideration of other subriver basins, the selection 
of the Yom and Nan river basins for infrastructure update is of particularly strategic importance 
as they retain the excess water flowing through Thailand’s central plain, Bangkok, and Gulf of 
Thailand.  
15. 
Given local social and economic conditions, it is appropriate that farming households are 
the focus of the project.  The climate resilience of 62,000 project participants (direct 
beneficiaries) in the Yom Nan River Basin and 25,000,000 (indirect beneficiaries) in the Greater 
Chao Phraya River system are expected to be improved by the interventions, as described in 
Section 2. The project selects agricultural communities in Phitsanulok, Sukhothai, and Uttaradit 
provinces (project sites) that depend on rice production as their main source of income. One of 
the main interventions of the project is the focus on capacity development. Increased 
community adaptive capacity is needed to implement more climate resilient practices for 
sustainable livelihoods (outputs 1 and 3). 
16. 
In summary, complementing ongoing work initiated by the Royal Thai Government, the 
project for climate-resilient water infrastructure is expected to result in enhanced livelihoods 
for Thailand’s most vulnerable people. Enhanced climate and risk decision-making and support 
services are not only provided to households, but also include ministries involved in water 
management and agricultural planning, and organizations across the relevant value chains.  The 
focus on agricultural livelihoods and water management is justified given Thailand’s 
development needs and goals. 
3.2 
Paradigm shift potential 
Scale: N/A 
17. 
The project aims to contribute to the shift from ‘traditional’ water management systems 
to an integrated system with the eventual careful inclusion of EbA. The inclusion of EbA with 
grey infrastructure has been shown to be less costly and promotes resilience of ecosystems and 
livelihoods by conserving and protecting existing ecosystem services (e.g., flood regulation, soil 
retention). The project is part of an overall strategy of the Royal Thai Government to support 
canal and embankment improvements of the Greater Chao Phraya River system coupled with 
improved extension services (i.e., soil rehabilitation, climate vulnerability assessments), 
focusing on low-lying areas.  
18. 
The major constraint facing farming communities lies in 1) inefficient water 
management; and 2) barriers to market access, both of which are tackled in this proposal. 
Improved information and capacity development will lead to better decision-making, especially 
as the project also seeks to improve management practices and market linkages. There is 
potential for a more profitable and climate resilient agricultural sector for project participants. 
This is underpinned by a series of proposed interventions seeking to build a more robust and 
conducive policy and institutional environment via knowledge building and exchange with 
responsible ministries. Innovation is also present, with the demonstration of a precision

=== PAGE 79 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 79 
 
 
agriculture model, which optimizes irrigation efficiency.  This is coupled with the enhanced 
intelligence-based real-time decision-making (IRDM) tool, which provides more downscaled 
rainfall and run-off information for better localised advice, will improve decision-making, and 
improve water management.  Thailand has developed a positive enabling environment for the 
integration of technology into smart agriculture. The IoT tool for water management has 
already been tested in Thailand by Kasetsart University and in Philippines by the International 
Rice Research Institute (https://waterice.irri.org/how-we-work). The project is well placed to 
further develop this technology with staunch support from Thai agencies and supported by 
Thailand’s national strategies which place an emphasis on integrating smart agriculture and 
digital technologies to transform the agricultural sector. Testing and future development of this 
highly innovative system to improve community managed rice lands, save water resources and 
reduce greenhouse gas emissions is in line with the Green Climate Fund’s strategic aims to 
integrate and develop innovative approaches to combat climate change. The GCF Secretariat 
view that as this is testing an innovative system, it should not be evaluated primarily based on 
cost-effectiveness criteria; since many emerging technologies and applications will encounter 
hurdles to move from the innovation to adoption phases. 
19. 
With respect to post-project sustainability, the project appears well-designed and 
phased to provide support to the eventual planned replication to other subriver basins and 
farming communities, though the incremental plan for eventual upscale across all river-basins 
by the Thai government is not yet clear, though it is planned in Thai national policies. Building 
on established in-country networks and refining government responsibilities, economic 
incentives for communities for more climate resilient agricultural practices are expected to be 
created, supported by government actors. For example, outputs of adaptation plans (with legal 
standing) and climate responsive cropping patterns at the household level are expected with 
on-hand implementation of on-farm measures for improved water storage and soil 
conservation, coupled with bankable agro-investments.  
3.3 
Sustainable development potential 
Scale: N/A 
20. 
The proposal contributes to Sustainable Development Goals: (1) No Poverty, (2) Zero 
Hunger, (5) Gender Equality, (6) Clean Water and Sanitation, (13) Climate Action, (15) Life On 
Land,  (17) Partnerships for the Goals. Positive environmental externalities are expected in 
improved water management leading to 1) efficient water use; and 2) controlled soil 
conservation mainly in the river and floodplain areas.  There is evidence that the project would 
enhance groundwater recharge, leading to further water availability. 
21. 
With Outputs 1 and 3, the linkages between government actors, local farming 
communities in the project sites, academic institutions (King Mongkut's University of 
Technology North Bangkok), and local financial institutions (co-financiers, cooperatives) are 
expected to increase mainly across the rice value chain. Capacity development and facilitated 
planning and coordination underpins these improved relationships. Coupled with the more 
efficient water resource use and enhanced market access, the enhanced linkages are intended to 
translate into financial benefits especially for smallholder farming communities, where female-
led households are prioritised. Overall, significant effort is made to contribute to the project 
sites’ overall self-sufficiency in health and food security. While the focus of this proposal is on 
water management for agriculture, the project may be strengthened with further discussion on 
the impacts on surrounding natural ecosystems.  
3.4 
Needs of the recipient 
Scale: N/A 
22. 
As previously noted, Thailand is facing abundant climate risks: extreme rain episodes 
lead to increased flood risk and drier dry seasons lead to water unavailability. Overall, the

=== PAGE 80 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 80 
 
 
selected project sites, Sukhothai, Phitsanulok and Uttaradit Provinces, are composed of 
impoverished rural communities that are geographically at risk, as they live on flood plains, and 
are financially underserved. With their livelihoods tied to the climate sensitive industry of 
agriculture, the targeted beneficiaries and their productive assets are assessed to be greatly 
affected by climate induced extreme weather events and will be disproportionately impacted by 
their effects.  
23. 
The Provinces are part of the Yom and Nan subriver basins which form part of the 
Greater Chao Phraya River Basin area. This area is of critical importance for food production 
and flood regulation. The Yom and Nan subriver basins are in the middle section. Water is 
stored here to prevent flooding to Thailand’s capital and economic centre, Bangkok. 
24. 
National and local resources are leveraged by GCF funding. Green Climate Fund and RID 
funding is focused on enhancing infrastructure (Output 2), while GCF also funds activities 
supporting an enabling environment, namely capacity developing, planning, knowledge 
management, and coordination (Outputs 1 and 3). Other co-financiers (Krungsri Bank and 
BAAC) are focused on supporting the project gains during and after implementation, through 
supporting market access and providing further opportunities for investment. 
25. 
Institutions benefiting from the interventions were chosen based on their sectoral 
contribution and decision-making power to implement the project’s integrated water resource 
management approach. This approach targets specific institutional needs, such as the 
refinement of intelligence-based real-time decision-making modelling (IRDM), which involves 
RID, the Agricultural Research Development Agency, the National Research Council of Thailand, 
KMUTNB, and the Thailand Research Organization Network. Institutional coordination is also 
targeted, including the Thai Meteorological Department, the Hydro and Agro Informatics 
Institute, the Geo-Informatics and Space Technology Development Agency, the Department of 
Water Resources, and the Hydrographic Department of the Royal Thai Navy.  
3.5 
Country ownership 
Scale: N/A 
26. 
The proposal has strategic alignment with the Royal Thai Government’s highest-level 
development and climate policy and master plan, namely the 20-year national strategic plan 
(2018–2037), National Economic and Social Development Plan (2017-2021), and the Climate 
Change Master Plan. It addresses the top two priorities detailed in the GCF country programme: 
water resources management and food security. Related activities have been adequately 
coordinated with ongoing and planned similar projects. The AE has consulted numerous 
stakeholders including the project participants, financial institutions, and national and local 
government agencies. Reported results include the need for a strong flood control system in the 
area with adjustments to be made in controlled water levels, intake and outlet systems, 
drainage system, road system, and monitoring system.  
27. 
UNDP has a strong track record of development projects in Thailand especially focusing 
on agriculture and water management. It has a country office and will continue to work with key 
agencies and organizations. The executing entity (EE) is the Kingdom of Thailand, represented 
by the Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives (MOAC). The project is implemented by RID 
who will held accountable by UNDP for the use of GCF proceeds. RID and other partners such as 
GIZ and KMUTNB have undergone the required assessments under the Harmonised Approach 
to Cash Transfers Framework. This Framework represents a common operational framework 
for UN agencies’ transfer of cash to responsible parties.  
28. 
RID is mandated to maintain water infrastructure and is uniquely posed to cater to both 
hard and soft aspects of the interventions with other partners. RID is also a co-financier and 
contributes to both the project implementation phase and post-project operations and 
maintenance. This co-investment is a clear indication of government commitment.

=== PAGE 81 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 81 
 
 
3.6 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
Scale: N/A 
29. 
The level of concessionality is assessed to be justified by the expected benefits from the 
proposed interventions. This is coupled with the limited financial return of adaptation activities 
for the benefit of vulnerable rural farming communities in northern Thailand. 
30. 
Output 3 is expected to spearhead the creation of further economic opportunities for the 
project participants in selected agriculture value chains as enabled by Outputs 1 and 2, which 
work in the enabling environment and physical protection, respectively, all of which can be 
considered as public goods with low financial returns but with high economic value. Private 
sector parallel financing is present and amount to a significant percentage of co-financing at 93 
per cent of the total project cost. This will stimulate potential revenue-generating activities and 
will thus be financed by non-GCF proceeds. 
31. 
In terms of enhanced resilience, 62,000 direct beneficiaries are estimated. The project 
utilizes GCF investment of USD 282.80 per direct beneficiary, and USD 56.20 per total direct and 
indirect beneficiaries.  
32. 
Thailand has developed a positive enabling environment for the integration of 
technology into smart agriculture. The IoT tool for water management has already been tested 
in Thailand by Kasetsart University and in Philippines by the International Rice Research 
Institute (https://waterice.irri.org/how-we-work ). The project is well placed to further develop 
this technology with staunch support from Thai agencies and supported by Thailand’s national 
strategies which place an emphasis on integrating smart agriculture and digital technologies to 
transform the agricultural sector. The GCF Secretariat view that as this is testing an innovative 
system, it should not be evaluated primarily based on cost-effectiveness criteria. It should be 
noted that emerging technologies and applications will encounter hurdles to move from the 
innovation to adoption phases and cannot yet be evaluated on traditional cost-effectiveness 
since the values, and future impacts, of the technologies are at innovation stage. This is in line 
with theoretical concepts of innovation development as exemplified by historical examples, 
from mobile phones to wireless sensors and other emerging technologies. Digital technologies, 
smart agriculture and building capacity for ‘smart farmers will transform Thailand’s approach 
to tackle climate change and create resilient livelihoods in the agriculture sector in the next 
decade. 
IV. 
Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies 
4.1 
Environmental and social safeguards 
33. 
Project background. The project aims to provide interventions to adapt water 
management and agricultural livelihoods in the Yom and Nan river basins in Thailand to 
extreme weather events such as droughts and floods, by enhancing climate and risk planning in 
the water and agricultural sectors, improve water management through strengthened 
infrastructure by integrating EbA measures, and reducing the volatility of livelihoods in these 
areas through various technical and financial support. Among the expected environmental co-
benefits of the interventions are improved soil and water quality including improved 
groundwater recharge, biodiversity conservation and enhanced ecosystem services. The social 
co-benefits include improved access to water supply during droughts, particularly for 
vulnerable groups such as female-headed households. 
34. 
Environmental and social risk category and safeguard instruments. The project is 
classified as category B since the project will have potential limited adverse environmental and 
social risks and/or impacts which are site-specific, largely reversible, and can be mitigated with 
appropriate environmental and social management measures. The project has been screened

=== PAGE 82 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 82 
 
 
against the AE’s Social and Environmental Standards Procedure (SESP) and an Environmental 
and Social Management Framework (ESMF) has been prepared including an Environmental and 
Social Management Plan (ESMP). The Secretariat confirms that the risk category is aligned with 
GCF’s environmental and social risk categorization and is within the environmental and social 
risk accreditation level of the AE. 
35. 
Compliance with GCF’s environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards. 
Subparagraphs (a)–(h) below summarize the programme’s compliance with GCF ESS standards 
and requirements: 
(a) 
ESS 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and 
impacts. The project has submitted an ESMF with sections containing the 
environmental and social risk assessment and management measures as provided in the 
ESMP. The impact risk assessment was undertaken using the AE’s SESP to assess the 
probability (expected, highly likely, moderately likely, or not likely) and the severity of 
impacts (critical, severe, moderate, minor, or negligible). The results of environmental 
and social risk assessment are presented and an ESMP is proposed that identifies, 
among other things, potential impact areas, required mitigation measures, and 
responsibilities of stakeholders. The potential negative impacts are on water quality 
through sediment movement during the replacement of existing infrastructure, channel 
and retention basin works; and EbA activities for wetland restoration. Noise and air 
quality may also be impacted during these works. Minor impacts also include increased 
waste from the removal of existing structures; 
(b) 
ESS 2: Labour and working conditions. The project underscores that activities will 
respond to the core labour standards per the International Labour Organization and the 
labour laws and occupational health and safety regulations of Thailand. This includes a 
number of performance standards in relation to labour and working conditions such as 
on freedom of association, elimination of discrimination in employment and occupation, 
elimination of forced or compulsory labour, and ensuring no forms of child labour. To 
limit risks and keep working conditions safe, generic contract clauses are provided as 
part of tender procedures that contractors need to comply with. These aspects will be 
monitored, and all workers will be informed and trained on the existence of a grievance 
mechanism in a continuous and systematic manner; 
(c) 
ESS 3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention. The AE has identified and 
assessed activities that generate emissions to air, noise and vibration, discharges to 
water, contamination of land and other project-associated wastes. The AE incorporated 
measures in the ESMP to avoid and/or reduce pollution and promote sustainable use of 
resources. The proposed project is expected to generate sediments during construction 
works that might lead to water and land pollution. However, the ESMP presents 
measures to manage, limit and avoid pollution including an approach to monitor and 
report environmental risks on a daily and weekly basis. In addition, an erosion, drainage 
and sediment control management plan and contaminated soil disposal management 
guidance are provided; 
(d) 
ESS 4: Community health, safety and security. The project recognizes the fact that 
issues related to site security might emerge, thus security of sites is part of the 
requirements in the works contract and should remain in force throughout the contract 
period. This also includes the management of on-site construction materials, 
construction and sanitary waste, additional strengthening of erosion control and soil 
stabilization systems and other conditions resulting from contractor activities which 
may increase the potential for damage. As regards noise, reasonable efforts to schedule 
activities during normal working hours will be made to minimize nuisance to the 
surrounding communities. This would also mean developing a public notification and 
noise management plan, as necessary. The project, through its contractors, will also

=== PAGE 83 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 83 
 
 
ensure that a traffic management plan for anticipated service disruptions will be 
prepared should transport and mobility of the communities be hampered by the 
project’s activities; 
(e) 
ESS 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement. The implementation of the 
project will not require any resettlement. None of the interventions will require the 
displacement of people or involve economic displacement. There may be instances 
where there may be a need to utilize areas of land adjacent to where the structural 
interventions will be undertaken such as for water infrastructure works. However, 
where access is required, the land will be returned in the same condition as it was prior 
to any use. The ESMP further highlights that access to this type of land will only be 
obtained through voluntary agreements with landholders. Where a voluntary agreement 
cannot be concluded, the land will not be used. The project will likewise ensure full 
compliance with the AE’s social and environmental standards guidance note on 
displacement and resettlement; 
(f) 
ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural 
resources. The project area and its surroundings are rich in flora and fauna, including 
aquatic and marine species. Two protected areas are located in proximity to the project 
area, but no activities will be undertaken in these areas. The ESMP has a number of 
performance criteria that will enable project activities to limit impacts on terrestrial, 
aquatic and marine flora and fauna. The management measures that will be 
implemented during construction activities include, among others, compliance with no 
clearance of vegetation outside of the designated clearing boundaries and no 
introduction of new weed species during construction works or implementing EbA 
activities. A flora and fauna monitoring programme will also be implemented;  
(g) 
GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7: Indigenous peoples. The ESMF notes that 
ethnic minorities make up 15 per cent of the total population of Thailand and the AE has 
undertaken screening to identify the presence of ethnic minorities in the general project 
area. It notes that there are no indigenous peoples and/or ethnic minorities among the 
smallholder farming communities within the project area but that there are Thai Khrang 
villages in the highland area away from the project sites. The AE notes the intention to 
include them in the project activities. Efforts to engage these groups will include 
extending knowledge on EbA and smart farming and involvement in project design and 
implementation, particularly with respect to EbA activities; and 
(h) 
ESS 8: Cultural heritage. The ESMF identifies three sites within the overall project 
footprint including a popular touristic cultural heritage site visited by tourists. Despite 
the presence of these sites, the AE expects that activities will be implemented with 
limited or no risks, following the performance criteria indicated in the ESMP. 
Nonetheless, should there be any unexpected cultural heritage items discovered during 
project works, the AE or respective EE will immediately cease work within the area and 
will consult with the relevant authorities to arrive at an appropriate decision. 
Nonetheless, should there be any unexpected cultural heritage items discovered during 
project works, a chance finds procedure will be followed to ensure that the project 
works are immediately ceased within the area and consultations are held with the 
relevant authorities to arrive at an appropriate decision. 
36. 
  Implementation arrangements. The implementing agencies will be responsible for 
the implementation and compliance with the ESMP, in collaboration with the various partners 
and contractors. The ESMP will also be part of the tender documents. The ESMP has clearly 
defined the roles and responsibilities of the stakeholders that will be involved during 
implementation. The monitoring and evaluation oversight and responsibilities are also defined.  
The ESMP has recognized the need to carry out capacity-building such as for project personnel

=== PAGE 84 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 84 
 
 
to attend induction training that covers health, safety, environmental and cultural requirements, 
and for workers engaged in any activity with the potential to cause serious environmental harm. 
37. 
Stakeholder engagement and grievance redress mechanism (GRM). The project has 
conducted field surveys and interviews with beneficiary households in the targeted districts. It 
also included interviews with local administrators and community leaders in the areas. These 
consultations, including with decision-makers, communities and community leaders have 
integrated their concerns into the project design. The project has prepared a stakeholder 
engagement plan that provides an indicative list of stakeholders for the outputs and activities 
for the lifetime of this project. The project will continue to engage with stakeholders, including 
those from civil society organizations, non-governmental organizations, private sector 
organizations, academia and public organizations. The stakeholder engagement plan is a living 
document and will be revised and updated if needs arise during project inception and 
implementation.  
38. 
The project has provided a GRM for complaints on environmental and social issues. A 
safeguards officer will be stationed at the project management unit and will be designated as 
the officer in charge of the GRM at the project level. A complaints register will also be 
maintained to keep track of grievances and monitor how they are addressed. Notwithstanding 
this, an independent two-tier GRM structure has been developed to address all complaints 
and/or grievances in the project both at the activity level and at the AE level. 
4.2 
Gender policy  
39. 
The accredited entity (AE) has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan 
and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.  
40. 
The AE has provided a gender assessment based on a desk review and stakeholder 
consultation with communities including women. The assessment indicates that Thailand has an 
enabling environment based on the constitution and civil and commercial code that accords 
women and men the same rights to own, use, and make decisions regarding land and non-land 
assets. It also has laws allowing for joint land entitlement and co-ownership of property for 
married and unmarried couples. Thailand has also ratified the Convention on the Elimination of 
All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW). It has a Department of Women’s Affairs 
and Family Development within the Ministry of Social Development and Human Security to deal 
with gender and women’s empowerment and social inclusion issues. While favourable policies 
and laws exist, customary laws still influence decision-making in rural areas and have impacts 
that may disadvantage women’s access to land, property, and inheritance. There is also a lack of 
clear policy directives on the integration of gender equality and limited participation of women 
in the formulation and implementation of climate policies and plans. This is further 
compounded by the lack of sex disaggregated data, which represents another key challenge in 
gender and social inclusion in climate change action. 
41. 
The gender assessment attests that women play a significant role in agricultural 
activities in the same way that men do despite having less access to mechanized technology, 
agricultural inputs, finance and credit, seeds, information, and knowledge. At the same time, 40 
per cent of the participants in the focus group discussions held during the assessment, indicated 
that, in practice, communities have access to, and decision-making power over land but are 
unsure of their formal ownership status. Further, 30 per cent of the Focus Group Discussion 
participants mentioned that new property (land as well as house) is commonly registered under 
the wife’s name because men tend to avoid paperwork and prefer to work in the field. 
Ownership of land by women seems to have increased – albeit slightly – in the northern region 
of Thailand, where women made up 28.7 per cent of the landholders in 2013, compared to 20.7 
per cent in 2003. However, a similar statistic for women landholders is not available for the 
project provinces.

=== PAGE 85 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 85 
 
 
42. 
Apart from their role in agriculture, women are also tasked almost exclusively with the 
additional roles of cleaning, cooking, childcare, care (average 5.4 hours a day on domestic tasks 
and childcare, compared to 2.9 hours for men) and home gardens for household consumption. 
Water for home consumption is also the responsibility of women, adding to their burden in 
periods of water stress. Young women and children are also engaged in the latter providing 
support to their mothers by fetching water and facing the same challenges. While the 
responsibility for household consumption lies with women, the management of water usage 
groups is usually led by men with women having the responsibilities of vice president, 
financing, and accounting as well as administrative positions. Social norms, inequitable power 
dynamics, and the gendered division of labour affect women’s and men’s access to resources in 
northern Thailand, including water. Men typically have greater rights of access to, and decision-
making power over water. Women in rural areas, particularly poor and ethnic minority women, 
may face disproportionate water insecurity compared to rural men. Climate change increases 
water scarcity and water-related insecurities, even in managing water user groups, particularly 
for women who rely on natural resources for their livelihood.  
43. 
The assessment indicates that five core areas of focus could address the challenges to 
vulnerable women and benefit communities through the project. These are: access to 
information about climate change and related impacts on water and agriculture; defining the 
localized and differentiated information needed and to be disseminated; engagement in 
decision-making related to water use/management and agriculture; access to technology and 
related training to advance climate resilient agriculture; and access to finance to invest in 
climate resilient agricultural practices.  
44. 
The gender action plan provided by the AE complies with the requirement of the GCF 
Gender Policy. It has provided specific activities, targets (50 per cent women participation and 
further disaggregated by age), timelines, financial and human resources by way of gender 
expertise for the project to address the challenges raised. It will employ a pilot approach such as 
gender-responsive Participatory Integrated Climate Services for Agriculture and piloting and 
scaling up household methodologies expected to support the work on addressing and 
strengthening gender equitable intra-household work and decision-making. The action plan will 
also formulate a working group on gender with various actors to ensure that issues raised are 
addressed and solutions implemented. The project will also focus on building data collection 
and management while at the same time documenting gender disaggregated data in a planned 
vulnerability and risk assessment. It will also target women and female entrepreneurs in 
trainings that will build their skills and provide them with financial incentives and support to 
access climate resilient agricultural practices and climate change information. Overall, to 
address some of the underlying causes of inequality, the project will facilitate the creation of an 
enabling environment by working with men to alter harmful stereotypes while also designing 
strategies to monitor and mitigate the potential issue of gender-based violence.  
4.3 
Risks  
4.3.1. 
Overall project assessment (medium risk) 
45. 
GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 17.5 million to adapt water management and 
agriculture livelihoods in the Yom and Nan river basins to climate change-induced extreme 
weather events in Thailand. The Royal Thai Government through its Irrigation Department and 
Krungsri Bank are providing in-kind contributions worth USD 16.2 million and USD 0.1 million, 
respectively. There is no co-financing from the AE.  
46. 
Thailand has investment grade credit rating, and its fiscal situation may support availing 
concessional debt from GCF. The AE has supported grant financing from GCF based on public 
good nature of the project anDd with 48 per cent of the budget coming from co-financing and

=== PAGE 86 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 86 
 
 
opined that the GCF grant is needed to cover the incremental cost to make the total project 
viable. 
4.3.2. 
Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current project (low 
risk) 
47. 
UNDP is the AE for this project. The AE has been operating in Thailand since 1966 and 
has an ongoing portfolio in the country with an annual budget range of USD 7–10 million over 
the past five years. The AE has its regional office representation in Thailand.  
48. 
The Kingdom of Thailand, represented by the Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives, 
(MOAC) is the EE for this project. The AE carried out the capacity assessment of MOAC, which 
resulted in low risk in all major categories. 
4.3.3. 
Project-specific execution risks (medium risk) 
49. 
Construction risks: the project implementation involves canal dredging, and 
construction related to the floodgate. A total of 54 per cent of GCF financing (USD 9.6 million) 
and 82 per cent of government co-financing (USD 13.4 million) is budgeted for Component 2. 
Under this component, GCF resources will cover the construction cost of upgrading two 
floodgates for flood mitigation and improvement of water storage for agriculture. The 
government co-financing will support canal dredging and strengthening of embankments to 
improve waterway connections. The AE has identified that the construction and on-groundwork 
may be affected by extreme weather conditions. Therefore, the implementation plan needs to be 
adaptable to such conditions.  
50. 
Operation and maintenance activities: GCF resources will complement the planned grey 
infrastructure with ecosystem-based solutions. Therefore, identifying the most appropriate2 
EbA measures for the specific project target area and responsibility for operation and 
maintenance are crucial for the infrastructure (e.g., floodgates) to serve the intended purpose. 
The design of EBA methods requires extensive participatory discussions with local 
stakeholders, including farmers, and has been integrated into the implementation plan. The 
project team plans to mobilize resources to cover the cost from different departments in the 
Government. As multiple departments are involved in the financing, the coordination and 
mobilization efforts will be important to sustainably finance the operation and maintenance 
costs. The fact that the Government will allocate USD 17.26 million for post-project operations 
and maintenance is reassuring. The irrigation department will maintain facilities under Outputs 
1 and 2, and other MOAC agencies will run Output 3 under regular government budget in the 
future. 
51. 
Economic analysis and project viability: an economic analysis has been carried out and 
results in economic internal rate of return (EIRR) ranges between 29 and 33 per cent. The 
analysis considered benefits mainly from the avoided cost of agriculture yield affected by 
climate change and damages from extreme events. The sensitivity analysis is based on the 
scenarios: 1) an increase in cost of 20 per cent; 2) a decrease in benefits of 20 per cent; and 3) a 
simultaneous increase in cost of 20 per cent and decrease in benefits of 20 per cent. The net 
present values under all scenarios remain positive.  
52. 
Concessionality of GCF financing: the AE has proposed 100 per cent grant financing thus 
asking for maximum concessionality. While the project does not lead to any direct revenue 
 
2 Standard & Poor's credit rating for Thailand stands at BBB+ with stable outlook. Moody's credit rating for Thailand 
was last set at Baa1 with stable outlook. 
(Accessed 1st June 2021: https://tradingeconomics.com/thailand/rating)

=== PAGE 87 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 87 
 
 
generation, the Government of Thailand and AE may consider partial concessional debt 
financing for the intervention based on the investment grade rating of the country. Moody’s has 
rated the country as Baa1 (2020) and stated in its credit opinion that the credit profile of 
Thailand reflects strong public and external finances that give the country significant room to 
counter shocks, including the economic hit from the coronavirus outbreak. Moody’s has also 
recognized lower government debt as compared to its peers and low share of foreign currency 
debt in overall debt of the Government.  
4.3.4. 
Compliance risk (medium risk) 
53. 
UNDP (the AE) has advised that they apply a zero-tolerance policy in relation to fraud 
and corruption. The guiding principles of UNDP's commitment to prevent, identify and address 
all acts of fraud and corruption have been laid out in the UNDP Policy against Fraud and Corrupt 
Practices (the FCP Policy), which applies to all activities and operations of UNDP, including 
projects and programmes.  
54. 
The fundamental principles of UNDP's FCP Policy serve as the basis for and are 
integrated in UNDP's policy frameworks in relation to procurement, financial management, 
internal control and accountability and staff rules and regulations. Under the terms of the FCP 
Policy and related policies, UNDP has a commitment, when developing a new programme or 
project, to ensure that risks related to fraud and corrupt practices are fully identified and 
considered in the programme/project design and processes and that adequate and effective 
measures to mitigate such risks are put in place.  
55. 
UNDP has advised that the processes and requirements for UNDP's partnership capacity 
assessment require UNDP to carry out a thorough due diligence of the risks in relation to the 
programme/project it intends to engage in, as well as in relation to the potential partners who 
will be involved in the implementation of such programmes/projects. Such capacity 
assessments include, among other things:  
(a) 
A screening of all entities being considered to be contracted by or partner with UNDP 
against the Consolidated UN Security Council Sanctions List (to which UNDP, by virtue of 
being an organ of the United Nations and therefore being part of the same entity, is also 
beholden).  
(b) 
An assessment of any history of fraud, corruption, or other fraudulent practices; and/or 
(c) 
Potential conflicts of interest.  
56. 
Based on this capacity assessment, UNDP then builds an internal control output into the 
design of the project and ensures that the financial management of the programme/project 
contains adequate safeguards to prevent, monitor and address any risks and acts of fraud, 
corruption or anti-money laundering/counter-financing of terrorism that may be identified.  
57. 
UNDP has also informed that they apply a Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfers 
(HACT), which represents a common operational (harmonized) framework for transferring cash 
to government and non-governmental partners (both implementing partners and responsible 
parties). The common objective of the HACT framework is to support a closer alignment of 
development aid with national priorities and to strengthen national capacities for management 
and accountability. It serves as a set of procedures on requesting, disbursing, providing 
assurance, and reporting on funds to effectively manage risks, reduce transaction costs and 
promote sustainable development in a coordinated manner.  
58. 
Cash transfers (above the threshold of USD 300,000 in a programme cycle, or otherwise 
in all circumstances where UNDP considers the operating environment to be high risk or has no 
prior experience with the partner) are presumed to present a material risk to UNDP and will 
require a HACT micro-assessment and risk rating by qualified third-party service providers.

Spot checks, programmatic Output verification and auditing requirements are then determined 
and carried out at a frequency determined in line with the risk rating. 
59. 
As per the accreditation master agreement between UNDP and GCF, UNDP will apply its

ensures protection against retaliation for any person that has reported allegations of 
wrongdoing or cooperated with a duly authorized audit or investigation.

reports.5 UNDP also has internal control protocols consisting of an Internal Control Framework 
and Anti-Fraud Policy.6

programming and project activities. Reference is made to the Partner Capacity Assessment Tool

request proceeding to the next steps for processing.

72. 
As an adaptation project, the intervention aims at enhancing climate resilience in the 
Yom and Nam river basins of Thailand through efficient water management and resilient 
agricultural practices. It is expected to benefit 62,000 direct and 471,561 indirect beneficiaries 
in the basin. 
73. 
 Overall, the funding proposal and log frame have been found to adequately apply the 
results management framework and performance measurement framework indicators. The 
outcomes, impacts, and the related indicators are aligned with GCF requirements. Pertinent 
information on Means of Verification, baseline and targets have been provided. Similarly, the 
log-frame contains clear presentation of project-level results, their indicators and other 
required information in a concise manner. Their diligent application during implementation 
would facilitate results monitoring, progress reporting, performance assessment and finally 
their delivery on the ground.

4.6 
Legal assessment 
75. 
The accreditation master agreement was signed with the accredited entity on 5 August 
2016 and became effective on 23 November 2016. 
76. 
The AE has provided a certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals

77. 
The proposed project will be implemented in the Kingdom of Thailand, a country in 
which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other 
things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need 
to be further assessed. The Secretariat submitted a revised draft of the privileges and 
immunities agreement to the Government of Thailand on 5 October 2018. However, no 
response has been received yet. 
78. 
The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and Independent Integrity Unit have 
both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or 
investigations, as appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with relevant privileges 
and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by GCF are made only after 
GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country or 
has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of 
FP170 
Proposal name: 
Enhancing climate resilience in Thailand through effective water 
management and sustainable agriculture 
Accredited entity: 
United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) 
Country/(ies): 
Thailand 
Project/programme size: 
Small 
I.
Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel1
1.1 
Impact potential 
    Scale: N/A 
1.1.1. 
Adaptation impact 
1.
Thailand’s vulnerability to climate change resides in the geographical and
socioeconomic context: an extensive coastline, a high dependence of rural communities on
agriculture, and extensive urban areas located on flood-prone plains. The proposal argues that
the most significant impacts would be the increased risk of floods and droughts, and that the
risk has already risen since 1955, as a consequence of a higher incidence of extreme rainfall
events and increased rainfall variability. The proposal states that, according to projections,
these climate extremes will increase both in frequency and intensity.2, 3
2.
The proposed project seeks to enhance the water sector’s climate change adaptation
capacity and agriculture livelihoods in the Yom and Nan river basins, by committing to establish
an integrated water resources management (IWRM) regime that focuses on year-round water
management, taking into consideration wet-season-specific floods and dry-season-specific
droughts in an integrated approach. In doing so, both the major concerns of floods and droughts
are likely to addressed, where flood control through hydraulic structures will be complemented
by early warning and by establishing ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA), and drought will be
managed by using water that is likely to be retained in reservoirs as well as by enhancing
infiltration of water in groundwater aquifers by means of water storage and induced recharge of
aquifers. Once the availability of water for use during the dry season is ensured for
compensating the growing moisture deficit, irrigation facilities are expected to ameliorate
drought and facilitate crop production even under climate change.
3.
Adaptation in the Yom and Nan basins is likely to be achieved through three main
components: (1) informed planning in the water and agricultural sectors through improved
climate information and cross-sectorial coordination; (2) improved water management through
strengthened infrastructure complemented by EbA measures; and (3) improved climate
resilience of farmers through support for incorporating climate forecasts and knowledge of
climate change risks into planning, and for developing the market for agricultural products.
1 This assessment is based on the funding proposal submitted to the independent Technical Advisory Panel on 12 July 
2021. 
2 Funding proposal, pages 2 and 5. 
3 The project’s climate rationale is discussed in section 1.4 of this assessment.

=== PAGE 92 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 92 
 
 
 
4. 
Component 1 is focused on improving the availability and quality of climate information 
and supporting its dissemination, and on facilitating interministerial coordination. The Royal 
Irrigation Department’s (RID) Intelligence-based Real-time Decision-Making Modelling (IRDM) 
tool,4 which currently aids the RID in managing water in the Chao Phraya River basin, would be 
updated to increase the level of forecasting precision as well as for generating projections for 
shorter time frames (currently, projections are made on an annual basis). Interministerial 
coordination for climate-informed and integrated planning would be facilitated through 
technical support in developing standard operating procedures (SOPs) for information-sharing 
across government stakeholders. Dissemination of climate information would involve the 
development of a communication strategy and the use of briefs, reports and smartphone apps. 
5. 
Although activity 1.1 includes the procurement and installation of climate and water 
monitoring equipment for a value of approximately USD 800,000, the original submission of this 
proposal did not include a description of this equipment. Upon the request of the independent 
Technical Advisory Panel (TAP), the accredited entity (AE) submitted a breakdown of the costs 
of the equipment. The detailed budget of the present submission was updated to include a list of 
equipment to be installed. However, as indicated below, the independent TAP questions the 
adequacy of some of the proposed equipment to achieve the proposed objectives. 
6. 
Through component 2, the project would strengthen water infrastructure through hard 
engineering works complemented with ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) measures. Activities 
related to grey infrastructure include the review and enhancement of the existing engineering 
designs for the 13 schemes of the Yom-Nan river basin area, to ensure the designs take into 
consideration climate change adaptation, and construction works to upgrade two canal head 
regulators along Khlong Muang Chang and Khlong Ta Kay Reg in the Yom-Nan river basin area. 
With increased rainfall run-off under climate change, these regulators (i.e. water 
infrastructures) would ameliorate flood vulnerability at the lower reaches of the Yom-Nan river 
basin. 5 
7. 
EbA measures are perceived to be complementary to the hard flood-controlling 
measures, with specific objectives to further strengthen flood amelioration and to enhance the 
adaptive capacity of affected communities within the river basin. EbA measures could include 
support for the implementation of integrated farming practices (such as rice-fish management), 
revegetation of riverbanks, wetlands management techniques, erosion control, and soil 
conservation measures. The German Agency for International Cooperation (GIZ) is currently 
developing, together with the Thai water sector, an EbA Guidebook to be used as a national 
standard for the systematic identification, prioritization and selection of EbA measures. The 
Guidebook will be complemented by a Code of Practice that will cover specific design issues of 
 
4 IRDM is a collaborative effort involving five organizations: Royal Irrigation Department, the Agricultural Research 
Development Agency, the National Research Council of Thailand, the King Mongkut’s University of Technology 
North Bangkok, and the Thailand Research Organizations Network. IRDM is a milestone in enhancing the 
institutional coordination between the agricultural and irrigation departments, as well as in developing the public 
forecasting and modelling capacity. IRDM can calculate the amount of rainfall and inflow of water into reservoirs 
during the wet season and design water release schedules for 12-month periods, with a relatively high level of 
accuracy. Its mathematical models can be used to forecast inflows into different water sources, support water 
management plans for individual irrigation zones and estimate agricultural yield and revenue. 
5 In 2012, hydrological modelling (Mike-11 ECOlab&AD) for the Yom-Nan river basin was conducted by the 
Department of Water Resources, under the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment. The model represented 
river profiles and modelled water levels under four different scenarios: (i) no water reservoir construction; (ii) no 
dam at the upper Yom river; (iii) reservoir construction; and (iv) dam construction at the upper Yom river, for both 
the year 2010 and future years (2015-2020). A number of models were used to model different aspects of the Yom-
Nan river basin, including MIKE FLOOD for flood, MIKE11 ECOLab&AD for water quality, and MIKE BASIN for water 
consumption. These models are updated every year, especially after flood events when river cross-sections can 
change. However, based on the TAP’s findings from the interview with the AE, hydrological modelling including 
climate change projections has not been carried out. Therefore, it is not possible to ascertain the impact of climate 
change on flooding and on the general river basin regime.

=== PAGE 93 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 93 
 
 
 
selected EbA measures. The EbA Guidebook and Code of Practice are expected to be ready by 
the end of 2021 and will be published by the Thai Office of the National Water Resources 
(ONWR).  
8. 
EbA measures would be designed using modelling techniques to assess the potential 
impact on the hydrological regime, in order to support the prioritization of EbA measures and 
cost-benefit analyses. The AE further explained that EbA measures would include a mixture of 
best practices and lessons learned in past and ongoing projects, which are briefly described in 
the feasibility study. Interventions would be designed through the following process: (1) 
geographical scaling (mapping of hot spots) to define the geographic boundaries of the pilot 
areas; identification of sub-regions within each project area and mapping of major hydro system 
features and economic activities; (2) scoping by assessing environmental and socioeconomic 
impacts and establishing priorities among the major concerns and issues as well as tracing the 
root causative factors behind the selected concerns and issues; (3) data collection; (4) modelling 
for verification and quantification of root-cause relationships with either physically 
deterministic or conceptual models (hydrologic modelling with TalsimNG software and/or 
hydraulic 2D modelling as required; erosion and sedimentation modelling with Modified 
Uniform Soil Loss Equation (MUSLE); water quality modelling with GISMO software; assessment 
to identify risks and uncertainties; identification and ranking of potential EbA measures).6 
9. 
The funding proposal includes a more thorough description of EbA measures. 7 The AE 
stated that “EbA measures will be implemented in and around the two water infrastructure 
interventions of the project area – Klong Mueang and Klong Takae. These areas are mostly 
dominated by the paddy fields, 27,936 rai and 58,964 rai are the cultivated areas, respectively. 
While the EbA measures are not necessarily oriented to agricultural production, they will 
support year-round better water management by contributing to IWRM and provide ecosystem 
services which will benefit cultivated areas.” 8 The independent TAP understands that the 
“27,936 rai and 58,964 rai” do not correspond to the coverage of EbA interventions, but to the 
cultivated areas around the water infrastructure works, that would supposedly be benefited by 
EbA. Without an estimated spatial coverage of the actual area that would be directly modified by 
EbA interventions (including aquatic and adjoining wetland-forest covered areas), it is not 
possible to assess the per hectare cost of EbA interventions or the estimation of target 
beneficiaries for the proposed activity 2.2. 
10. 
The design and implementation of EbA measures would include multiple measures in 
three locations and would cost USD 1.7 M (activity 2.2G in project budget). Potential EbA sites 
have been narrowed down to Pichai Srinakorn, Khonkairas, Sawankha Lok, Promphiram, 
Bangrakham and Muang Phitsahulok. As indicated in paragraph 9 above, the method for 
estimating beneficiaries of this activity is not described in the proposal. 
11. 
Component 3 is focused on enhancing the climate resilience of farmers. The project 
would provide training and technical assistance to farmers and extension officers on the 
application of climate forecasts and knowledge of climate change risks in household agriculture 
planning, including the development of district-level adaptation action plans and 
implementation of climate-smart agricultural practices. 
12. 
The project would support on-farm interventions to improve drought and flood 
resilience through technical assistance for the development of on-farm EbA guidelines, the 
procurement of small scale equipment for farmers (such as water pumps, small cultivators, on-
farm water level measurement, etc.), the construction of 100 ponds as micro-scale water 
 
6 Explanations given in AE’s responses, 22 September 2020. 
7 Feasibility Study, section 4.2.1. Measures would include wetland / floodplain / oxbow lake restoration; living weirs; 
riparian zone rehabilitation / bank stabilization by vetiver grass, buffer zones, sedimentation traps and/or an 
appropriate combination of such measures. 
8 See AE responses to independent TAP comments, dated 29th June and 21st July, 2021.

=== PAGE 94 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 94 
 
 
 
reservoirs, the establishment of community nurseries, the co-design of a financial instrument 
with the Bank of Agriculture and Agriculture Cooperatives (BAAC) to support farmers with 
adaptation investments, and capacity-building for farmers to support market access for their 
products. BAAC has committed USD 16 million for this instrument, as parallel financing. Upon 
request from the independent TAP, the AE provided a commitment letter from BAAC.9 
13. 
Activity 3.1 includes the implementation, at a demonstration scale, of what is called 
precision agriculture or smart farming. This approach to agricultural production involves the 
use of modern observation and communication technology to generate and use geo-referenced 
information for the control of agricultural systems, integrating characteristics of soil, plants and 
climate. Through the use of these systems, algorithms executed by machines and based on 
parameters provided by machines, make an increasing number of decisions. For adequate 
implementation of precision agriculture to address droughts, the evapotranspiration level of the 
specific crops in the targeted area must be well understood. Hourly evapotranspiration data or, 
at least, daily data, as against data on water availability at root zones and water deficit, are 
needed. Although the use of precision agriculture has been applied in extreme water-stressed 
agriculture systems across the world with the aim of saving as much water as possible during 
irrigation applications, the intention to use precision agriculture in water-abundant areas such 
as the Yom-Nan basin appears technologically feasible but not viable in the economic sense.  
14. 
Within activity 3.1, the project would install five Internet of Things (IoT) platforms, 
which would include IoT sensors, drones with multispectral cameras for surveying agricultural 
areas, hardware and software for data processing, storage and communication, and technical 
support and related training. Although it is widely recognized that precision agriculture has the 
potential to increase production and reduce the need for inputs with large-scale and/or high-
cost production, the use of such advanced technology by poor, vulnerable farmers has 
numerous challenges. For example, the platforms include drone kits for spraying liquids (water 
or organic fertilizers), which can carry only 15 litres and would cost USD 20,000 each. 
Recognizing that smallholders in the Yom-Nan basin are among the most vulnerable population 
under climate change, there is no clear reason why agricultural activities including precision 
irrigation should be mechanized in small farms. The selection criteria for this activity stipulate 
that the landowner must have an agricultural area not larger than 1,6 hectares).10  There is no 
analysis in the feasibility study (FS) in relation to the potential annual increment of productivity 
in pilot farms as against annual depreciation of the investment plus the annualized operation 
and maintenance (O&M) costs. Questioned by the TAP, the AE responded that: “A separate 
economic cost-benefit analysis of farm-level precision agriculture platforms – that include IoT 
investments and drone technology – does suggest a benefit-cost ratio greater than one and a 
positive NPV [net present value].  This analysis includes the full costs of investment (upfront 
capital costs) along with annual O&M costs. The primary benefit for farmers that adopt the 
technology platform is increased farming income as the investment allows them to grow higher 
yield varieties and in marginal seasons. This is all under the overhang of a changing climate i.e., 
under the assumption farm incomes will erode as the climate changes.” The economic analysis 
referred to is not included in the proposal. Apart from upfront investment requirements for 
precision agriculture involving modern technologies, there is a significant investment 
requirement for operationalization of precision agricultural practices, which often becomes a 
barrier to adoption of such techniques, especially at smallholder levels. In the absence of 
adequate analytical explanations as well as a roadmap for the extension of such techniques 
among smallholders, there is considerable uncertainty regarding the immediate uptake of the 
proposed intervention within the project implementation period. 
 
9 Annex 13: Co-financing commitment letters, part 2. 
10 Response to TAP comments at B.27, response 5.

=== PAGE 95 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 95 
 
 
 
15. 
Information regarding the time required for complete realization of the capital 
investment from the proceeds of increased productivity has not been provided in the FS. It 
appears difficult to understand whether there is any economic viability at all of precision 
agriculture to address potential water stress in the farms indicated, keeping in view their 
average potential decline in productivity due to claimed climate-change-induced increased 
water stress. Since this type of technology is not developed in Thailand, parts will need to be 
imported, which may pose a threat to the sustainability in the medium and long term.  
16. 
The proposal does not provide an evaluation of the reach these platforms would have in 
terms of direct beneficiaries (people and agricultural area), or of the potential economic 
efficiency of this approach. Questioned about this matter, the AE responded that “Based upon 
survey of existing data of the target area and criterion of beneficiary’s selection, 10 farms in 
each district will be selected for demonstrating as pilot farms. Total pilot farms will be 70 farms 
from 7 districts11; Pichai Srinakorn, Khonkairas, Sawankha Lok, Promphiram, Bangrakham and 
Muang Phitsahulok…” and that an economic evaluation by the BAAC “…confirmed that using 
drone and IoT can increase efficiency of farming by reducing water consumption, cost of soil 
testing, fertilizer mixing, seeding and fertilizing. The intervention will further establish best 
practices which can be replicated and up-scaled (further supported by the credit line to be made 
available through BAAC).” The AE indicated that this activity would serve as a pilot project to 
demonstrate results and accelerate the introduction of precision agriculture.  
17. 
Although the proposal states that IoT equipment would be owned and maintained by 
the district governments,12 the operation and maintenance (O&M) costs were not found in the 
O&M plan (annex 21) of the first submission of this FP (B.27). Questioned by the TAP on this 
matter, the AE responded that “… O&M cost is built into the sale price and warrantee from the 
dealers in the domestic market (Thailand). The dealers include an insurance and drone permit 
as one price. The after-sale service is generally provided by the dealers.” Even with the included 
insurance and warrantee, it is not clear how the dealers would cover the operation costs or the 
maintenance costs after purchase or for how long they would do so. Also, the dealers would 
have a natural incentive to promote the use of precision agriculture and IoT in farms with 
minimum O&M cost, which may generate unfairness in the distribution of assistance and may 
also make the application of this technology less efficient. The AE also added that “the project 
management unit will support resource mobilization for further replication and upscaling, and 
O&M cost (if applicable) from the regular public budget, distributed to government departments 
under the Ministry of Agricultural and Cooperatives (MoAC). Additional resources for IoT/O&M 
can be mobilized through the regular public budget for capacity-building to farmers and 
innovation research…” and that “there is high possibility to mobilize the O&M cost from the CAT 
(Communication Authority of Thailand) that has promoted the IoT for smart farming.” The 
economic analysis in the current submission (B.30) included the O&M costs for the IoT 
platforms, estimated in USD 107,000 per year after year five of project implementation. 
However, the O&M Plan does not include a description of the composition of this cost and the 
method used to estimate it. Asked by the TAP, the AE described the method used to estimate 
O&M costs of IoT platforms.13 
18. 
Each IoT platform would cost USD 270,000, or USD 27,000 for each farm, making it 
unlikely for small farmers (up to 1.6 ha) to adopt it or even operate and maintain it by their own 
means. Following the estimation presented of the O&M costs, for groups of ten farmers sharing 
a platform to use this technology, each farmer would have an annual O&M cost of USD 2,000. In 
this case, being a pilot, the government would cover these costs, but for widespread 
 
11 The original submission included the installation of seven IoT platforms (one for each district), which was reduced 
to five in the present submission. Hence, 50 farms would participate in the pilot programme. 
12 Funding proposal, paragraph 98. 
13 Response to TAP comments, question 8.

=== PAGE 96 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02
