implementation by small farmers, it is uncertain how these systems would be operated and 
maintained. The use of this technology would not only be unaffordable for small farmers but 
would also require very high technical skills for O&M, thereby relying on external support and 
imported parts.  
19. 
In response to concerns raised by the independent TAP, the present submission reduces 
the installation of IoT platforms from 7 to 5,14 reducing the budget for this activity by 
approximately USD 0.5 million, which was transferred to activity 3.2, which supports farmers 
with low tech/simpler interventions (e.g. on farm ponds, small equipment, etc.).  The total 
budget for component 3 remains unchanged. 
20. 
The independent TAP noted that project performance indicators do not include targets 
for this activity. In response to this comment, the AE suggested adding a performance indicator 
to assess the rate of IoT adoption by gender, age, socioeconomic profile of household, and the 
impact on productivity. The AE further clarified that “The benefit from IoT is captured under 
indicator A1.2, ‘Number of males and females benefiting from the adoption of diversified, 
climate resilient livelihood options (including fisheries, agriculture, tourism, etc.)’. Any further 
upscaling using the BAAC credit would be captured under indicator 3.2, ‘Number of households 
with access to finance for climate resilient farming investment’.”15 
21. 
The Department of Royal Rainmaking and Agricultural Aviation (DRRAA) undertakes an 
annual average of 5,000 rain-seeding flights.16 Such activities may have an effect on 
precipitation patterns. The project does not include a description of reach and impact of the 
cloud-seeding programme being carried out by this governmental agency. Questioned by the 
independent TAP, the AE responded that despite the fact that cloud-seeding activities have not 
been the subject of long-term research, “published research on the effect of rainmaking in 
Thailand did not find a statistically significant relationship between cloud seeding and increases 
in rainfall amount or rainfall area.”17 The AE also added, “the seeding programme is still mostly 
active during drier pre-monsoon periods, before the southwest monsoon. The seeding 
programme is therefore not expected to affect rainfall during flooding events, which occur later 
during the year when the southwest monsoon is more intense.” 
22. 
Direct beneficiaries of project interventions are estimated to be the 62,000 people who 
live in the project site area, with indirect beneficiaries reaching approximately 470,000 people 
that live in project districts, plus 25 million people living in the greater Chao Phraya River Basin.

1.2.1. 
Innovation 
23. 
Although most of the project revolves around expanding and improving existing climate 
and water monitoring and management systems, and redesigning and constructing water 
infrastructure (floodgates and canals), the proposal includes some innovative activities in the 
form of EbA measures. Although concrete EbA measures are not yet defined, the present 
submission includes an accurate description of the potential alternatives, of which three would 
be selected for implementation based on a “diagnostic analysis including vulnerability and risk 
assessment, modelling, cause-impact chains and economic evaluation”.18 The implementation of

EbA measures for climate resilient water management and livelihood development would 
represent an innovative approach for Thailand, for the first time integrating both grey and 
green approaches to water drainage, storage, diversion and irrigation. This paradigm shift to a 
hybrid approach to adaptation has potential to maximize effectiveness and cost-effectiveness.19 
The project is also innovative in integrating climate risk management into IWRM, employing 
solutions which simultaneously tackle drought and flood by diverting floodwaters to replenish 
groundwater for use in dry periods, whilst also mitigating the floods. 
24. 
Precision agriculture is also in its early days globally, and although BAAC launched a soft 
loan for purchasing drones in 2019, the disbursement rate was low.20 This could be because 
precision agriculture is still at the introduction stage in Thailand, or because the idea of using 
such expensive equipment did not make economic sense to Thai small farmers. Perhaps the 
potential returns are too small compared to the capital cost, the incremental loss of opportunity 
costs through depreciation and annualized O&M costs, despite the fact that the BAAC has 
created access to credit. Despite institutional incentives, the current lack of enthusiasm 
involving the advanced precision agriculture technology may be an indication that the 
innovation is mostly theoretical. Overall, the funding proposal fails to provide adequate 
explanation justifying the use of precision agriculture. Although innovative, it is costly (both 
from a CapEx and an OpEx basis), thereby hindering diffusion of this advanced technology to 
low-income small farmers in the region.

1.2.5. 
Scalability and replicability 
31. 
Given that the entire country faces similar challenges regarding floods and droughts, 
and considering that capacity-building would benefit national institutions (RID and TMD), the 
project has good potential for scaling up project activities to other regions of Thailand without 
equally increasing the total costs of implementation. Parallel financing from BAAC in the form of 
credit for farmers practicing climate-informed and sustainable agriculture would also support 
scaling up. However, scaling up of practicing precision agriculture without increasing the total 
cost of implementation appears over-optimistic. 
32. 
The proposal presents a clear theory of change describing the existing barriers and root 
causes that undermine the capacity for mitigating flood and drought risks, and how they relate 
to project activities.

1.4 
Needs of the recipient  
 
 
 
 
         Scale: N/A   
1.4.1. 
Vulnerability of the country and climate rationale 
38. 
The proposal argues that wet seasons are becoming more intense, with the target basin 
being increasingly unable to manage slow run-off to the central plains and the greater Bangkok 
area. Further, the proposal states that dry seasons are becoming drier, with the water that the 
infrastructure is able to retain being insufficient to cover the irrigation needs of farmers. The 
increased run-off of more intense precipitation events could also be accelerating soil erosion 
and impacting groundwater resources. 
39. 
The climate rationale presented in a previous submission of this proposal was 
questioned by the independent TAP for using gridded data sets instead of real observed data 
from stations for evaluating historical trends. The present submission states that it “utilized a 
daily rainfall and air temperature dataset from 17 stations within the Chao Phraya basin 
provided by Thai Meteorological Department” complemented by “freely available global gridded 
data products”.21 This observed data complemented with gridded data is used for trend 
analysis, for calculating extreme rainfall, temperature and drought indices, and for validating 
climate models. Model-based outputs are only useful when a model is selected on the basis of its 
performance to reproduce observational data trends. The process is called “validation”, and is 
intended for the country or the region in question. If a model output is used without proper 
validation, the outputs are inadequate for making projections on which to base and design a 
project.  
40. 
The validation of the model ensembles used for climate projections is made against 
computer-assisted reanalysis data sets22 developed by the Climate Research Unit (CRU) and the 
Global Precipitation Climatology Centre (GPCC). The feasibility study presented in the first 
submission was missing a comparison between observed and gridded data. The AE later 
submitted charts for monthly and annual rainfall and monthly and annual mean temperature 
showing a comparison between data from observations, CRU, GPCC and Climate Hazards group 
Infrared Precipitation with Stations (CHIRPS).23 Since the CHIRPS dataset takes into account 
satellite observations of rainfall data, calibrated against point-source based observational 
datasets, the alternative validation of rainfall data involving computer generated gridded 
datasets is acceptable. The proposal deals with seasonal high rainfall and run-off to cause floods 
as well as a dry seasonal lack of rainfall leading to high evapotranspiration and droughts. 
 
21 Annex 26, section 1.3.1. 
22 Annex 26, figures 14a, 14b, 15a, 15b. 
23 AE responses to TAP comments, 29 June 29 2021.

=== PAGE 100 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 100 
 
 
 
Therefore, the use of CHIRPS rainfall datasets for the validation of gridded data and the use of 
the latter for the validation of projection models is also acceptable. 
41. 
The study concludes that while precipitation has become less frequent in most parts of 
Thailand, with a significant reduction in the occurrence of consecutive wet days, and total 
annual precipitation remaining mostly unchanged, precipitation events have become more 
intense.24 Such claims, however, remain questionable in the absence of rainfall intensity analysis 
involving the peak rainfall season. The trends shown in the study are mostly based on a non-
significant relationship involving rainfall-related indices, and their extent of change is not very 
high. For example, the number of rainy days per year and in May to October fell by only one day 
over a full decade, and the simple daily intensity index increased by only 0.17 mm/day/decade. 
Regarding rainfall historical data, annex 26 concludes that: “Trends are weak and inconsistent 
and should not be interpreted as systematic drivers behind historical extreme events (such as 
the 2011 flood), nor as expressions of anthropogenic climate change.” 
42. 
Despite such weaknesses in trend analyses, it is easily recognized that the total rainfall 
is increasing against a reduction in the number of rainy days, which has resulted in 
intensification of rainfall during the rainy season. In such a condition, rainfall run-off will 
increase and tend to generate flow volumes to overtop riverbanks and cause floods.  
43. 
Flood is a phenomenon that occurs when there is more water than available drainage 
capacity. In a river basin, flood generally occurs as a seasonal phenomenon, when the water 
flow at any given point exceeds drainage capacity and water level rises above a locally perceived 
‘danger level’. The normal flood can become an ‘extreme flood’ event if the water level surpasses 
a perceived threshold beyond the known and defined danger level. Flood can also occur due to 
congestion, especially when the river is choked due to siltation or other obstacles. Urban floods 
occur when there is an extreme rainfall event (e.g. equal or more than a rainfall event 
surpassing an upper threshold in several consecutive hours; for example, the accumulation of 
100 mm or more rainfall in about 24 hours) that exceeds the urban drainage capacity. In a river 
basin, unusual and sudden (flash) flood can occur if a large volume of water is drained (for some 
reason, like breaking of a dam, or outburst of a cloud, or breach of a glacier lake to trigger a 
glacial lake outburst flood) beyond a threshold in excess of the drainage capacity of any given 
point in the river basin. In a previous submission, the independent TAP could not find any 
supporting information in the funding proposal defining flood in the target river basin. No 
relationship was made in the proposal between any defined extreme rainfall event and a given 
water flow threshold beyond which floods occur. There was no sense of cause and effect in the 
discussion in the feasibility study. The present submission defines flood as when the flow of the 
Chao Phraya River exceeds 2,800 cubic metres per second (m3/s), which happened during the 
devastating 2011 floods. 
44. 
The feasibility study presents data, showing that the validation of the models used for 
climate projections also show the projections for the MRI-AGCM3.2s atmospheric model. This is 
used for rainfall projections, and discharge projections are obtained from these through 
hydrological modelling. The feasibility study presents the results from a study by Wichakul et al. 
(2014), which estimated that there would be increases in run-off of more than 20 per cent at the 
beginning of the Chao Phraya River due to increases in mid-season rainfall. Available data25 
show the maximum daily discharge corresponding to different return periods for present 
climate, near future climate and future climate for four locations. As per the data presented by 
the AE, at the beginning of the Chao Phraya River, a flow of 2,800 m3/s has a return period of 
five years at present. In the near future a flood with a return period of five years would produce 
 
24 Feasibility study, page 40. 
25 Annex 26, figure 29.

=== PAGE 101 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 101 
 
 
 
a discharge of 3,300 m3/s. This clearly exhibits that under climate change, flood intensity will 
significantly increase due to a significant increase in run-off. 
45. 
Also in annex 26, in section 6.2, which deals with mitigation of flood risks, it is concluded 
that: “Given the results of the modelling above and the uncertainty associated with future 
projections of rainfall it is recommended that interventions to mitigate flood risks be based on 
current flood risks, known areas where flooding risks pose a threat, and to the extent possible 
allow for flexibility to adapt to changes in future rainfall distributions and flooding as they 
evolve.” The AE clarified that project interventions “have been selected as highest priority 
activities, based on in-depth modelling of current flood risks by the Thailand Government…”. 
Since the Yom-Nan river basin is managed by hydraulic structures, hydrological modelling must 
show at which levels of discharge beyond an upper threshold flooding actually occurs beyond 
the operation of the management structures. The AE clarified that the maximum discharge 
levels presented in figure 79 of the feasibility study for each section of the Chao Phraya River 
represent flood thresholds and take into account the current water management 
infrastructure.26 
Figure 19: Changes in maximum water discharge at Phrae (northern Chao Phraya) for different 
future periods under RCP4.5 (left) and RCP8.5 (right) 
 
46. 
The independent TAP acknowledges the fact that, as per the above-mentioned modelling 
results, the possibility of greater flooding in future decades cannot be ruled out and the system 
will only get wetter, despite the two regulators in the Yom-Nan river basin. Under such 
circumstances, even under the RCP4.5 scenario, the investment in water infrastructure (i.e. 
flood regulators) can be considered to be a climate-driven investment, despite having a weak 
evidence base regarding flooding induced by climate change. 
 
26 AE responses to TAP comments, 21 July 2021.

=== PAGE 102 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 102 
 
 
 
47. 
Usually in monsoon-influenced regions across the globe, agricultural drought as a 
phenomenon occurs, if at all, only in non-monsoon months. In the broader climatological 
perspective, Thailand’s climate is influenced by the El Niño-Southern Oscillation (ENSO). 
Therefore, the potential influence of the El Niño phase of ENSO in contributing to increased 
droughts cannot be ruled out, especially under climate change. Precipitation in non-monsoon 
months was found to be increasing (low confidence) at a rate of 10.8mm/decade.27 Increased 
rainfall during the non-monsoon period would not exacerbate flooding, rather it might 
compensate for evapotranspirative losses from the topsoil, thereby reducing drought impacts. 
This increase in rainfall, as well as the increase in temperature over all the months, have been 
analysed to examine the past and projected drought-related indices such as the standard 
precipitation index (SPI), standardized precipitation and evapotranspiration index (SPEI) and 
potential evapotranspiration (PET). While SPI and SPEI are indices widely used for 
understanding landscape-level drought, PET is widely used for understanding crop-specific 
drought and for calculating crop water deficits – a quantitative index for analysing droughts.   
48. 
Although annex 26 (Climate Change in Chao Phraya Basin) concludes that “Trends in 
extreme rainfall indices are weak, and do not show any consistent sign, nor clear spatial 
pattern,” the PET analyses exhibit an increasing drought intensity in the near future (2021–
2050) for all months. This indicates that the perceived compensation from increased rainfall 
during the wet season would not be adequate to prevent the occurrence of drought, which 
would require irrigation water for dry season paddy cultivation in the target areas. Moreover, 
the 12-month SPEI trends in the four sub-basins including the Yom-Nan basin clearly show that 
the projected values in future years would continue to decline, leading to higher levels of 
persisting droughts. Accordingly, the estimated water deficits would be likely to increase under 
such drought conditions, as presented by model outputs.  
1.4.2. 
Vulnerable groups 
49. 
The project targets farming households and farming communities, which are the groups 
most vulnerable to flooding and drought in Thailand. The design phase of the project included a 
survey that revealed that 96 per cent of households in the Yom-Nan River basin were dependent 
on rice farming as a source of income. Selection criteria prioritized vulnerability and urgency, 
considering the proximity of communities to the river basin and their reliance on water for 
agriculture. 
1.4.3. 
Economic and social development 
50. 
Thailand’s rural population is mostly engaged in agriculture, which generates over 10 
per cent of the gross domestic product, and accounts for 74 per cent of water consumption. 
Almost 6 million households in Thailand depend on agriculture as their source of income. The 
agricultural sector in Thailand employs approximately half of the country’s population and is, 
overall, dominated by smallholder farms which, in the project area, have an average land size 
between 4.00 and 4.64 hectares. 
51. 
In 2015, Thailand became an upper-middle income economy with average per capita 
annual income of USD 5,775. Over the last four decades, Thailand has made remarkable 
progress in social and economic development. Poverty declined from 67 per cent in 1986 to 11 
per cent in 2014. However, income inequality is evident between urban and rural populations. 
Poverty is higher in the northern-central region, where the target area is located. 
1.4.4. 
Absence of alternative sources of financing

and technical officials within MoAC with expertise in water and agriculture were also consulted

1.6.4. 
Best practices 
64. 
EbA can be considered a best practice for improving climate resilience in a cost-efficient 
manner. GIZ is currently developing an EbA Guidebook to be used as a national standard for the 
systematic identification, prioritization and selection of EbA measures at river basin scale. 
65. 
Potential EbA measures include Monkey Cheek, which is a flood control concept that 
focuses on building reservoirs to retain rainwater and alleviate discharge, vetiver grass to 
prevent soil erosion and landslides, the “living weir” concept to stabilize soils and riverbanks, 
forest restoration using temporary weirs, wastewater treatment using grass filtration, lagoons, 
wetlands and mangroves, and conservation agriculture practices. 
66. 
Monkey Cheek projects should involve intense public consultations and adequate 
compensation to farmers who lose their lands to water reservoirs. The details regarding costs 
and benefits are not provided in the funding proposal and associated documents. 
II. 
Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
67. 
The available evidence base suggests that both floods and drought might take place in 
the near future in the targeted basin. Although the extent of increase in intensity of such hazards 
remains highly uncertain, the continuing occurrence of both phenomena is likely in the Yom-
Nan basin.  
68. 
In the baseline period (based on CRU data), the occurrence of drought events (SPEI 
values) was increasing. Thereby, an increase in drought driven by climate change could be 
established in the Chao Phraya River basin. 
69. 
Despite a weak evidence base, the data indicate that flooding will likely be a problem in 
the Chao Phraya basin. The incidence of higher levels of rainfall has been observed (not so much 
in the Chao Phraya basin itself, but in the country, especially in the southern sub-catchments). 
The model outputs indicate that run-off in the Chao Phraya basin will increase by 20–25 per 
cent by the 2040s. The return period of floods at the Chao Phraya headwaters will also become 
shorter, meaning that there will be more flood incidents in the medium future than in the past. 
Since there have been past floods, floods will likely continue to occur at a higher frequency.  
70. 
For these reasons, the independent TAP endorses this funding proposal with one 
condition:  
(a) 
The AE must present, prior to the first disbursement by the GCF under the project, (i) a  
feasibility assessment (based on existing case-studies and platforms in South East Asia) 
on the implementation of the project’s proposed utilization of an Internet of Things 
(IoT) irrigation system for rice cultivation; this should include a costing analysis, review 
of technical requirements, review of equipment suppliers, plan for development of a 
Thai version of  an IoT irrigation software platform; and (ii) a plan for development and 
scaling up such IoT irrigation systems by the Thai government as part of its plans for 
digital transformation and sustainability.  
71. 
The risk of cross posting or poaching of TMD and RID and other relevant agency 
personnel trained under this project may put at risk the effectiveness of this capacity-building. 
The independent TAP recommends that appropriate safeguards need to be in place, such as a 
minimum number of years for remaining in a post.

=== PAGE 106 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 106 
Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical 
Advisory Panel's assessment (FP170) 
Proposal name: 
Enhancing climate resilience in Thailand through effective water 
management and sustainable agriculture 
Accredited entity: 
United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) 
Country/(ies): 
Thailand 
Project/programme size: 
Small 
Impact potential 
We note iTAP’s assessment that by addressing both the major concerns of floods and 
droughts, the project is expected to facilitate climate resilient crop production. The project 
will, through integration of EbA and infrastructure establish a regime of integrated water 
resource management for year-round water security. 
Below, we address specific comments presented in this assessment, referring also to prior 
responses. 
Paragraph 9 
The comment on the per hectare cost of EbA interventions or the estimation of target 
beneficiaries is well noted. Since the EbA measures will be undertaken in and around the 
cultivated areas, the spatial coverage is indeed estimated based on these areas. The 
estimation will be vetted and refined during the participatory process with the communities 
whereby the final determination of the EbA measures will be made. A comprehensive Annex 
18(b) - Lessons Learned from Baseline Projects has been included in the FP package detailing 
the process by which EbA measures will be identified/selected. In addition, the proposal 
package includes the safeguards measures to be put in place to protect farmers in the area. 
Please refer to Annex 6(b) – Environmental and Social Management Framework. 
Paragraph 14 
We underscore that the government is proposing the establishment of five platforms (each 
covering an aggregate of 10 farms) to pilot precision agriculture technology across project-
targeted farms that will benefit from the investment of capital from GCF funds and O&M by 
the districts. Through a consultative process, the set of farms will be selected to serve as 
pilots whereby the farmers benefit from the higher yields and incomes and the data collected 
from these farms serves the government vision to improve climate resilient agriculture 
planning in the sector and drive future investments. The platform based on an IoT technology, 
has already been tested in other SE Asian countries including Thailand, and will address the 
issue of improved water management in the aggregated irrigation system thereby dealing 
with increased water demands predicted from increased evapotranspiration and increased 
temperature. The smallholder farmers are not expected to invest any funds to facilitate this 
pilot and the O&M, post-project, will continue to be the responsibility of the districts. It is also 
important to note that drones referred to in the ITAP comments serve multiple purposes,

=== PAGE 107 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 107 
 
 
 
particularly through remote sensing, data collection on crop health and herbicide application, 
and are not intended as the source of irrigation for the farms. Irrigated water will be applied 
through communal system of irrigation channels. The below picture is provided to clarify the 
use of precision agriculture involving Internet of Things (Iot) and drones: 
 
IoT is indeed an innovative technology (approx. $1.3M of the total GCF funding request) that 
is being adopted by the government across its many investments (government and donor 
funded) and there is a commitment for O&M and vision for upscaling that will ensure that 
these technologies become cost-effective over time. The precision agriculture interventions 
will be based on a successful research conducted by the Kasetsart University Thailand in 
collaboration with the Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives and international best 
practices, such as International Rice Research Institute application in SE Asia. The research 
indicates the IoT system is especially effectively for multiple purposes: 
•              Assist timing for planting; 
•              Land preparation; 
•              Drone usage for remote sensing (crop health data collection)_and efficient 
herbicide/pesticide application if this is needed; 
•              Enabling farmers to effectively manage water levels through better management of 
soil moisture levels (as monitored by IoT sensors)  
•              Data collection for enabling policy and planning with local government; and 
•              Climate data forecasting for better decision making.

=== PAGE 108 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 108 
 
 
 
On iTAP’s comment related to cost-benefit analysis, as per standard practice for the projects, 
the economic analysis for the overall project has been undertaken. This includes the cost-
benefit analysis of farm-level precision agriculture platforms – that include IoT investments 
and drone technology – and it does result in a benefit-cost ratio greater than one and a 
positive NPV [net present value] for the project in its entirety.  This analysis includes the full 
costs of investment (upfront capital costs) along with estimated annual O&M costs of this 
activity. The primary benefit for farmers that adopt the technology platform is increased 
farming income as the investment allows them to grow higher yield varieties and in marginal 
seasons. This is all under the overhang of a changing climate i.e., under the assumption farm 
incomes will erode as the climate changes. 
As acknowledged in the assessment, the intent is to demonstrate results to support Thai 
Government’s strategy to accelerate smart agriculture. The project will support this through 
testing the application in a new context with the view to working with Universities, private 
sector, financial institutions, communities and district agencies for upscale.  
With regards to replication and upscaling, the expectation is not that the project-targeted 
smallholders will adopt this technology en-masse. Rather, as part of the vision, the results will 
support early adoption and uptake by larger farms or groups of farms in Thailand, including 
through support from the BAAC credit thereby easing the way for a wider market for smart 
agriculture. This paves the way for innovative technologies to take root, leading to overall 
reduction of costs and wider adoption in the medium- to long-term. Please see the transition 
plan below from the National Strategy Thailand 4.0 to shift traditional agriculture into smart 
agriculture, highlighting the need for IoT and precision farming.   
 
Analysis done in the project will help define the appropriate number of rai (farm area) where 
precision agriculture results in benefits.  Results of the pilot will be shared widely to inform 
investments by other districts, small farmer groups or by larger farms.    
Paragraph 15 
We maintain that piloting technology does not necessitate that it is locally sourced. There are 
no pre-selected firms to provide this support, or preference of international vs local 
providers. In addition, there are Thai companies working in this space. For example:

=== PAGE 109 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 109 
 
 
 
https://www.arv.co.th/varuna/.  During project implementation, an open, competitive 
procurement process will ensure cost-effectiveness and value for money, with considerations 
given to training, maintenance and warranties available.   
Paragraph 17 
We recognize that O&M costs related to IoT and drones are not fully clear as it is a new 
industry and the estimations are based on existing case studies. Dealers are not expected to 
cover O&M per se, but protections offered by warrantees would affect actual O&M costs.  It is 
to be noted that in the project, district authorities would be responsible for O&M and related 
resource commitments (e.g., personnel/finance). Further, before any investments are 
commenced, the districts will be expected to provide a letter of support for long term O&M 
(beyond the project and supplier contracts), as the O&M estimates based on final site-
selection and supplier commitments are finalized during implementation.   
Paradigm shift potential 
We note the iTAP’s acknowledgement of the pioneering and innovative approach the project 
is advancing for water management, integrating grey and green approaches. We also note 
that project is promoting an innovative approach in integrating climate risk management into 
IWRM. 
Regarding iTAP concerns on the approximately US1.3M estimated for introduction of 
innovative precision agriculture technology in the project, we address specific concerns 
below. (Also refer to the response in the previous section) 
Paragraph 24 
It is indeed, as iTAP notes, true that precision agriculture is still in the introductory stage in 
Thailand. We would like to posit that new technologies require testing; new innovations do 
not have the network of retailers, repair and service entities as one would find with well 
tested technology. The early adoption and piloting of innovative technology should not be 
evaluated in terms of standalone cost-effectiveness, since it is not at a stage for mass rollout 
(when the technology would not be described as ‘innovative’).  
The Government of Thailand has requested UNDP to help facilitate the testing and piloting of 
technology to support its National Strategy-Thailand 4.0: the government's industrial 
economic model, which aims to shift traditional agriculture into smart agriculture. The 
advancement of technology in agriculture is also in line with Thailand’s 20 year Agricultural 
Development Plan (2017-2036) and “MOAC’s Digital Agriculture Strategic Plan (2017-2021)”. 
The market value of smart farming in Thailand amounted to around 128.7 million U.S. dollars 
in 2018, and was forecasted to reach 269.9 million U.S. dollars in 2022.  There is growing 
literature to suggest precision agriculture can be viable for Thailand, notably: “Small Scale 
Farmers under Thailand’s Smart Farm System” by Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives; 
“Precision Agriculture for Rice Production in Thailand” by Kasetsart University; “A 
comprehensive solution for landscape-scale sustainable water management in rice” by 
International Rice Research Institute. Around the country, there are many examples and 
piloting projects with industry partners on precision agriculture and usage of drone for 
farming: for instance, telecommunications firm DTAC is working with Department of 
Agricultural Extension on new IoT-based precision farming solution. In recognition of

=== PAGE 110 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 110 
 
 
 
Thailand’s interest in precision farming, an international conference is planned for January 
2022, providing political and financial momentum for the country to accelerate these 
innovations. (https://waset.org/advances-in-precision-farming-technologies-conference-in-
january-2022-in-bangkok) 
In light of this, we stress that the project intends to focus on paving the way for the paradigm 
shift envisioned by the Thai Government. The iTAP review comments state that: “Although 
innovative, it is costly (both from a CapEx and an OpEx basis), thereby limiting diffusion of 
this advanced technology to low-income small farmers in the region.” It is not expected that 
smallholder farmers in the project would upscale to full IoT individually or immediately. A 
few aggregate farms benefit from the introduction of the technology, with full support for 
capital and O&M costs (beyond the project), while serving as pilots that allow the government 
to consolidate successes and failures on their path to digitalization of agriculture over time.  
Paragraph 27 
Regarding concern of staff poaching, capacity building in the relevant departments would be 
sustained through the following approaches: (i) Training of Trainers approach for the 
department staff and (ii) Codification of the training materials and dissemination. These 
approaches could help address any staff turnover by institutionalizing the training and, to an 
extent, broadening the reach of the capacity building efforts.  
Sustainable development potential 
We note iTAP’s acknowledgement of the environmental (reduced soil erosion, improved 
surface water quality and groundwater recharge, and enhanced biodiversity), social 
(enhanced food and water security and better health conditions), economic (enhanced 
income generation and safeguarding against loss of assets), and gender (provisions to 
address gender inequality) benefits of the project. 
Needs of the recipient 
Comments from iTAP are well noted on the reach of the project to benefit the most vulnerable 
and its contribution to socio-economic development. 
We appreciate the discussions with iTAP and the opportunity provided to clarify several 
aspects of the climate rationale for the project. As noted above we have used a combination of 
multiple (all globally available) sources of both observed and projected future climate data, 
including Thai weather stations from the basin, CRU, GPCC, CHIRPS and ensembles of CMIP 5 
& 6 and CORDEX climate models, both for RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 (see Annex 2, Feasibility Study, 
sections 1.7.3 & 1.7.4,). We used these multiple data sources, a calibrated hydrological model 
of the Chao Phraya basin and available literature to assess the evidence for increases in 
rainfall intensity, surface runoff and water discharge (to assess the likelihood that flooding 
risk will increase under climate change). All data sources and variables indicate likely 
increases in flooding hazard and risk (see FP paragraphs 11, 12, 16 & 17 and Annex 2, 
Feasibility Study sections 1.7.4 and 1.7.5). The same multiple data sources were used to 
evaluate indicators of agricultural drought, including potential evapotranspiration (PET), 
SPEI, crop and irrigation water requirements (CWR & IWR), all of which indicated increasing 
agricultural drought risk (see FP paragraphs 13 & 17 and Annex 2, Feasibility Study sections

=== PAGE 111 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.02 
Page 111 
 
 
 
1.7.4 and 1.7.5). As recommended within the recently released IPCC AR6 WGI report (chapter 
10), we compared these different sources of data and information to ensure that observed 
trends and projected future changes (from bias corrected GCMs and RCMs) were consistent 
and not contradictory in terms of the expected direction (sign) of change, indicating whether 
climate hazards and hence climate-induced risk will increase. As iTAP acknowledges, these 
indicators and models consistently demonstrate that flooding risk (through increases in 
rainfall intensity) and agricultural drought risk (through increases in PET, CWR and IWR) are 
likely to continue to increase over the Chao Phraya basin in the future, thereby providing the 
necessary rationale for project interventions to address these expected elevated climate risks 
(see FP, paragraph 17 for a summary). 
 
Paragraph 51 
iTAP’s comment on (d) Absence of alternative sources of financing is noted. Challenges of 
accessibility and affordability of climate finance for small farmers is a global development 
challenge. In this project, the credit line provided by BAAC is new and additional, developed 
specifically to complement this project’s interventions, with the GCF investment allowing for 
crowding in of this co-financing. The project will work with BAAC to design the credit to 
ensure that project beneficiaries will have better/enhanced access.  Credit will support 
investments in adaptation (e.g. farm ponds, precision agriculture, etc.) taking into account the 
financial capacity of the farmers. Krungsri bank will also partner with the project to provide 
the financial literacy training to the project beneficiaries. The capacity building and 
investments for climate resilience, financial literacy training from Krungsri bank and credit 
line provided by BAAC will together facilitate a holistic approach to build creditworthiness 
and enable access to finance for the beneficiary farmers. This could also serve as a model for 
BAAC to replicate and scale up their loan programme in other regions. 
 
Country ownership 
Comments from iTAP are well noted.  
 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
iTAP’s concern on efficiency of precision agriculture is noted. Refer to previous response in 
Impact Potential section.

Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Action Plan 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 
Gender Assessment, Action Plan and Budget 
Enhancing climate resilience in Thailand through effective 
water management and sustainable agriculture 
I.
Introduction
This gender assessment aims to provide an overview of the gender situation in Thailand, with a specific 
focus on supporting climate resilience and transformational change in water management and sustainable 
agriculture, identifying gender issues that are relevant to the project, and examining potential gender 
mainstreaming opportunities at the policy and project level. The assessment is based on available data 
from studies conducted by the Royal Thai Government (RTG), UN agencies, and multilateral development 
agencies; and includes: 
•
Undertaking a desk review on gender-related studies conducted for Thailand
•
Drawing on earlier stakeholder consultations [and engaging potential female beneficiaries of
the project and incorporating feedback into the design of the proposed project]
•
Incorporating information and lessons learned from past studies and assessments on gender
in Thailand;
•
Documentation on Thailand’s relevant gender and climate commitments under the SDGs,
CEDAW, and the Paris Agreement (including Nationally Determined Contributions)
•
Integrating gender considerations in the project indicators, targets and activities.
II.
Gender and Climate Change Related Risks in Thailand
Thailand is considered one of the sixteen countries in the "extreme risk" category that are most vulnerable 
to the impacts of climate change over the next thirty years (Thailand NDC). Thailand’s agriculture sector 
is particularly at risk with 55% of the nation’s total area under agricultural use. Changes in climatic 
conditions have the potential to destabilize agricultural productivity and impact farmers’ incomes and 
lives.1 2
While the impacts of climate change affect the agricultural sectors and the country, climate change and 
its impacts are not gender neutral. Inequitable access to and control over natural resources, particularly 
land, water, and forests, as well as exclusion from the policy- and decision-making processes that affect 
resource distribution and their livelihood. These disproportionately affect the poor, particularly poor rural 
women, members of indigenous and ethnic minorities, who rely on natural resources for the livelihood.  
1 Talberth, J., Reytar, K., 2014. Climate Change in the Lower Mekong Basin - An Analysis of Economic Values at Risk, USAID 
Mekong Adaptation and Resilience to Climate Change (USAID Mekong ARCC). World Resources Institute, Washington, D.C. 
2 Plan International. 2018. Climate change, young women and girls: Vulnerability, impacts, and adaptation in Northern Thailand. 
Gender documentation for FP170

=== PAGE 114 ===
H 
Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

Women as well as men are also both important change agents and critical actors in actions to address 
climate change and in adopting adaptation options to strengthen resilience of communities and 
households. Their potentially different knowledge and skills as well as needs and priorities are important 
to consider in the the design, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation of climate change and natural 
resources projects. 
 
 
III. 
Existing Gender Inequality in Thailand 
 
 
The Gender Inequality Index (GII) measures gender inequalities across three important dimensions of 
human development—reproductive health, measured by maternal mortality ratio and adolescent birth 
rates; empowerment, measured by proportion of parliamentary seats occupied by females and proportion 
of adult females and males aged 25 years and older with at least some secondary education; and economic 
status, expressed as labour market participation and measured by labour force participation rate of 
female and male populations aged 15 years and older. The GII exposes differences in the distribution of 
achievements between women and men and measures the human development costs of gender 
inequality. The higher the GII value the greater the disparities between females and males and the greater 
loss to human development. 
The 2017 GII sheds light on the position of women in 160 countries; it yields insights in gender gaps in 
major areas of human development. The component indicators highlight areas in need of critical policy 
intervention and stimulate proactive thinking and public policy to overcome systematic gender-based 
disadvantages. 
Thailand has a GII value of 0.393, ranking it 93 out of 160 countries in the 2017 index (see Table 1). In 
Thailand, 4.8 percent of parliamentary seats are held by women, and 42.4 percent of adult women have 
reached at least a secondary level of education compared to 47.5 percent of their male counterparts. For 
every 100,000 live births, 20 women die from pregnancy related causes; and the adolescent birth rate is 
51.9 births per 1,000 women of ages 15-19. Female participation in the labour market is 60.5 percent 
compared to 77.3 for men.3 In comparison, the Philippines and Viet Nam are ranked at 97 and 67 
respectively on this index. Unemployment rate for youth (2019) is men 3.4%, women 5.3%, while the 
share of youth not in employment, education or training (2019) is men 11.2%, women 18.6%.4

Based on the sex-disaggregated Human Development Index (HDI), the Gender Development Index (GDI) 
is defined as a ratio of female to male HDI. The GDI measures gender inequalities across three dimensions 
of human development; health (measured by women’s and men’s life expectancy at birth); education 
(measured by expected years of schooling for children and mean years for men and women aged 25 years 
and older); and command over economic resources (measured by estimated Gross National Income GNI) 
per capita for women and men. 
The GDI for 2017 is calculated for 164 countries. The 2017 female HDI is 0.753 in contrast to 0.756 for 
males.  The GDI value for Thailand is 0.996 compared to the Philippines (1.000) and Viet Nam (1.005) (See

The Gender Social Norms Index (GSNI) attempts to measure the prevalence of gender biases affecting 
gender equality. The index includes four dimensions – political, economic, educational, and physical 
integrity. According to GSNI, 95.47 % show a gender bias in at least one of the dimensions. 74.5 % show 
biases in two or more dimensions. Physical integrity is the dimension that indicate the strongest gender 
bias at 84.53%. This dimension uses proxies to measure attitudes to intimate partner violence and 
reproductive rights. For the political dimension, which measure attitudes to women as political leaders,

=== PAGE 117 ===
H 
Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Action Plan 
 
                                                    GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 
 
67.3% indicate that they have a bias. The economic and educational dimensions show 50.86% and 29.02% 
respectively.5  
 
Per UN Women, challenges in realizing gender equality and the empowerment of women in Thailand 
remain in the areas of lack of sex disaggregated data, traditional attitudes and stereotypes which underpin 
domestic violence and violence against women, low participation of women in politics and decision-
making positions, discrimination and vulnerabilities of ethnic and rural women as well as women in the 
informal sector, HIV prevalence, trafficking and exploitation.6  
 
In addition, data drawn from draft national assessment on social dimension of CC impact report7, provides 
the following information: National poverty rate (2017) is 7.21% overall. Urban 5.49%, Rural 8.91%. Men 
7.59%, Women 6.87%. Higher rates for both genders in the rural areas, 9.23% men and 8.61% women. 
Lower poverty rates for women in all regions. (National Statistic Office)  
5.3 million people are in absolute poverty (living below national poverty line), 5.4 million are near poor 
(just above national poverty line). 1.5 million households are landless. 
 
The National Economic and Social Development Board (NESDB) in its 2017 report on poverty and 
inequality describes the general characteristics of Thailand’s poor as follows:8 
• 
Individual characteristics: low education, working in agricultural sector, landless, low income 
without job benefits 
• 
Household characteristics: large-sized, skipped-generation, elderly or female-headed households 
most vulnerable to poverty with limited opportunity to engage in income generation 
• 
Concentration: southern, north-eastern and northern regions, more rural than urban. 
 
Agriculture Sector 
Poverty tends to concentrate in farming households. According to the Office of Agricultural Economics 
(OAE), based on 2013/2014 data, farming households in the northeast have the highest rate of poverty 
with a regional average of 37.27 percent, followed by the north 26.09 percent, the central region 13.86 
percent, and the south 9.57 percent.9 One-third of Thai labor force is in agriculture. Nearly 60 million rai 
of farm land is prone to drought or floods, and over 80% of which is outside of irrigated area.  
The 2017 NESDB report also shows the highest incidence of poverty among children and elderly 
populations. Due to low income in agricultural work, young agricultural workforce migrated to cities for 
jobs, so by 2013, 1 in 4 Thai farmers were over the age of 55, leading to rise in “skipped-generation”

households (grandparents living with grandchildren).  There are some indications that there are more 
single elderly female-headed skipped-generation households than the male-headed ones.  
In 2013, 1.4 million single-parent households (7.1% of all households); majority single-mother often in 
difficult situation. 400,000 skipped-generation households (2% and growing trend) (NESDB, 12th 
Development Plan).  A growing number of Thai elders (38%, even male-female split) are living in 
vulnerable family situation (living alone, with elderly spouse or with grandchildren). 63% living in rural 
area. However, more elderly female heads in skipped-generation households (meaning more elderly 
women with grandchildren). About 20 percent of elders in skipped-generation households have income 
below the poverty line10 - more than twice the national poverty rate. 
In the OAE analysis, factors contributing to poverty in farming households include geographical region, 
access to irrigation, age and education of head of household, number of family members, dependent 
burden, type of farmland, household net assets, and household debt. In the OAE’s view, farming 
households in the northeast and the north are the most likely to be poor and should be prioritized in 
poverty reduction interventions.11 The National Economic and Social Development Board (NESDB) also 
stressed the need to reduce poverty with the priority for the north and northeast in the Twelfth Plan. 
Roughly 40% of expenses of Thai household debts is spent on basic consumption, and 14% (formal) & 
9.9% (informal) for agricultural activities, and 7.9% formal & 32.9% informal for businesses.  
While the agricultural sector produces overall only 10 percent of the GDP, it is important for Thailand’s 
rural economy, representing 31 percent of the GDP for the north, 28 percent for the south, 23 percent for 
the northeast, and 20 percent for the west.12 GDP in 2014 value. NESDB. 2014 figures. Office of Agricultural 
Economics.13  The agricultural census carried out in 2013 showed an increased proportion of female land 
holders. In the Northern Region, women made up 28.7% of the landholders in 2013, compared to 20.7% 
in 2003.14  
Focus group discussions were held in the project sites during project development, including 401 including 
as a sampling group. The number of female and male respondents was 199 and 202 persons, respectively.   
According to perceptions, the farmers in the four districts reported on the incidence of climate change 
related impacts in the long-term shifts over the last 10-20 years and adaptive strategies in coping with the 
shifts. The survey showed that 93% of the farmers feel a increase temperature and that the temperature 
becomes hotter, and 84-91% feel the change in rainfall amount and intensity, with greater concerns about 
drought. In response, a number of autonomous adaptation strategies have been undertaken by farmers. 
The most common were changing crops and/or crop patterns and timing. Some other strategies used 
were swapping to crops that were less water-intensive and stopping cultivating for a while to other career,

Gender Division of Labour 
Focus group discussions further indicated that women generally do the same activities in agricultural 
cultivation as men do.  This is in addition to the role of caregiver for families and household work.  This is 
consistent with a study in Northern Thailand15 where it was found that women often hold the primary 
responsibility for unpaid household labour (e.g. cleaning, cooking, childcare, and caring for home gardens 
for household food consumption). Another study by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United 
Nations (FAO) into gender roles and use of technology in Thailand found that while women were generally 
involved in all aspects of agricultural production, while men dominate mechanized tasks. Further, women 
have significantly reduced access to resources (e.g. inputs, funds/credit, seeds, breeds and plant varieties, 
and information and knowledge)16 and therefore decision-making power when it comes to agriculture 
activities.  
Water insecurity creates livelihood-related challenges and potential losses of income. A shortage of water 
can add stress to women’s lives through increased workloads (fetching water). Young women and children 
in Northern Thailand often work support their mothers with household labour. They also contribute to 
agricultural labour and subsequently household’s income. Climate change increases water insecurity, 
increasing the burden particularly for women and children as primary household caretakers and 
agricultural labourers.17 18  Focus group discussions in the project sites indicate that for managing water 
usage group , the leaders are usually men. However , roles and responsibilities of women in these groups 
include vice president , financing and accounting as well as administrative positions. 
A recent study indicated that reduced fertility, access to electricity and running water have contributed 
to easing rural women’s lives and labour burdens. Even with these, women in rural Thailand still carry the 
bigger burden of domestic work than men. While it is several years old and the economic and social

contexts have evolved, a 2001 national survey noted that in rural areas, women spent an average 5.4 
hours a day on domestic tasks and childcare, compared to 2.9 hours for men.19 
COVID-19 and the extent to which it could impact Thailand must be mentioned.  As women are the 
primary caretakers, illness in the family would add to the responsibilities of women. One of the measures 
to contain the spread of the virus has been to close schools and care facilities, this is likely to have added 
to the burden of unpaid care work of women. As at 30 June 2020, there have been 3,171 diagnosed cases 
in Thailand, 12 of which were in the project sites.  Restrictions are slowly being lifted and the related socio-
economic impacts will be felt for the longer term. For a population of 65 million, the relatively low number 
of cases can be seen as a success towards containment.  There is however uncertainty going forward as 
restrictions continue to be lifted and international travel re-opens.          
 
Access to Resources 
Thailand’s Constitution (Articles 33 and 41) and the Civil and Commercial Code (Section 1336) state that 
women and men have the same rights to own, use, and make decisions regarding land and non-land 
assets. The law allows for joint land titling and co-ownership of property for married and unmarried 
couples. Women and men’s equal right to inheritance is protected by Thailand’s Civil and Commercial 
Code and traditionally the youngest daughter inherits most of the property. 2021 Customary law still 
influences decision-making in rural areas communities, with communal rules allocating resources, 
decision-making, and conflict resolution in ways that may disadvantage women’s access to land, property 
and inheritance.22 23 The Civil and Commercial Code stipulates that, after divorce, commonly held property 
(Sin Somros) is to be divided equally between man and woman (Thailand, 1925, Sec. 1533). Spouses 
manage his or her own individual property (Sin Suan Tua) (Thailand, 1925, Sec. 1473). In administering 
common marital property, either the spouses are joint managers, or one spouse must obtain consent 
from the other (Thailand, 1925, Sec. 1476).24  
Women and men have the same rights to open bank accounts, obtain credit, and access formal financial 
services.25 Government measures such as the National Village and Urban Community Fund extends loans 
to villages and urban communities and provides vocational training and try to reach women and men.26

Social norms, inequitable power dynamics, and the gendered division of labour affect women’s and men’s 
access to resources in Northern Thailand.  This includes water.27 Men typically have greater rights of access 
to, and decision-making power over water. Women in rural areas, particularly poor women of ethnic 
minority, may face disproportionate water insecurity compared to rural men and people in urban areas 
due to overlapping sources of vulnerability and marginalization (Plan International, 2018).  
A recent study demonstrates some of the challenges faced at the community level around land ownership, 
the pattern of which is mixed in Thailand. Forty percent of the participants in focus group discussions 
indicated that, in practice, they have access to, and decision-making power over land but are unsure of 
their formal ownership status. In 30 percent of the FGDs, participants mentioned that new property (land 
as well as house) is commonly registered under the wife’s name because men tend to avoid paperwork 
and prefer to work in the field.28 
 
Education 
Thailand’s 1999 Education Act grants “all children, without discrimination” a right to a quality education 
and the Ministry of Education supports all children a twelve-year education. From a gender perspective, 
Thailand has been very successful at promoting the right to girls’ education. Between the years 2005 to 
2010, the primary school completion rate for girls in school was 89 % while the completion rate for boys 
was 97 percent.  While there are still fewer girls than boys attending primary school, the gender gap has 
decreased considerably from slightly more than 8 % in 1971 to slightly less than two percent in 2009. 29 By 
2012, there was little difference in enrolment between urban and rural areas.30 
In 1998, the Child-Friendly School (CFS) initiative was launched to promote education for all, and many of 
CFS’s activities are ongoing in Thailand today.  Yet, girls continue to face challenges accessing education 
including poverty, language, and mobility/distance to school. Marginalized populations also face 
challenges. 31   
From the perspective of higher education, women have more opportunities than men. However, although 
53 percent of science students in higher education are female, less than 24 percent of women study 
engineering, manufacturing and construction studies.  In recent years, Thailand’s Ministry of Education

Health 
Thailand has worked to ensure wide access to health services including sexual and reproductive health 
(SRH) services. A primary health care system expanded to sub-districts beginning in the 1980s and access 
to health care became more equitable. From a gender perspective, achievements include the following: 
80% of pregnant women used prenatal care in 1987 and 92% in 2000. With Universal Health Care (UHC), 
use reached 100% in 2012. Birth attendance increased from 90.8% in 1990 to 99.7% in 2012. Further, 
contraceptive prevalence rate (CPR) increased from 14.8% in 1970 to 79.3 % in 2012. The total fertility 
rate also fell from 5.9 children per woman in the 1970s to 1.6 in 2011.33 
Challenges continue however, including the increase in adolescent birth rate from 31 per 1000 women 
aged 15 – 19 year in 2000 to 53 in 2012.  The adolescent birth rate has sharply increased from 31 per 1000 
women aged 15–19 years in 2000 to 53 in 2012. Unmet need for contraception is 6.9% higher among 
women aged 15–19 years (11.6%) than among women aged 45–49 years (8.3%).34  
Women still face challenges accessing safe abortions even though it is permitted by law, when conducted 
by a physician, if the pregnancy threatens a woman's life or health, or is the result of a sexual crime. 
Official reports suggest that about 30,000 abortions occur each year (including induced, spontaneous and 
therapeutic), but other estimates go as high as 200,000–300,000 annually, suggesting that abortion is 
widely practiced.35 
Indigenous peoples in rural Thailand experience health inequities as demonstrated by a recent study In 
Northern Thailand. 36  Healthcare is limited or not available in rural areas and indigenous people are less 
likely to access public health services, leading to negative health consequences. Many women with 
preventable diseases (e.g. cervical cancer and HIV/AIDS) are indigenous and face discrimination for being 
women and being indigenous; this discrimination also affects their access to healthcare.  Further, the 
unequal labour distribution in their households can also lead to unequal opportunities for indigenous 
women to seek healthcare, invest in educational and vocational skills, and participate in paid work.

The impacts of COVID-19 in Thailand and in the world are not yet fully known. The project beneficiaries 
are expected to be vulnerable agriculture households, many female-headed and including elderly, which 
are in a high risk category.   
 
Political Participation 
Article 65 of the Constitution states that men and women in Thailand have the same rights to hold public 
and political office in the executive, judiciary and legislative branches (Thailand, 2007). There are no legal 
quotas at national or local levels. Further, the government provides incentives to encourage women’s 
political participation, including training for female political candidates, public education campaigns, and 
pushing for quotas within political parties. Even though women have the same rights, they face cultural 
barriers to political participation. For example, patriarchal norms pose challenges to women who run for 
office at the local level – norms that expect them to stay at home. Election rigging, corruption, and patron-
client relationships also complicate women’s advancement in the political arena and their advancement.37  
Women are still underrepresented in public and private sectors, including in the parliament, government, 
judiciary and administration both at national and local levels. Thailand has ranked as one of the lowest 
countries for gender equality in politics in the region of Asia and the Pacific.38 The percentage of women 
in provincial and local governments in Thailand is much lower than that of the national government. There 
is only one female governor from 76 provinces (1.32%).39 Female Chiefs of Provincial Administrative 
Organizations (PAO) and Sub-district Administrative Organizations (SAO) account for 8% and 6.45% 
respectively.40 
In reference to agriculture, a recent study on gender equity in agriculture in Southeast Asia found that 
women in Thailand were active members of village and agricultural organizations and had direct contact 
with extension officers. Women’s involvement in decision making in rice farming was observed to be 
greater in Thailand compared to the Philippines, Indonesia, and Myanmar – other countries in the study.41   
 
Gender-based Violence 
Patriarchal norms and attitudes in Thailand contribute to violence against women in the public and private 
spheres. Thailand does not currently have one overarching law addressing all forms of violence against 
women. The Domestic Violence Victim Protection Act of 2007 criminalizes domestic violence and 
perpetrators are liable to imprisonment of up to six months or a fine (Thailand, 2007b, Art. 4). The law 
extends to current and former partners, and includes physical and psychological violence (Thailand, 
2007b).  While the definition does not specify sexual and economic violence, these can fall under the law, 
as domestic violence is broadly defined as “any act done with the intention or in the manner likely to

over a family member in order to wrongfully cause him to do, not to do or yield to any act” (Thailand, 
2007b, Art. 3).42  
Rape is criminalized by the Penal Code of 1965, and the subsequent Criminal Code Amendment Act of 
2007. The Amendment Act removed the provision allowing a husband to rape his wife. (OECD, 2019, SIGI 
Thailand). The Labour Protection Act of 2008 and the Penal Code foresee legal protection in the workplace 
and the Labour Protection Act extends protection against sexual abuse, harassment or nuisance by 
superiors to cover all workers, both female and male, with penalties up to THB 20 000 baht (Thailand, 
2008).  Sexual harassment continues to be an issue in the workplace, and women may be verbally and 
physically abused in the workplace, pressured to trade sexual favours for career advancement 
opportunities, and face risks of violence when traveling between their home and workplace.43 
 
Intimate Partner Violence (IPV) is perceived as a private or a family matter, and is considered to be 
underreported in Thailand.  Statistics show that the numbers of reported domestic violence cases have 
risen from 25,767 to 31,866 between 2010 and 2013 respectively (Thai Health Promotion Foundation 
2011), (The Women's Affairs and Family Development 2013).  In addition, the report from the OSCC, 
Ministry of Public Health (MOPH) found that, in 2015, there were 23,977 women who used their services.  
Recent region-specific data is lacking, however, a report 2011 report indicates a higher proportion in the 
North and Central regions, and there was a correlation with limited education44.

The Domestic Violence Act enables government officials to make available medical, social and 
psychological assistance to survivors of domestic violence. The Thai government had implemented 
preventative measures including the establishment of shelters and half-way homes for children and 
families in crisis, One Stop Crisis Centres to provide physical and psychosocial health services to victims of 
sexual and domestic violence, and a 24-hour hotline. Police officers are said to be trained in gender 
perspectives and the psychological nature of sexual violence victims, and certain courts have integrated 
women-friendly measures such as the provision of counselling services and the separation of victims and 
perpetrators.45 The Department of Women’s Affair and Family Development Learning Centres hears 
complaints of gender discrimination and the Committee on Consideration of Gender Discrimination 
ensures that victims of gender discrimination receive legal remedies and compensation.46 However, 
Thailand has been criticized under CEDAW for the lack of access to justice for survivors of gender-based 
violence. The criticism includes issues such as law enforcement officials’ negative attitudes resulting in 
failure of register and investigate complaints.47

IV. 
Legal and Administrative Framework Protecting Women and Gender Equality 
 
From the perspective of  key international commitments protecting women and gender equality, in 2000, 
Thailand ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women 
(CEDAW) in 1985 and its Optional Protocol and endorsed the Beijing Platform for Action (BPFA), the 
Millennium Development Goals (MDGs), and finally the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).  
  
Thailand has made significant efforts to integrate the international principles and instruments into the 
policy and programming framework, evident in the Constitution B.E. 2550 (2007), which has anti sex 
discrimination and gender equality provisions.   
 
A Working Group for the Implementation of SDG 5 supports the implementation of the Goal. 
Representatives from government   agencies   and   civil   society   such as   the   Thailand   Sustainable   
Development   Foundation, the   Association   to   Empower   the   Potential   of   Women   with   Disabilities 
(AEPWWDs), the Foundation for Women, Homenet Thailand Foundation for Labour and Employment 
Promotion and WeMove are all active in the Working Group.  The Working Group allows government 
agencies and civil society to exchange information, as well as discuss future direction in the 
implementation of SDG 5.48  
 
The Strategy for Women’s Development Plan (2017 – 2021) is Thailand’s main mechanism for promoting 
women’s development; it aligns with SDG 5 (Gender Equality). The Strategy has the vision of “[creating] 
social equality without discrimination, for women to have good quality of life, be safe and secure.” The 
Government also designed an action plan to implement the Strategy. The action plan focuses on 
community development, for example, the Vocational Training Program for Women and Families which 
provides training for the unemployed, educationally disadvantaged, and people at risk of sexual 
exploitation and human trafficking.   
 
The Department of Women’s Affairs and Family Development, of the Ministry of Social Development and 
Human Security, is the national women’s machinery responsible for promoting gender equality and the 
empowerment of women, and social inclusion. It serves as a secretariat of the National Inter-ministerial 
Committee on Policy and Strategy for the Improvement of the Status of Women, chaired by the Prime 
Minister and develops.  The Office implements a five-year cycled National Women’s Development Plans, 
which currently falls under the period of 2012-2016.49 
 
In 2015, Thailand passed its first law to promote gender equality, namely the Gender Equality Act B.E. 
2558, which aims to provide protection to all people from gender discrimination. It was Thailand’s first 
legal instrument to specifically mention transgender people. Further, the Department of Women’s Affairs 
and Family Development recognizes the different challenges faced by people whose gender identity do 
 
48 Thailand's Voluntary National Review on the Implementation of the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development,  June 2018 
http://www.mfa.go.th/sep4sdgs/contents/filemanager/images/sep/VNR%202018%20English%2010.07.18.pdf  
49 UN Women: Thailand http://asiapacific.unwomen.org/en/countries/thailand

not match their sex assigned at birth.50 However, the Act has been criticized by the CEDAW Committee 
for making exceptions on issues related to religious principles and national security.51 
 
Thailand’s 2017 Voluntary National Review on the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development reported 
that women’s status in Thailand has drastically improved in Thailand over the past several decades. The 
country has achieved gender parity in basic education with women doing slightly better in higher 
education and the proportion of women in poverty than men overall. While noting that Thailand is 
recognized as having among the most favourable conditions for women entrepreneurship in Asia and the 
Pacific,52 the Review also recognized the low representation of women in the public sector, in particular 
in national politics, in government leadership positions and in local administration. The 2017 Constitution 
calls for gender responsive budgeting, stipulating that the government should take into account the 
different necessities and needs with respect to gender, age, and conditions of persons to ensure fairness.  
The 2017 Voluntary National Review also noted that elements of SDG 5 would be included in the Women 
Development Strategy (2017-2021), which recognizes that Thailand has fallen short on several 
international goals and measures and the country needs to step up efforts in women’s development and 
promote a more equal society. Thailand considers the development of a gender-disaggregated database 
a next step in its effort to achieve gender equality. The Government reaffirmed its commitment in 2017 
to work with all stakeholders at both national and international levels for the successful promotion of 
gender equality and sustainable development.53  
 
The weak gender dimension in Thai policy has been observed by the Committee on the Elimination of 
Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW). In CEDAW’s 2017 Concluding Observations on the state of 
gender equality in Thailand, the Committee expressed a particular concern for the situation of rural Thai 
women, including indigenous women and those ethnic and religious minorities, remain disproportionately 
affected by poverty and limited economic opportunities. CEDAW spells out that Thai women, in particular 
rural women, are also excluded from participation in the elaboration and implementation of policies and 
action plans on climate change and disaster risk reduction, despite the fact they are disproportionately 
affected by the effects of climate change and disasters.54  
 
Besides lack of clear policy directives on the integration of gender equality and limited participation of 
women in the formulation and implementation of climate policies and plans, another key challenge in 
gender and social inclusion in on climate change action is lack of disaggregated data.

V. 
Gender Analysis and Recommendations  
 
This gender analysis acts as an entry point for addressing gender equality and women’s empowerment 
through: i.) strategic approaches, i.e. with interventions targeted specifically at women and girls and  ii.) 
mainstreamed approaches, i.e. ensuring that all areas of the project promote the transformation of 
gender inequalities to strengthen climate resilience and provide greater opportunities for all rural people 
to adopt adaptation options that reduce their vulnerability to the impacts of climate change and 
strengthen the climate resilience of households and communities in the areas directly and indirectly 
targeted by the project. 
 
Over 50% of project beneficiaries are expected to be women, please see table below.  The project should 
ensure that the particular climate challenges and needs faced by women in water management and 
agriculture are fully captured in project implementation.  These include disadvantages related to: 
 
• 
Access to information about climate change and related impacts on water and agriculture, and 
related ability to define information disseminated 
• 
Decision making related water use/management and agriculture 
• 
Access to technology and related training to advance climate resilient agriculture 
• 
Access to finance to invest in climate resilient agriculture

The project is aligned with national gender equality and women’s empowerment related policies and 
programmes as well as international commitments under the Paris Agreement, CEDAW, SDGs, etc.  The 
project will aim to support transformational change towards gender equality and women’s empowerment 
and will:  
• 
Seek to understand in greater depth the potentially gender-differentiated knowledge, experience, 
and skills of women and men at sub-district, district and provincial levels  as well as their needs, 
challenges, and vulnerabilities (e.g. around access to resources, decision-making, etc.) in order to 
strengthen project interventions including information sharing between implementers and 
beneficiaries, decision-making around adaptation at community and household levels, 
production, post-harvest, and market development decisions, etc. This can be done through 
introducing/piloting approaches such as gender-responsive participatory Integrated Climate

Services for Agriculture (PICSA) and piloting and scaling up household methodologies (HHM). 55 56  
Several organizations have championed these approaches which have been applied in several in 
different regions to strengthen agricultural livelihoods, production, and value chain/market 
development, etc. 
• 
Formulate a working group on gender. The gender focal point of each ministries (national level) 
will be informed about the project implementation. The project management team, led by the 
Royal Irrigation Department will invite a member from a provincial representative of the Women 
Empowerment Fund, Krungsri Bank, BAAC, female head of villages, Tambon Administrative Office, 
water user groups, FFS in the project provinces, Yom-Nan river basin committee, as well as a co-
operatives in the project provinces.  The Ministry of Social Development and Human Security 
(MSDHS) is mandated to promote gender equality and is therefore important to engage on issues 
related to gender. MSDHS has staff working on issues such as gender responsive budgeting and 
planning, which would be useful to engage in the project. Equally important will it be to use and 
try to strengthen the system om gender focal points appointed within each ministry. 
• 
Due to the low level of women in decision-making roles in Thailand, which also applies to water 
management, efforts to promote and support women in these roles will be done throughout the 
project. This might also include ensuring that there are enabling environments, including working 
with men to alter harmful stereotypes. 
• 
Ensure capacity building includes the active participation of women and men and incorporates, 
where relevant, issues around gender and climate information services, market development, 
farmer engagement, irrigation, use of innovative technology, etc.  
• 
Given the lower percentage of land owned by women, the project needs to ensure their equal 
participation by providing an enabling environment for participation. The project also needs to 
have a broader inclusion of households members, not only focusing on the person who the land 
is registered on.  
• 
Mitigate any negative impacts on rural women and girls (e.g. in terms of benefit sharing, labour 
division of labour, access to resources, access to technology and skills development, etc.) Where 
possible, enhance role of new generation, both female and male youth (aged 18-25) to build a

Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Action Plan
